From the question to the model

A four-hour workshop on carbon data when the response is not Gaussian

Bastien Chassagnol

PEPR Carbon statistical workshop

10 October 2026

How the four hours are split ⏳

  • 30 min. Who is here, and two datasets we will keep in view
  • 50 min. The question, a PICO exercise, and twelve reporting pitfalls
  • 70 min. Non-Gaussian responses: QQ plots, then Poisson, negative binomial, and logit
  • 20 min. Experimental design and hierarchical models
  • 20 min. An introduction to causal inference
  • 50 min. Quiz and the SAMPL draft, then reproducibility and the close

Down walks this section. Right jumps to the next section. The PICO writing block and the closing SAMPL block are protected. If a GLM slide runs long, shorten the tour of pitfalls 6 to 8 rather than the exercises. Speaker view: press S. A chalkboard is available if you want to annotate a PICO live.

FairCarboN, and why the carbon budget needs it

The Paris Agreement asks to hold warming well below \(2^\circ\mathrm{C}\), and to reach climate neutrality in the second half of this century. Continental ecosystems have to cut emissions and absorb those that remain:

  • forests, grassland, cropland, fresh water, peatland, coasts, and cities

That has to happen without giving up the other services those ecosystems provide.

FairCarboN is the French programme for that science. CNRS and INRAE co-lead it, with €40 million over 2023–2029. The next slide is that page.

One minute. The point is the contribution the ecosystems are being asked to make, not a tour of the governance chart. If the following frame is blank, open the link: some hosts refuse to be embedded. Preview links are on, so the click opens an overlay.

Let the page sit for a few seconds. If the frame is blank, say the URL aloud and move on. Do not read the navigation menu.

Four scientific axes

The same questions are grouped on the axes page:

  1. Land to water. Sources, transfer, transformation, and storage along the terrestrial–aquatic continuum
  2. Coupled cycles. Carbon with nitrogen and phosphorus, and the water that constrains both
  3. Biomass. What sets plant traits and production, and whether a new use is viable and acceptable
  4. Economy and society. Behaviour, public policy, and the environmental and economic consequences

The next slide is the axes page.

Name one axis that matches a person in the room if you already know the group. Otherwise leave it as a map.

Optional: point at one axis on screen. Thirty seconds unless someone asks for a project name.

Who is in the room? 💬

  2. The carbon question, in one sentence
  3. What is your current objective? and modelling framework?
  4. Which software do you use to implement your statistical analyses?
  5. How do you enforce reproducibility in your research?

Two or three people answer aloud. Listen for Gaussian assumptions, compositional data, and repeated measures. Do not solve their analysis yet. About four minutes.

What we are practising

Not a catalogue of tests. A sequence you can reuse:

  • a scientific question, with a purpose
  • an estimand, including its unit
  • a distribution for \(Y\) that respects the support of the data
  • a check you can show, preferably a plot
  • a sentence a colleague can re-implement

This is the pedagogical aim from the course plan. About one minute.

\(Y\) is the response, \(x\) is the covariate

One row of the table holds both. \(Y\) is the response: what we measure and want to describe. \(x\) is the covariate: what we compare or condition on.

Every later symbol is built from these two. Ask which column in their own table is \(Y\). The answer should be the measurement, not the treatment or the depth. About one minute.

A distribution says how often each value occurs

\[ \Pr(a \le Y \le b) = \int_a^b f(y)\,dy \]

\(Y\) is random: a new core gives a new value. The density \(f\) says which values are likely. R names its four tools d, q, p, and r.

Read the panels left to right, then down. dnorm is the height of the curve. The shaded tail is qnorm(0.05), about \(-1.64\), and its area is 0.05; pnorm adds that area from the left. rnorm draws 400 values, seed 20261005, so the histogram wobbles around the curve. A count has a probability for each integer instead of a density; the same four tools exist (dpois, ppois, …).

Going further. Kolmogorov’s axioms define \(\Pr\) as a non-negative, countably additive measure with total mass one. The density is the derivative of \(\Pr\) with respect to a reference measure: Lebesgue measure for a continuous \(Y\), counting measure for a count. That is why a Poisson probability mass function and a Gaussian density sit in the same likelihood machinery. The four functions are linked by inversion: \(F(q_\alpha) = \alpha\), and a draw is \(F^{-1}(U)\) for \(U\) uniform on \((0, 1)\).

Mean and variance: the centre and the spread

\[ \mu = \mathbb{E}(Y), \qquad \sigma^2 = \mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}\{(Y - \mu)^2\} \]

The mean \(\mu\) moves the curve. The standard deviation \(\sigma\) widens it. The two numbers are separate.

Point at the left panel: same shape, shifted. Then the right panel: same centre, flatter. Ask which of the two a treatment usually targets. The answer is \(\mu\); a change in \(\sigma\) is a different question. Illustrative density functions, no data.

Going further. For a Gaussian, \(\mu\) and \(\sigma^2\) are free of each other, which is why a linear model can describe the mean and treat the variance as one nuisance number. For a Poisson count \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mu\), and for a binary response \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \pi(1 - \pi)\): the spread is tied to the centre. That tie is the variance function \(V(\mu)\) of a GLM, and it is why the non-Gaussian block starts from the family rather than the transformation.

The average estimates the mean

\[ \bar Y = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} Y_i \quad \text{estimates} \quad \mu = \mathbb{E}(Y) \]

\(i\) numbers the rows, from 1 to \(n\). The average \(\bar Y\) is computed from the sample; the expectation \(\mu\) belongs to the distribution. On a skewed \(Y\), the mean sits to the right of the median.

Two words for two objects: the average is a number from your rows, the expectation is a property of the distribution that produced them. Here the sample average is 4.01 and \(\mu = 4\). The long right tail pulls the mean above the median, which is why a skewed carbon stock is often summarised by its median too. Gamma draw, \(n = 300\), seed 20261005.

Going further. \(\bar Y\) is the plug-in estimator: replace the distribution by the empirical distribution of the rows and take its mean. It is unbiased, and by the law of large numbers it converges to \(\mu\) whenever \(\mathbb{E}|Y| < \infty\). Changing the loss changes the target: squared error gives the mean, absolute error the median, and the check loss a quantile. Rice covers plug-in estimation (Rice, n.d.).

Two spreads, not one

Empirical

\[ s = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_i - \bar Y)^2} \]

\(s\) is the spread of the observations around \(\bar Y\). The empirical standard error of the mean replaces the unknown scale with that \(s\):

\[ \frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} \]

Theoretical

The target here is \(\mu\). The sampling variance is \(\sigma^2/n\), so

\[ \mathrm{SE}(\bar Y) = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}. \]

In the draw on the next slide, \(\sigma = 15\) and \(n = 50\), so \(15/\sqrt{50} = 2.12\). The standard deviation of the 1,000 means is 2.11.

Read both formulas once. The common mistake is reporting \(s\) where the reader expects an uncertainty for \(\bar Y\). Ask the room which number would go in an error bar for the mean.

Going further. \(\mathrm{Var}(\bar Y) = \sigma^2/n\) needs the \(Y_i\) to be uncorrelated with a common variance; with clustered rows it is multiplied by the design effect. \(s^2\) is unbiased for \(\sigma^2\), but \(s\) is slightly biased for \(\sigma\) (Jensen’s inequality). Under normality \((\bar Y - \mu)/(s/\sqrt{n})\) follows a \(t_{n-1}\) distribution exactly; otherwise the central limit theorem makes it approximately standard normal for large \(n\), at a rate that worsens with skewness. See Hogg, McKean and Craig (Hogg et al. 2019).

The mean moves less than the observations do

The left-hand spread is \(s\). The right-hand spread is the standard error. The draw is M. Fatih Tüzen’s, seed 42, not a carbon series.

Point at the two histograms, not the code. The right panel should look visibly narrower; that is the whole lesson. Forty-five seconds.

Precise is not the same as right

\[ \underbrace{\mathbb{E}\{(\hat\mu - \mu)^2\}}_{\text{error}} = \underbrace{\mathrm{Var}(\hat\mu)}_{\text{spread}} + \underbrace{\{\mathbb{E}(\hat\mu) - \mu\}^2}_{\text{bias}^2} \]

A hat marks an estimate: \(\hat\mu\) is computed from the rows, \(\mu\) is the target. A small variance means repeat samples agree. It does not mean they agree with the truth: that is bias.

Repeat the experiment 2,000 times, \(n = 10\) each, seed 20261005. The orange estimator is precise and wrong; the blue one is right on average and noisy. The standard error measures only the width. A large sample, a narrow interval, and a biased design can all go together.

Going further. The decomposition is the mean squared error identity. Among unbiased estimators, the Cramér–Rao bound \(\mathrm{Var}(\hat\theta) \ge 1/I(\theta)\) holds under regularity conditions: the support does not depend on \(\theta\) and differentiation passes under the integral. Shrinkage, penalised smoothing, and priors deliberately trade a little bias for a larger cut in variance, as the orange curve does. Hogg, McKean and Craig state the bound (Hogg et al. 2019).

\(\mu\) is the conditional mean

Model

\(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\)

Read \(\mid\) as “given”. Sex is \(x\), height is \(Y\). Pooled, the histogram has two modes. Given sex, each curve is a Gaussian, and the conditional mean \(\mu\) is its centre. Illustrative draw, seed 20261005: 163 cm for F, 177 cm for H.

Point at the grey bars first: one sample, two humps, not one Gaussian. Then one colour: given sex, the curve is Gaussian and the dashed line is its mean. The curves are drawn at half weight so that they add up to the pooled histogram. These centimetres are a teaching draw, not a census.

Going further. \(\mu(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x})\) is the function \(m\) that minimises the expected squared error \(\mathbb{E}\{(Y - m(\mathbf{x}))^2\}\); geometrically it is the \(L^2\) projection of \(Y\) onto functions of \(\mathbf{x}\). Change the loss and the target changes: absolute error gives the conditional median, the check loss a conditional quantile. That is why “regression” without a named target is ambiguous for a skewed response. A standard treatment is in Rice, Mathematical Statistics and Data Analysis (Rice, n.d.).

A mean that changes with \(x\)

\[ \mu(x) = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x \]

\(\beta_0\) is the intercept; \(\beta_1\) is the change in \(\mu\) per unit of \(x\). We call \(\eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x\) the linear predictor. The model describes a whole distribution of \(Y\) at each \(x\), not only a line.

The sideways curves are the point: at each \(x\) the model says how \(Y\) scatters, and the line joins their centres. Simulated, seed 20261005. With several covariates the same sentence holds for the row \(\mathbf{x}\): \(\eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_1 + \beta_2 x_2 + \cdots\).

Going further. A parametric model is a family \(\{P_\theta\}\) of conditional distributions of \(Y\) given \(\mathbf{x}\). When the truth is not in the family, the maximum-likelihood estimate still converges, to the \(\theta^*\) closest to the truth in Kullback–Leibler divergence (White, 📄 Maximum likelihood estimation of misspecified models, Econometrica, 1982). The fitted line is then the best approximation in that family, and its standard errors need the sandwich form.

The link \(g\) keeps \(\mu\) in range

\[ g(\mu) = \eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x \]

A count mean cannot be negative; a probability stays between 0 and 1. The link \(g\) lets \(\eta\) be a straight line while \(\mu\) stays possible. When \(Y\) is 0 or 1, \(\mu = \pi = \Pr(Y = 1 \mid x)\).

Read the dashed lines first: a straight line on the scale of \(\mu\) predicts negative counts and probabilities above 1. The link bends the line into the allowed range. The log link gives \(\mu = \exp(\eta)\); the logit gives \(\pi = 1/\{1 + \exp(-\eta)\}\). \(g\) acts on \(\mu\), never on \(Y\): this is not a transformation of the data.

Going further. A GLM has three parts: a family from the exponential dispersion class, the linear predictor \(\eta = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta\), and a monotone, differentiable link \(g\) with \(g(\mu) = \eta\). The canonical link makes \(\eta\) equal to the natural parameter: log for the Poisson, logit for the Bernoulli, identity for the Gaussian. It gives \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\) as sufficient statistic and makes observed and expected information coincide. Agresti covers the construction (Agresti 2015).

Notation, all together

Symbol Meaning
\(Y\) Response on one row
\(x\), \(\mathbf{x}\) A covariate, or the row of covariates
\(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x})\) Conditional mean, the usual target
\(\pi\) \(\mu\) when \(Y\) is 0 or 1
\(\eta = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x\) Linear predictor
\(g(\mu) = \eta\) Link
\(\hat\mu\), \(\hat\beta\) Estimates from the rows
\(i\) Row index (a core, a date, a collar)
\(j\) Group index: the unit that received the treatment (a site, an enclosure, a batch)

Everything here except \(j\) has already appeared. \(j\) is new: rows that share a site or an enclosure are not independent, and the design block counts \(j\), not \(i\). Later slides reuse these letters and do not switch.

What is an estimand? 🃏

Press f to turn the card.

In one sentence: what is an estimand?

The quantity the analysis is trying to learn: \(\mu\), the unit it belongs to, and the contrast.

f flips. Later, q resets a quiz question and x checks it. Do not use q here. The notation table has just named \(\mu\) and \(j\); this card asks them to say both.

Likelihood: which value fits the rows best?

\[ \ell(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log f(Y_i; \theta), \qquad \hat\theta = \arg\max_{\theta} \ell(\theta) \]

\(\theta\) is any unknown number in the model, such as \(\mu\). The likelihood scores each value of \(\theta\) by how probable it makes the observed rows. More rows give a sharper peak, so a narrower interval.

Point at the dots: the best value. Then the width where each curve crosses the dotted line: that is a 95% likelihood interval, wide for 10 rows and narrow for 100. Poisson draws with true mean 4, seed 20261005. Every GLM in the afternoon is fitted this way.

Going further. Under regularity conditions (identifiable model, support free of \(\theta\), true value inside the parameter space), the maximum-likelihood estimator is consistent, asymptotically normal with variance \(I(\theta)^{-1}/n\), and invariant: the estimate of \(h(\theta)\) is \(h(\hat\theta)\). The dotted line is \(-\chi^2_{1, 0.95}/2 \approx -1.92\), from Wilks’ theorem. The conditions fail at a boundary, for instance testing \(\sigma_u^2 = 0\) in a mixed model, where the likelihood-ratio statistic is a 50:50 mixture of \(\chi^2_0\) and \(\chi^2_1\). AIC, \(-2\ell(\hat\theta) + 2p\), estimates prediction loss; BIC, with \(p \log n\), approximates a log marginal likelihood.

Large samples make the average Gaussian

\[ \bar Y \approx \mathcal{N}\!\left(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right) \quad \text{for large } n \]

Even when \(Y\) is skewed, the central limit theorem makes \(\bar Y\) close to Gaussian as \(n\) grows. A large \(n\) shrinks the spread. It does not remove a bias or a wrong model: that is the large-sample fallacy.

Left to right: one skewed draw, then means of 5, then means of 30. The blue curve is \(\mathcal{N}(1, 1/n)\). This is why intervals for a mean work without a Gaussian \(Y\), and why they fail when the rows are not independent. Exponential draws, seed 20261005.

Going further. The Lindeberg–Lévy central limit theorem needs independent, identically distributed rows with finite variance; Lindeberg–Feller relaxes identical distribution. Under misspecification the maximum-likelihood estimator converges to the pseudo-true \(\theta^*\), with \(\sqrt{n}(\hat\theta - \theta^*) \Rightarrow \mathcal{N}(0, A^{-1} B A^{-1})\), where \(A\) is the expected Hessian and \(B\) the variance of the score: the sandwich of Huber (1967) and White (1982). The gap \(\theta^* - \theta_0\) does not shrink with \(n\). With clustered data the relevant \(n\) is the number of clusters \(j\), not rows.

Correlation is a cosine

\[ r = \frac{\sum_{i}(x_i - \bar x)(Y_i - \bar Y)}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}(x_i - \bar x)^2}\,\sqrt{\sum_{i}(Y_i - \bar Y)^2}} = \cos\theta \]

The Pearson correlation \(r\) is the cosine of the angle \(\theta\) between centred \(x\) and \(Y\): 0° gives \(r = 1\), 90° gives \(r = 0\). It measures a straight-line association only.

This is the one piece of linear algebra the afternoon needs. Read the titles: angle and correlation move together. A curved relation can have \(r\) near 0, so plot before you quote \(r\). Simulated pairs, seed 20261005.

Going further. With centred vectors \(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\) and \(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\), \(r = \tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{y}} / (\|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\|\,\|\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\|)\), which is the cosine of their angle in \(\mathbb{R}^n\). The least-squares slope is \(\hat\beta_1 = r\, s_Y / s_x\), and in simple regression \(R^2 = r^2\). Under bivariate normality, Fisher’s \(z = \operatorname{artanh}(r)\) is approximately normal with variance \(1/(n - 3)\), which gives an interval for the population correlation. For ranks, the same cosine on ranked data is Spearman’s \(\rho\).

Multivariable is not multivariate

One \(Y\) versus several correlated outcomes: let the diagram do the work. Ask who has seen “multivariate logistic” in a paper title when the authors meant several predictors.

Say the precise word

Prefer Avoid, unless you mean it
Median and quartiles, when \(Y\) is skewed Mean and SD as the only summary
Multivariable: several covariates Multivariate: several responses
Association, for an observational coefficient Effect, when nobody assigned \(x\)
The estimate and the interval “Significant” as the whole result
Quantile: the cut point Quantile: the group the cut creates

The multivariable row is the figure already on multivariable is not multivariate.

Do not redraw the multivariable figure. Read the association row. Ask for one sentence from the room that uses effect when nobody assigned \(x\).

Two datasets

🌱 Thirty minutes, with the welcome. One count table, one designed experiment.

CA-SYS soil bacteria 🔬

The count table for this workshop is the CA-SYS 16S survey (Klockenbring et al. 2026b).

  • 120 ha of arable land at the Domaine d’Épiosses, near Dijon
  • Four pesticide-free cropping systems, sampled 2018–2021
  • 42 plots, four subplots each; 640 samples, 595 after quality control
  • 10,633 OTUs from 16S rRNA amplicon sequencing

Data and the analysis vignettes are public (Klockenbring et al. 2026a). The next slide is that record.

Two minutes. The headline is that this is a real count table with a real sampling design, not a tidy teaching matrix. The four systems are the treatment, the plot is the unit, the subplot is the repeat. Hold the statistics until the next slide. If someone asks why not NEON: the NEON soil-microbe product now requires an account for portal downloads and a token for the API, so it no longer satisfies “click a link and download”.

Recherche Data Gouv record for the CA-SYS deposit. If the frame is blank, the link on the previous slide opens in the preview overlay.

Four systems, two contrasts

System Tillage Nitrogen input
SD1 Permanent no-till —
SD2 Non-permanent no-till —
TS1 Tilled Exogenous nitrogen
TS2 Tilled None

The systems sit in three zones: a no-till zone, a tilled zone, and a mixed zone where both occur. That spatial arrangement is deliberate, and it is the reason the tillage contrast is not a clean comparison. The consequence is on the practice contrast and where it sits.

The zone structure is the key design fact to plant now. It pays off twice: in the design block, where it is a blocking question, and in the causal block, where it is confounding by construction. Do not yet give the numbers.

The zones are the design

SD1 and SD2 are the no-till systems. TS1 and TS2 are tilled, and only TS1 adds exogenous nitrogen. The mixed zone is where both practices occur.

Use the picture to fix the two contrasts: tillage runs left to right, nitrogen is only the TS1 label. The mixed panel is why a tillage coefficient is not a clean comparison. One minute, then the code.

Reading the published object

casys.R

ps <- readRDS("data/16s_RNA_sequencing/casys_phyloseq_clean.rds")

counts <- unclass(attributes(ps)$otu_table)   # 10,633 OTUs x 595 samples
taxonomy <- as.data.frame(unclass(attributes(ps)$tax_table))
meta <- as.data.frame(unclass(attributes(ps)$sam_data))

casys <- tibble(
  sample_id = colnames(counts),
  system = factor(meta$System, levels = c("SD1", "SD2", "TS1", "TS2")),
  year = as.integer(meta$Year),
  plot = meta$Parcelle,
  total_reads = colSums(counts),
  richness = colSums(counts > 0),
  nitrospira_reads = colSums(counts[taxonomy$Genus == "Nitrospira", ])
)

The deposit is a phyloseq object, built to be read with Bioconductor. This excerpt reads the same slots. prepare-teaching-data.R writes casys_teaching.csv and leaves the raw object untouched. Column meanings are in the dataset README.

Bioconductor’s phyloseq is the reader the object was made for: otu_table(), tax_table(), sample_data(). The excerpt reaches those slots without loading the package, so the deck still renders where Bioconductor is not installed. The script is the only place the teaching table is built. Offer the three download links if someone wants the files.

The teaching columns

Five rows at a time. Design, effort, and response are the three roles. The file behind the table is casys_teaching.csv.

Do not read every row. Show Design, then Effort, then Response, and say the colour is the role. Sequencing depth and total reads are both library sizes, and they are not the same number: one is the published depth, the other is recomputed after filtering. Richness, a taxon count, and a presence are the three responses on the next slide.

What \(Y\) is in that table

Three responses, three supports:

  • Reads for one taxon. A non-negative integer, with a sampling effort that differs between samples
  • Richness. The number of OTUs observed in a sample, an integer bounded by 10,633
  • Presence. Whether a taxon was seen at all, a 0/1 response

None of the three is a free Gaussian. The family that matches each is the family table.

Ask which of the three the room would have analysed with a t-test. The honest answer is usually a relative abundance, which is a fourth object again: a composition. Say that compositions are deliberately out of scope today and are on the what-next slides.

Going further. The support fixes the candidate families: \(\{0, 1, 2, \dots\}\) suggests Poisson or negative binomial, \(\{0, \dots, N\}\) with a known \(N\) suggests a binomial, and \(\{0, 1\}\) forces a Bernoulli, whose variance \(\pi(1 - \pi)\) is set by its mean. Richness is a count of a different kind: it depends on sequencing depth through the accumulation curve, and its expectation is not linear in depth, so an offset on log depth is only an approximation. Agresti’s Foundations of Linear and Generalized Linear Models sets out the families by support (Agresti 2015).

Two facts about the count table

74.6% of entries are zero, and the library size runs from 11,123 to 85,010 reads. Both numbers come from generate-data-figures.R.

These two panels do most of the work for the GLM block. The zeros kill the Gaussian story. The unequal library sizes are why a raw taxon count cannot be compared across samples, and they are the reason the offset appears later. The 74.6% figure matches the published data descriptor, which is a useful check that the object was read correctly.

Sampling effort is a covariate

If sample A has twice the reads of sample B, it has about twice the chance of showing any given taxon, with no biology involved.

Model

\[ \log \mu_i = \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \log(\text{library size}_i) \]

The last term is an offset: a column with a coefficient fixed at 1. The model is then about a rate per read, not a raw count. The fitted version is one mean, two variance assumptions.

This is the slide where a modelling idea comes from a property of the measurement rather than from a textbook. Note the offset is not a covariate whose coefficient is estimated. If someone asks about rarefying to a common depth instead: it throws away data and it does not remove the dependence, and the offset is the model-based alternative. Keep it short; the evidence is two slides later.

Going further. With exposure \(t_i\) (here the library size) and rate \(\lambda_i\), the model is \(Y_i \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(t_i \lambda_i)\) with \(\log \lambda_i = \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta\), which gives the displayed equation; \(\exp(\beta_k)\) is then a rate ratio per read. Replacing the offset by \(\log t_i\) as a covariate with a free coefficient tests proportionality: a coefficient far from 1 says that effort and counts are not proportional. Dividing the count by \(t_i\) and fitting a Gaussian model instead changes both the target and the variance structure. The same offset works unchanged in the negative binomial.

Why a Gaussian lm is the wrong first tool

  • A count cannot be negative, and a Gaussian mean can predict a negative count
  • The variance of these counts rises with the mean, so one \(\sigma^2\) does not fit
  • A proportion is bounded, and a rise in one taxon is a fall in another’s share
  • Zeros are a property of community tables, not rows to delete

The evidence for the second line is on the mean against variance slide. The deep dive is after the pitfalls.

Going further. Each line is a statement about the variance function \(V(\mu)\): a Gaussian model fixes \(V(\mu) = 1\), a Poisson \(V(\mu) = \mu\), a binomial share \(V(\mu) = \mu(1 - \mu)/N\). Wedderburn’s quasi-likelihood (1974) shows that a mean model plus a correct \(V(\mu)\) is enough for consistent estimates and valid standard errors, without the full distribution. Transforming first, as \(\log(Y + 1)\), makes the result depend on the arbitrary constant and handles the zeros badly; O’Hara and Kotze compare the two approaches by simulation (📄 Do not log-transform count data, Methods in Ecology and Evolution, 2010).

Mean against variance, for every OTU

9,924 of 10,603 OTUs have a variance above their mean. A Poisson fixes those two quantities to be equal, which is the assumption the negative binomial relaxes.

This is the real version of the simulated mean-variance figure the deck used to show. Each point is one OTU: its mean across the 595 samples against its variance. The diagonal is the Poisson. Note honestly that a few hundred OTUs sit on or below the line, which is what you expect for the very rare ones where almost every count is zero.

Going further. A Poisson has variance function \(V(\mu) = \mu\). The negative binomial arises as a Poisson whose mean is itself gamma-distributed, giving \(V(\mu) = \mu + \mu^2/\theta\); quasi-Poisson keeps \(V(\mu) = \phi\mu\) and estimates \(\phi\) from the Pearson statistic \(\sum_i (y_i - \hat\mu_i)^2/\hat\mu_i\) divided by \(n - p\). The two make different predictions on this plot: a quadratic rise for the negative binomial, a line parallel to the diagonal for quasi-Poisson. Here each OTU’s variance also mixes samples of different depth and system, so part of the excess is unmodelled mean structure rather than biology.

Four analyses, only one of them today

Approach What it targets
Compare proportions between systems A share, with a false independence story
Poisson or negative binomial, with an offset \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\) for a count, per read
Log-ratio (CLR and its relatives) A composition, after the zeros are handled
Absolute against relative Whether the taxon changed, or only its share

Today we fit the count models and the logit. Compositions return on the what-next slides.

This is the pedagogical contract. Do not start a CLR derivation. If pressed, say that a log-ratio needs a rule for the 74.6% zeros before any transformation, and that this rule is a scientific decision rather than a default.

The hierarchy in the sampling

The cropping system is a property of the plot. Four subplots in one plot are not four independent trials of that system. 2018 was sampled before the treatments were applied, so it is a baseline rather than a treated year.

Two things to flag. The subplot point is the replication pitfall, three hours early; the design block returns to it as a random intercept on the plot. The baseline year matters because a naive model with year as a factor will happily report a 2018 “effect” of a treatment that did not exist yet. That is a design fact you can only get from the dataset documentation.

Going further. Hurlbert (📄 Pseudoreplication and the design of ecological field experiments, Ecological Monographs, 1984) defines pseudoreplication as testing a treatment effect with an error term taken from a level below the experimental unit. Here the system is assigned to the plot, so the denominator for the system contrast must come from plot-to-plot variation; a random intercept \(u_j\) on the plot does that. With a baseline, the usual efficient analysis adjusts the post-treatment response for the 2018 value (an ANCOVA-type model) rather than using change scores or a year factor.

SPRUCE 🌲

A whole-ecosystem warming experiment on the S1 bog, Marcell Experimental Forest, Minnesota.

  • Question on the design page: how do plants, microbes, and ecosystem processes respond to warming, and how does elevated \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) change that response?
  • Air and deep peat warmed from ambient to \(+9^\circ\mathrm{C}\)
  • Elevated \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) atmospheres of about 800–900 ppm
  • Source: SPRUCE experimental design

The next slide is that page.

All treatment numbers on the following slides are taken from that page, which cites Hanson et al. (2017) for the temperature differential and Cottingham et al. (2005) for regression designs. Do not expand those citations beyond what the page states. Two minutes. If the frame is blank, use the link.

Ten enclosures, not a two-group test

Ambient and elevated \(\mathrm{CO}_2\), each at \(+0\), \(+2.25\), \(+4.5\), \(+6.75\), and \(+9^\circ\mathrm{C}\).

spruce-grid.R

spruce |>
  distinct(plot, warming_c, co2, chambered) |>
  arrange(plot)
#> plots 4, 10, 11, 16, 19 are the elevated-CO2 enclosures
#> plots 7 and 21 are unchambered ambient plots, with no assigned warming

Twelve plots: ten chambered enclosures on the warming grid, and two ambient plots that were never assigned a level. The teaching table is spruce_teaching.csv, built by prepare-teaching-data.R from the published copy-number file.

Run the distinct() call in front of the room if the laptop allows. The point is that the design is recoverable from the data, and that the two ambient plots are a different kind of row: they have no warming level, so they cannot enter a regression on warming without a decision about what to do with them. That decision is a modelling choice, and it belongs in the analysis plan.

The grid, drawn

Each circle is one enclosure, and it receives one warming step and one CO₂ atmosphere. The two open squares were never assigned a warming level.

Count to ten circles, then point at the two open squares. Those two cannot sit on a warming slope until the analysis plan says what their warming value is. Do not invent plot numbers: the sketch is the grid, not a map of which plot is which step.

What is measured here

The response in this dataset is a gene copy number per gram of dry peat, from quantitative PCR (Roth et al. 2024).

  • 12 plots \(\times\) 9 depth increments \(\times\) 2 sampling months in 2021: 216 rows
  • Bacterial, archaeal, and fungal copies, each with a log column
  • Missing numeric values are coded \(-9999\) in the published file

A qPCR copy number is an estimated abundance, not a counted integer. It is a positive, strongly skewed continuous response, so it belongs with the transformation discussion and not with the Poisson.

Be explicit that this is not a count, even though it looks like one. Treating a qPCR abundance as Poisson would be a category error, and it is a mistake the room will have seen. The sentinel value is the other teaching point: 15 values in this file are -9999, and a script that reads the column as a number without that rule will report a mean of about -9999 times the missing fraction. The rule lives in prepare-teaching-data.R and nowhere else.

The sentinel is a documented rule

sentinels.R

# "Missing numeric data are indicated by -9999" (dataset documentation)
numeric_cols <- c("Bacteria_copy_dry", "Archaea_copy_dry", "Fungal_copy_dry")
for (nm in numeric_cols) {
  spruce_raw[[nm]][spruce_raw[[nm]] == -9999] <- NA_real_
}

Fifteen values across the nine numeric columns are sentinels. One of the 216 bacterial measurements is missing.

Short slide. The message is that the rule came from the dataset documentation, not from a guess, and that it is written once in a script rather than repeated in every analysis. Link it forward to the missing-data pitfall: this is missingness you can describe, which is the easy case.

The scale of the response

On the log scale the fall with depth is about \(-0.079\) in \(\log_{10}\) copies per 10 cm, and depth alone accounts for \(R^2 = 0.23\).

The left panel is why the dataset ships a log column. The right panel is the transformation argument in its legitimate form: the transform is chosen because the mechanism is multiplicative, not because a normality test failed. Flag that this slope is a description of 12 plots at one site in one year, not a global law, and that the interval for it needs the plot structure on the next slide.

Going further. If \(\log Y\) is normal with mean \(m\) and variance \(\sigma^2\), then \(\mathbb{E}(Y) = \exp(m + \sigma^2/2)\), so back-transforming a fitted mean on the log scale gives the median, not the mean. Duan’s smearing estimator (📄 Smearing estimate: a nonparametric retransformation method, Journal of the American Statistical Association, 1983) corrects this without assuming normality. A Gamma or log-normal GLM with a log link targets \(\log \mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x})\) directly. \(R^2\) depends on the range of depth sampled, so it is a property of this design, not of the process.

One line per plot

Nine depths inside one plot are not nine independent plots. Where the replicate is depends on the question: where the replicate is.

This figure is the hierarchy made visible. Each line is a plot; the points on it are depths. A regression on 215 rows that ignores the plot has about 12 units of real information about warming, not 215. That is the same arithmetic as the CA-SYS subplots. Both datasets make the same point, which is why the design block can treat them together.

Going further. With \(m\) rows per plot and intraclass correlation \(\rho\), the effective sample size for a plot-level contrast is \(n/\{1 + (m - 1)\rho\}\). Here \(m\) is about 18 (nine depths by two months), so even a modest \(\rho = 0.3\) cuts 215 rows to about 35, and as \(\rho \to 1\) it tends to the 12 plots. For a covariate that varies within plots, such as depth, the plot effect instead removes between-plot noise and can increase precision.

The model the design is asking for

For plot \(j\), depth \(i\):

Model

\[ \log_{10} Y_{ij} = \beta_0 + \beta_1 \mathrm{depth}_{ij} + \beta_2 x_{1j} + \beta_3 x_{2j} + \beta_4 x_{1j} x_{2j} + u_j + \varepsilon_{ij} \]

  • \(x_{1j}\): warming assigned to the enclosure
  • \(x_{2j}\): elevated \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) against ambient
  • \(u_j\): plot-level departure, \(u_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma_u^2)\)

A \(t\)-test of “warm against cool” is a different estimand, and a poorer one.

Point at the indices: j is the plot, i is the depth increment. The interaction is the scientific question the design was built for, which is why binning the five warming levels into two groups throws the design away. Two minutes, then move on: the fitting details are in the design block.

Going further. \(\sigma_u^2\) is usually estimated by restricted maximum likelihood (Patterson and Thompson, 1971), which maximises the likelihood of error contrasts and removes the downward bias of the ML variance estimate, analogous to dividing by \(n - p\). Because \(x_{1j}\) and \(x_{2j}\) vary only between plots, their standard errors are driven by about 12 plots minus the plot-level parameters, so small-sample corrections to the denominator degrees of freedom (Satterthwaite, Kenward–Roger) matter. The regression design across five warming levels uses the gradient to estimate a slope, which is more powerful than an ANOVA on binned levels when the response is close to linear (Cottingham et al., 2005).

Two datasets, one syllabus

Response Dataset Family
\(\log_{10}\) gene copies SPRUCE Gaussian, identity link
Taxon reads, per read CA-SYS Poisson, then negative binomial
Taxon presence CA-SYS Bernoulli, logit link
Richness CA-SYS A count that is not Poisson

Same ecological story throughout: carbon and nitrogen cycling in soil. The statistics change because the response changes.

This table is the map for the next two hours, and it is worth leaving up for a moment. The last row is deliberately awkward: richness is an integer, so the reflex is Poisson, and the data say otherwise by a factor of 28. We come back to it.

Going further. All four rows are exponential-dispersion families, \(f(y) = \exp[\{y\theta - b(\theta)\}/\phi + c(y, \phi)]\), with \(\mu = b'(\theta)\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \phi\, b''(\theta) = \phi V(\mu)\). Choosing a family is choosing \(V(\mu)\); the link is a separate choice, and the canonical one is \(g = (b')^{-1}\). That shared form is why one fitting algorithm, iteratively reweighted least squares, and one deviance-based toolkit serve every row. See Agresti (Agresti 2015).

Twelve pitfalls

📐 About fifty minutes, including ten minutes to write your own PICO.

A poster, then one rule at a time

The poster is a visual summary of Riley and colleagues and of the SAMPL reporting principles (Riley et al. 2022).

Open access PDF · CC BY-NC 4.0

Do not read the poster. Name the key principle along the bottom: align the question, the estimand, the data structure, the assumptions, and the report. About one minute.

The twelve, in the order we will use

  1. Start from the question
  2. Report the effect, not only \(p\)
  3. Handle missing data on purpose
  4. Do not dichotomise
  5. Allow a curve
  6. Quantify a subgroup difference
  1. Read heterogeneity carefully
  2. Check a prediction in three ways
  3. Respect clusters and repeats
  4. Select covariates from a causal sketch
  5. Sensitivity is part of the result
  6. Report so a reader can redo it

The poster numbers are the workshop numbers. The BMJ article uses the same twelve ideas, in a slightly different order.

Read the left column down, then the right. These numbers are the roadmap for the next hour. Thirty seconds.

1 · Start from the question

1 · Six purposes, six different \(\mu\)

  • Describe a pattern
  • Compare groups
  • Explain an intervention
  • Predict a new unit
  • Explore and generate
  • Confirm a pre-specified claim

A significant coefficient does not say which of these you did. The three we will separate are explain, predict, and confirm.

Riley and colleagues ask authors to say whether the work is descriptive or causal, prognostic or predictive, exploratory or confirmatory. One minute.

1 · From a slogan to a statistical question

Vague: “What is the effect of drought on forest carbon?”

Sharper, still one estimand:

  • difference in what? Net \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) flux, in stated units
  • between which conditions? Rain exclusion versus control
  • over which period, and on which population of sites?
  • at which unit? The plot that received the exclusion, not the technical replicate

Work one participant’s slogan into this shape if time allows. Two minutes.

1 · The estimand is the target, not the software

The estimand is the quantity you claim to estimate.

  • a difference in tonnes of C per hectare
  • a flux ratio
  • a slope against warming
  • a mean over seasons, or a mean for one season
  • a site-specific effect, or an effect you want to transport to new sites

Changing the unit, the contrast, or the set of covariates changes the estimand. It is not a cosmetic choice of summary.

Ask someone to name their estimand in one clause before you show PICO. If they say “the regression output”, push back: the software fits a quantity; you have to name it. One minute.

Going further. The ICH E9(R1) addendum on estimands (2019) formalises an estimand through five attributes: the population, the treatment conditions compared, the variable \(Y\), the handling of intercurrent events, and the population-level summary (difference of means, ratio, odds ratio). In potential-outcome notation a typical target is \(\mathbb{E}\{Y(1) - Y(0)\}\), which is a property of the population, not of a fitted model. A regression coefficient equals that target only under conditions on the design (randomisation or no unmeasured confounding), on the model (the mean is correctly specified), and on the scale: for a non-collapsible summary such as an odds ratio, the conditional and marginal estimands differ even without confounding. The estimator, \(\hat\beta\) from a GLM say, is then judged by its bias and variance for that named target.

1 · PICO, when the question is a comparison

For a designed contrast or a systematic comparison, name population, intervention or exposure, comparison, and outcome (Riley et al. 2022).

Walk through one row of the figure aloud: population is not “the dataset”, it is the system and the unit that carries the contrast. Do not linger; the exercise slide is next.

1 · Ten minutes: write your PICO 📝

Four lines, for your dataset.

  1. Population or system, and the unit
  2. Intervention, exposure, or factor
  3. Comparison
  4. Outcome \(Y\), and the estimand in one clause

Protect ten minutes. Walk the room. Collect two examples on the board. Do not advance until most people have four lines. The next slide is one possible answer, not the answer.

1 · One possible answer, after you have written

For the SPRUCE enclosures:

Population S1 bog, the enclosure as the experimental unit
Intervention Assigned warming, \(+0\) to \(+9^\circ\mathrm{C}\)
Comparison The \(+0^\circ\mathrm{C}\) enclosure at the same \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) level
Outcome Mean soil \(\mathrm{CH}_4\) flux; estimand: slope on warming, and the warming \(\times \mathrm{CO}_2\) interaction

Your four lines will not look like this. They need to name your unit.

Stress that SPRUCE is one honest answer, not the template. If someone’s unit is a core while the treatment sits on the enclosure, that mismatch is exactly what you want them to notice.

1 · Start with the question

Question Target
Causal What would change in \(Y\) if the exposure \(x\) changed
Prediction How close \(\hat{\mu}\) is to \(Y\) for a new unit
Exploratory Hypotheses to test later; the fit is not the final claim
Confirmatory A contrast and covariates fixed before the data

Write down which row you are answering. The comparison form is PICO.

Point at the first two rows. Ask the room which branch their PICO belongs to. Many carbon papers mix explanation and prediction in one sentence; naming the branch is the fix. Ninety seconds.

1 · Explain, predict, or confirm

  • Explain. Which covariates, and how large is \(\beta\)? The coefficient and its interval are the result.
  • Predict. How close is \(\hat{Y}\) on a unit the fit did not see? The score is a prediction error, not a \(p\)-value.
  • Confirm. The contrast, the covariates, and the claim were written down before the fit. A search until something is significant is exploratory.

MetricGate (April 2025) works the first two through a linear model and a random forest. Confirmatory is the pre-specification on the six purposes. The checks for a prediction are three different questions.

Step the fragments slowly. The common mistake is to treat a small \(p\) on \(\hat\beta_j\) as proof of good prediction. Ask which metric they would put in the abstract for each purpose. Two minutes.

Going further. Shmueli’s article “To explain or to predict?” (Statistical Science, 2010) is the standard reference. The expected squared prediction error at a new \(\mathbf{x}\) decomposes as \(\sigma^2 + \mathrm{bias}^2\{\hat\mu(\mathbf{x})\} + \mathrm{Var}\{\hat\mu(\mathbf{x})\}\), so a deliberately biased but less variable model (dropped covariates, shrinkage) can predict better than the correctly specified one. Explanation instead needs \(\hat\beta_j\) to be consistent for a named causal or structural quantity, which is a statement about bias, not about total error. Confirmatory inference adds a third requirement: the error rate of the test is only valid if the hypothesis, the covariates and the analysis were fixed before the data, otherwise the selection step must be accounted for (post-selection inference, Berk and colleagues, Annals of Statistics, 2013).

1 · Two different scores

Prediction, on new units:

\[\mathrm{RMSE} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_i - \hat{Y}_i)^2}\]

Explanation, for one coefficient, under the linear model:

\[\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} = (\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}, \qquad t = \hat\beta_j / \mathrm{SE}(\hat\beta_j)\]

A small RMSE does not say that \(\hat\beta_j\) is the effect of changing \(x_j\).

You do not need to derive the normal equations. The takeaway is two symbols on the slide: RMSE for prediction, \(t\) for one coefficient. If someone asks about causality, park it until the covariate slide.

Going further. The least-squares estimator solves the normal equations \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{0}\). Under \(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y} \mid \mathbf{X}) = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{Y} \mid \mathbf{X}) = \sigma^2 \mathbf{I}\), the Gauss–Markov theorem makes it the best linear unbiased estimator, with \(\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2 (\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\); with Gaussian errors, \(t\) follows a Student distribution on \(n - p\) degrees of freedom. None of this says that \(\beta_j\) is causal: it is the coefficient of the best linear predictor given the other columns, and it changes whenever a correlated covariate is added or removed. RMSE computed on the training rows is optimistic by roughly \(2p\sigma^2/n\) in squared error (Mallows’ \(C_p\)), which is why the prediction score is computed on held-out rows (Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman, The Elements of Statistical Learning, ch. 7).

1 · The simulation that makes the contrast

Truth uses only \(X_1\) and \(X_2\). Three further covariates are noise. \(n = 200\). Seeds 123 for the draw and 42 for the split. The excerpt is MetricGate’s script. It is not refit here.

Random forest

The flexible predictor. Held-out RMSE 2.40.

forest.R

rf_fit <- randomForest(Y ~ ., data = train)

The trees chased the noise covariates. A more flexible fit predicted worse.

Linear model

The explanatory fit. Held-out RMSE 1.01.

linear.R

Y <- 3 * X1 - 2 * X2 + rnorm(n)
lm_fit <- lm(Y ~ ., data = train)

The coefficients are the explanatory object, and here they also predicted better.

Say the seeds once. Pause on 2.40 versus 1.01. Someone will say forests always predict better; this draw is the counter-example, because the mean really is linear. The forest is not bad software. It is the wrong tool for an explanatory coefficient.

2 · Report the effect, not only \(p\)

2 · An effect, an interval, and a reason it matters

The two intervals are the published example in Riley and colleagues (Riley et al. 2022).

Two intervals, two different failures. The narrow one is precise and small: detected, and possibly irrelevant. The wide one is large and uncertain: absence of evidence, not evidence of absence. The CA-SYS rate ratio later today is the first kind, and the tillage richness contrast is the second. Neither is read off a p-value.

Going further. A \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval is the set of null values \(\theta_0\) that a level-\(\alpha\) test would not reject, so it carries strictly more information than one \(p\) against \(\theta_0 = 0\) (test–interval duality, Hogg, McKean and Craig, Introduction to Mathematical Statistics (Hogg et al. 2019)). For a ratio such as a risk ratio the interval is built on the log scale, \(\log\widehat{\mathrm{RR}} \pm 1.96\,\mathrm{SE}\), and exponentiated, which is why the two intervals on the figure are asymmetric. The coverage is a property of the procedure over replicates; a given interval either contains the true value or it does not.

2 · Detection is not importance

  • Statistical evidence: could the data have come from a world with no effect?

  • Practical evidence: a minimal important difference, set before the fit. It is not a formula, and \(p\) does not supply it (Olszewski)

  • Replace the clinical threshold with a flux, a stock, or a decision that would change

  • Keep the estimate and the interval in the sentence (Halsey et al. 2015)

This slide used to be two, which said the same thing twice. The one idea is that significance and importance are separate judgements and only one of them comes from the data. Ask the room what change in soil carbon would alter a management decision. Most groups have never written that number down, which is the point. In a confirmatory analysis it is written before the fit.

2 · A yes-or-no decision has two errors

A Type I error rejects the null when it is true; its long-run rate, when the test is calibrated, is \(\alpha\). A Type II error fails to reject the null when it is false; its rate is \(\beta\), and the power is \(1 - \beta\) (Biswas).

The table is a decision table, and it only makes sense once you have agreed to make a yes-or-no decision. That is a choice, not an obligation. The two rates are properties of the procedure across many replicates, not statements about the study in front of you. The next slide is the same table as a smoke alarm.

Going further. This is the Neyman–Pearson framework: fix \(\alpha\), then choose the rejection region that maximises power \(1 - \beta(\theta)\) at each alternative. The Neyman–Pearson lemma gives the most powerful test of a simple null against a simple alternative, the likelihood-ratio test; uniformly most powerful tests exist only in special cases, such as one-sided tests in a one-parameter exponential family. Power depends on the true effect, the noise and the sample size: for a two-sample \(z\) test with \(n\) per arm and standard deviation \(\sigma\), \(1-\beta \approx \Phi\left(|\delta|\sqrt{n/2}/\sigma - z_{1-\alpha/2}\right)\). The Type I and Type II rates are not the error rates of the claims made; that needs the prior share of true nulls, as on the \(\alpha\) slide.

2 · A smoke alarm

The null is: there is no fire.

  • Type I: the alarm rings, and the building is empty. You evacuate for nothing.
  • Type II: the building is burning, and the alarm stays quiet.

Power is the alarm ringing when there is a fire. \(\alpha\) is how often a quiet building still sets it off. A soil-carbon test has the same two cells, and a different cost for each one.

Ask which failure they would rather live with in a hallway, then which one they would rather live with in a management decision. The costs are not symmetric, and the table does not choose them. Do not turn this into a lecture on alarms.

Going further. The alarm is a Neyman–Pearson test with a fixed false-alarm rate \(\alpha\). Making it more sensitive, a smaller threshold, lowers \(\beta\) and raises \(\alpha\): fewer silent fires, more empty evacuations. No threshold removes both. The same trade is the receiver operating characteristic, power against the false-alarm rate as the threshold moves.

2 · \(p\) is not that rate

If the treatment has no effect, a \(p\) of 0.03 means that 3% of replicate studies would obtain an effect this large or larger.

  • Comparing that number with \(\alpha\) is the yes-or-no decision
  • The number itself is a probability about the data, not about the hypothesis
  • \(\alpha\) is fixed before the data; \(p\) is computed from them

The careful statement is the replicate definition, and the rate of false assertions is \(\alpha\).

The commonest error in the room will be to read p as the probability that the null is true. Say the two conditional probabilities out loud: P(data this extreme given no effect) against P(no effect given the data). Only the first is available from a frequentist test, and only the second is what most readers want.

Going further. For a continuous test statistic \(T\) with null distribution \(F_0\), \(p = 1 - F_0(T_{\mathrm{obs}})\) is itself a random variable, and under the null it is exactly uniform on \((0, 1)\) (probability integral transform). Rejecting when \(p \le \alpha\) therefore has size \(\alpha\), which is the only sense in which \(p\) and \(\alpha\) meet. Under an alternative, \(p\) is stochastically smaller, but its distribution is wide unless power is high. Bayes’ theorem gives \(P(H_0 \mid \text{data}) = P(\text{data} \mid H_0) P(H_0) / P(\text{data})\): turning \(p\) into a posterior requires a prior and a likelihood under the alternative, which a test does not supply.

2 · A p-value is about the data

Under identical replicates of this experiment, with the effect set to zero, \(p\) is the probability of a result more extreme than the one in hand (Harrell, ch. 22). 📄 Thiese, Ronna, and Ott collect the same reading in P value interpretations and considerations.

A \(p\) of 0.06 says that, under the model and the null, a replicate would be more extreme about 6 times in 100. It does not say that the null has probability 0.06.

Write “given \(H_0\)” on the board if you have one. The 0.06 example is deliberately awkward — neither side of 0.05 — to keep the focus on the conditional, not on the threshold ritual.

Going further. Formally \(p = P_{H_0}(T \ge t_{\mathrm{obs}})\), a tail area computed under every assumption of the model, not only the zero effect: independence, the error distribution, the sampling plan and the choice of \(T\). “More extreme” needs an ordering of possible results, so a one-sided and a two-sided test give different \(p\) for the same data. With a discrete statistic (counts, ranks) \(p\) is not exactly uniform under the null and the test is conservative; mid-\(p\) values correct part of this. Harrell’s Biostatistics for Biomedical Research, chapter on hypothesis testing, and the ASA statement (Wasserstein and Lazar, The American Statistician, 2016) are the standard references.

2 · α is a rate of false assertions

\(\alpha\) is the long-run probability of asserting an effect when the effect is exactly zero. It is not the probability that a positive result is false, and it is not “the probability of an error”.

A procedure that assumes the null cannot report the probability that the null is true. Fisher’s 1956 wording treated a 1% test as a 1% rate of mistaken decisions, which swaps \(P(\text{assert} \mid H_0)\) with \(P(H_0 \mid \text{assert})\) (Harrell).

\(\alpha\) is chosen before data; \(p\) is computed after. The Fisher quote is the Bayesian confusion in one line — read it slowly.

Going further. The probability that a positive result is false is the false discovery proportion among assertions. With a prior share \(\pi_0\) of true nulls, \(P(H_0 \mid \text{assert}) = \alpha\pi_0 / \{\alpha\pi_0 + (1-\beta)(1-\pi_0)\}\). With \(\alpha = 0.05\), power \(0.8\) and \(\pi_0 = 0.9\), that is \(0.045 / (0.045 + 0.08) \approx 0.36\), far above \(5\%\); Ioannidis (“Why most published research findings are false”, PLoS Medicine, 2005) and Colquhoun (Royal Society Open Science, 2014) work this arithmetic. It is the same quantity that the Benjamini–Hochberg procedure controls in expectation over many tests (Farcomeni 2008).

2 · One P, or a screen of them

  • Halsey and colleagues simulated a small normal experiment and showed a single \(P\) jumping across 0.05 from sample to sample. They called that fickleness a source of irreproducible decisions (Halsey et al. 2015).

  • van Helden repeated the simulation. The interval crossed the null in the same runs where \(P > 0.05\), so swapping the interval for \(P\) did not change the decision. He put the instability on the small sample, not on an intrinsic property of \(P\) (van Helden 2016).

  • Halsey, Curran-Everett, and Drummond replied that the shared mathematics was not the recommendation. The recommendation was to read the effect and its margin of error, rather than to re-apply a threshold (Halsey et al. 2016).

Step all three fragments. The van Helden point matters for methodologists; for this room, fragment three is the punchline. Do not re-litigate NHST — point back to the effect slide.

Going further. Under an alternative with power \(1-\beta\) the distribution of \(p\) is strongly right-skewed: Cumming (Perspectives on Psychological Science, 2008) showed that an 80% prediction interval for the \(p\) of an exact replication, given an initial \(p = 0.05\), runs from about \(0.00008\) to \(0.44\). A \(p\) is a monotone function of the standardised estimate \(\hat\theta/\mathrm{SE}\), so it inherits all of that estimate’s sampling variability on a strongly non-linear scale, which is van Helden’s point. The interval keeps the scale of the effect, which is Halsey’s.

2 · A planned question is not a screen

Huber called the exchange a clash of cultures (Huber 2016).

  • A craftsman reports one \(P\) for one question written in advance.
  • An industrialist uses \(P\) to screen thousands of hypotheses; the histogram of all of them is part of the evidence.
  • One \(P\) can fall anywhere in \((0,1)\) when the null is true; the distribution of many \(P\) values is a different object.
  • Both sides agree that \(P\) alone is an insufficient report. 📝 StatHarbor, Multiple testing correction. 📄 Qin Ma and colleagues, Ad hoc, post hoc and intrinsic-hoc in bioinformatics.

Genomics and high-throughput ecology are the industrialist case. Ask whether anyone has seen a “significant” result from a fishing expedition without multiplicity control. One minute.

Going further. With \(m\) tests the histogram of \(p\) is a mixture \(\pi_0\,\mathrm{U}(0,1) + (1-\pi_0) f_1\), so its flat part estimates the share of true nulls \(\pi_0\) (Storey’s \(q\)-value method). Bonferroni controls the family-wise error rate, the chance of even one false rejection, by testing each hypothesis at \(\alpha/m\); Benjamini and Hochberg control the expected false discovery proportion by comparing the ordered \(p_{(k)}\) with \(k\alpha/m\), which is valid under independence or positive dependence (Farcomeni 2008). Closed testing and gatekeeping procedures for structured confirmatory families are in Bretz, Hothorn and Westfall (Bretz et al. 2016).

2 · Five readings to retire

Lantz lists five things a \(p\)-value is not:

  • the probability the result is due to chance
  • the probability a repeat will match
  • the probability the null is false
  • the probability the result matters
  • the probability it generalises

The first three confuse a probability about the data with a probability about the hypothesis (the definition). The last two are the effect and the interval.

Read two items, ask the room to finish the list from memory, then show the rest. The next slide puts one situation on each line. Forty seconds.

2 · Five situations, none of them \(p\)

  • Due to chance. A rise in soil carbon with \(p = 0.04\) is not “a 96% chance it was not luck”. The 4% is how often a flat field would look this steep.
  • A repeat will match. That \(p\) does not give the next enclosure a 96% chance of the same call. A repeat is a new experiment.
  • The null is false. \(p = 0.01\) is not a 99% chance that the treatment works.
  • The result matters. A drug can clear \(p < 0.05\) and still fall short of a change a patient would feel. Statistical significance is not therapeutic value.
  • It generalises. A small \(p\) on one bog, in one year, does not carry the result to the next site.

The \(p\) values are made up, to show the misreading. Linger on the drug: the missing number is the smallest change that would alter a prescription, written before the test. The bog is the same point in their own units. Then Type M.

Going further. “Matters” is a comparison of the estimate and its interval with a minimal important difference, fixed in advance. “Generalises” is a question about the population the sample represents, which no tail probability answers. A prediction interval for a new site, or a new trial, is a different functional from the \(p\) of the fitted contrast.

2 · A small study can detect the wrong size

Gelman and Carlin: Type M is how many times too large a significant estimate is. Type S is how often that significant estimate has the wrong sign.

Type M and Type S only apply when someone filters on “significant”. The scatter is the visual; the definitions are the labels. Link to low power in the next slide.

Going further. With an estimate \(\hat\theta \sim N(\theta, s^2)\), the Type S rate is \(P(\hat\theta < -z s) / \{P(\hat\theta > z s) + P(\hat\theta < -z s)\}\) for \(\theta > 0\), and the exaggeration ratio is \(\mathbb{E}(|\hat\theta| \mid |\hat\theta| > z s)/\theta\), both functions of \(\theta/s\) only. Gelman and Carlin’s retrodesign calculation evaluates them at a plausible effect size taken from outside the study, not at the observed estimate, which would be circular. This is the winner’s curse: selecting on significance is a truncation, and the truncated mean is biased away from zero.

2 · Those significant runs were about eleven times too big

Olszewski drew the previous figure from two normal samples, \(n = 10\) each, standard deviation 10, true difference 1, seed 1000, 10,000 \(t\) tests.

Power is about 5.5%. The significant estimates average about 11 times the true difference, and about a quarter to a third of them point the wrong way. A small \(p\) from a small study is a reason to doubt the size, which is why the effect stays in the sentence.

The seed and sample sizes are on the slide so nobody thinks you made up the 11×. Emphasise conditional on significance — most runs are not significant and are not shown.

Going further. The numbers follow from the retrodesign formulas: \(\mathrm{SE} = 10\sqrt{2/10} \approx 4.47\), so \(\theta/\mathrm{SE} \approx 0.22\), the two-sided power at \(\alpha = 0.05\) is just above \(5\%\), and only estimates beyond about \(\pm 9.4\) (the \(t_{18}\) critical value times the SE) can reach significance. A true difference of 1 can then only be declared through an estimate roughly ten times too large, in either direction, which is why the sign is so often wrong. Button and colleagues (Nature Reviews Neuroscience, 2013) document the same mechanism across a literature.

2 · Power pulls the exaggeration down

On his curve the inflation is several-fold below about 10% power, and close to 1 once power passes about 80%. The same contrast is Lukas Vermeer’s simulator.

If time is short, skip the URL and point at the curve: design for power before you trust a significant estimate’s magnitude. Bridge into missing data: bias that does not shrink with \(n\) is a different slide in the heterogeneity block.

Going further. Ioannidis (Epidemiology, 2008) and Gelman and Carlin (Perspectives on Psychological Science, 2014) give the curve analytically: the exaggeration ratio falls to near 1 by about 50% power and is close to 1 at 80%, under a normal approximation. Power must be computed for an effect worth detecting, fixed in advance; “observed power” computed from the study’s own estimate is a one-to-one function of \(p\) and adds no information (Hoenig and Heisey, The American Statistician, 2001).

3 · Handle missing data on purpose

3 · Missing data ❓

❓

Write both answers before the next slide.

  1. What are the three mechanisms that put missingness into the distribution of \((Y, \mathbf{x})\)?
  2. What would you do about each, and which of those repairs do you think are the wrong ones?

Two minutes, aloud. Do not name MCAR, MAR, or MNAR until someone offers them. The card with the avoid and prefer lists is the last slide of this block.

3 · Three mechanisms

Figure credit on the source image: @daansan_ml.

Name the three letters only after the room has tried. MCAR is the idealisation; MNAR is where honest papers stop pretending listwise deletion is neutral.

Going further. Rubin (Biometrika, 1976) defines the mechanisms through the distribution of the missingness indicator \(R\) given the full data: MCAR when \(P(R \mid Y_{\mathrm{obs}}, Y_{\mathrm{mis}}) = P(R)\), MAR when it depends only on \(Y_{\mathrm{obs}}\), and MNAR otherwise. Under MAR, and if the parameters of the missingness model are distinct from those of the data model, the mechanism is ignorable for likelihood and Bayesian inference: the observed-data likelihood alone gives valid estimates. Complete-case analysis is unbiased under MCAR, and for a regression also when missingness depends only on the covariates \(\mathbf{x}\), not on \(Y\). MAR cannot be tested against MNAR from the observed data alone, which is why MNAR calls for a sensitivity analysis (Little and Rubin, Statistical Analysis with Missing Data).

3 · The same idea as a graph

\(R\), the missingness indicator, has to be in the story. If \(R\) depends on the unobserved value of \(Y\), a model that only conditions on the observed covariates does not fix the bias.

The dog-and-homework DAG is memorable; tie \(R\) to “was this row kept in the analysis?”. MNAR is when the dog eats because the homework was bad, not because of assignment.

Going further. Mohan and Pearl’s missingness graphs (Journal of the American Statistical Association, 2021) add \(R\) and the proxy \(Y^*\) to the causal graph and answer a sharper question than Rubin’s taxonomy: is the target quantity recoverable from the observed data? Some MNAR structures are still recoverable, for instance when \(R_Y\) depends on another partially observed variable but not on \(Y\) itself; when \(R_Y\) depends on \(Y\) directly, the distribution of \(Y\) is not identified without extra assumptions, such as a selection model or a pattern-mixture model with a sensitivity parameter.

3 · Missing data are part of the design

Avoid Prefer
Zero or mean fill Report extent of missingness in \(Y\) and in \(x\)
Indiscriminate complete-case deletion Name the mechanism you assume; multiple imputation when MAR is defensible

Report how much is missing, in \(Y\) and in \(x\), and what you did. Zero-fill, a mean fill, and dropping every incomplete row are assumptions, not defaults (Riley et al. 2022). Definitions: three mechanisms.

This is the answer to the opening question. Ask which row someone has used as a default. Complete case is fine only when you can defend MCAR or a clear MAR story.

Going further. Multiple imputation draws \(M\) completed datasets from the posterior predictive distribution of the missing values, fits the analysis model to each, and pools with Rubin’s rules: the estimate is the mean \(\bar\theta\) and the variance is \(\bar W + (1 + 1/M)B\), with \(\bar W\) the mean within-imputation variance and \(B\) the between-imputation variance. The imputation model must contain the outcome \(Y\) and every variable of the analysis model, including interactions and non-linear terms, otherwise the associations are biased towards zero. Mean imputation underestimates the variance and attenuates correlations; a single imputation, however good, ignores \(B\). Van Buuren’s Flexible Imputation of Missing Data is the practical reference for chained equations (mice).

4 · Do not dichotomise

4 · Do not dichotomise a continuous \(x\)

Cost of a cut Better
Loses order; unstable threshold Keep \(x\) continuous: \(Y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \epsilon\)

A threshold throws away order, spends power, and makes the result depend on the cut (Riley et al. 2022). Altman and Royston (2006) is the short note.

The absurd pairing is the teaching point: 131 with 220, not with 129. Ask if anyone has binned soil moisture at a round number because the software defaulted to tertiles.

Going further. For a normal \(x\) split at its median, the correlation with the dichotomised variable is \(\sqrt{2/\pi} \approx 0.80\) of the original, so the efficiency is \(2/\pi \approx 0.64\): about a third of the sample is thrown away (Cohen, Applied Psychological Measurement, 1983). Splits away from the median lose more. The step function also misspecifies \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\) within each group, which leaves residual confounding when \(x\) is a confounder. Royston, Altman and Sauerbrei (Statistics in Medicine, 2006) give the full case.

4 · The same mistake in a carbon table

“Dry” versus “wet”, or soil temperature split at \(10^\circ\mathrm{C}\).

  • \(9.9^\circ\mathrm{C}\) and \(10.1^\circ\mathrm{C}\) become different regimes
  • \(10.1^\circ\mathrm{C}\) and \(25^\circ\mathrm{C}\) become the same regime
  • a second cut, chosen because it was “significant”, will not travel

Keep warming, moisture, and pH continuous unless the threshold is itself the scientific object. SPRUCE kept the warming gradient rather than binning it.

Walk through the three bullets with a soil-temperature example. The third bullet is p-hacking by another name: a cut that worked once will not travel.

Going further. Choosing the cut point that minimises \(p\) is a search over many tests: Altman and colleagues (Journal of the National Cancer Institute, 1994) showed that the “optimal” cut inflates the Type I error from \(5\%\) to about \(40\%\) and overstates the effect. A corrected test exists (the maximally selected statistic of Lausen and Schumacher), but the effect estimate at the chosen cut remains biased. If a threshold is the scientific object, a segmented (broken-stick) regression estimates it with an interval, rather than assuming it.

5 · Allow a curve

5 · A straight line is an assumption

Restricted cubic splines and fractional polynomials are the usual parametric checks for \(f(x)\) (Riley et al. 2022).

A one-unit step in \(x\) need not have the same effect at the bottom and the top of the range.

You are not selling GAMs today; you are refusing a default line. Ask where a linear warming term would misread the SPRUCE gradient. The simulated hump is next.

Going further. A restricted cubic spline with \(K\) knots is cubic between knots and linear beyond the outer knots, and uses \(K-1\) parameters; Harrell (Regression Modeling Strategies) places the knots at fixed quantiles of \(x\) and finds 3 to 5 knots enough for most data. Fractional polynomials (Royston and Altman, Applied Statistics, 1994) choose one or two powers from \(\{-2, -1, -\tfrac{1}{2}, 0, \tfrac{1}{2}, 1, 2, 3\}\), with \(0\) meaning \(\log x\). Linearity can then be tested as the nested hypothesis that the non-linear coefficients are all zero, with a likelihood-ratio test on \(K-2\) degrees of freedom. Penalised splines, which choose the smoothness from the data, are the generalised additive model of the next section.

5 · A line can miss the hump

Illustrative simulation, seed 20261005. Not a SPRUCE fit. The clay line on the next slide is \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\) forced to be linear.

hump.R

mu <- 4 + 0.35 * temp - 0.08 * (temp - 4.5)^2
y <- rnorm(length(temp), mu, 0.35)
fit_line <- lm(y ~ temp)

Point at output-location: slide if your build uses it — the figure is the next slide. Emphasise illustrative, not SPRUCE.

5 · A line can miss the hump

Let the picture sit. The green curve is the generative \(\mu\); the clay line is what a reviewer might accept without looking. Thirty seconds.

6 · Quantify a subgroup difference

6 · A subgroup claim needs its own contrast

Common misread What to estimate
Significant in one subgroup, not in another Not proof the effects differ
Overlapping interval bars Not a test of interaction

\[Y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 T + \beta_2 S + \beta_3 (T \times S) + \epsilon, \quad \text{report } \hat\beta_3 \text{ with its interval}\]

“Significant in fens, not in bogs” is not an interaction. Estimate the difference of effects, with an interval (Riley et al. 2022). Overlapping interval bars are not a test.

Use fens and bogs from the slide text. Ask what estimand the interaction coefficient answers. If someone says “test both subgroups separately”, redirect to the product term.

Going further. Two subgroup tests compare each effect with zero; the interaction test compares the effects with each other, with \(\mathrm{SE}(\hat\beta_3) = \sqrt{\mathrm{SE}_1^2 + \mathrm{SE}_2^2}\) for independent subgroups. Gelman and Stern (The American Statistician, 2006) summarise it as “the difference between significant and not significant is not itself significant”. With equal subgroups the interaction has twice the standard error of the main effect, so detecting an interaction as large as the main effect needs about four times the sample size (Brookes and colleagues, Journal of Clinical Epidemiology, 2004). In a GLM the interaction is scale-dependent: no interaction on the log scale implies an interaction on the additive scale unless one of the effects is null.

7 · Read heterogeneity carefully

7 · Two spreads in every pooled table

Each site, or study, reports one estimate. Its scatter around the pooled mean has two sources:

  • the true effects differ between sites: their spread is \(\tau^2\)
  • each estimate is noisy: its sampling variance is \(s^2\), and it shrinks as the site collects more cores

\(I^2\) mixes the two. \(\tau^2\) does not.

This is the whole block in one slide. Keep the two words apart: heterogeneity is about the true effects, noise is about how well each site measured its own. In this block one row is a site, not a core. About one minute.

Going further. The working model is \(y_i = \theta_i + \varepsilon_i\) with \(\varepsilon_i \sim N(0, s_i^2)\) and \(\theta_i \sim N(\mu, \tau^2)\), where \(y_i\) is the reported site estimate. In a meta-analysis \(s_i^2\) is treated as known, although it is itself estimated; this is harmless with large within-site samples but biases small-study meta-analyses, especially log odds ratios with few events. Borenstein and colleagues’ Introduction to Meta-Analysis is the readable reference for the whole block.

7 · \(\tau^2\) is how far the true effects spread

\(\tau^2\) is an absolute spread, on the squared scale of the effect. It is not a percentage.

Arithmetic, not a carbon dataset: \(\tau^2 = 0.09\) on a log risk ratio gives \(\tau = 0.30\). A rough band for the site effects is the mean \(\pm 1.96\tau \approx \pm 0.59\). Call that large or small only after you name the scale.

Stress the units. If someone quotes \(I^2 = 25\%\) as “low heterogeneity”, ask for \(\tau^2\) in the effect’s units.

Going further. \(\tau^2\) is usually estimated by REML or Paule–Mandel; the DerSimonian–Laird moment estimator is the historical default. With few sites every estimator is imprecise, and the Hartung–Knapp–Sidik–Jonkman adjustment gives better coverage for the pooled mean. A proper prediction interval for a new site is \(\hat\mu \pm t_{k-2}\sqrt{\hat\tau^2 + \mathrm{SE}(\hat\mu)^2}\) (Higgins, Thompson and Spiegelhalter, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society A, 2009).

7 · \(I^2\) is a share, relative to the noise

\[ I^2 \approx \frac{\tau^2}{\tau^2 + s^2} \]

Keep \(\tau^2 = 0.04\):

  • noisy sites, \(s^2 = 0.16\): \(I^2 = 0.04/0.20 = 20\%\)
  • precise sites, \(s^2 = 0.0025\): \(I^2 = 0.04/0.0425 \approx 94\%\)

The true effects did not move. The measurements got sharper, so heterogeneity is a larger share of what you see.

Same \(\tau^2\), different \(I^2\): that sentence is worth repeating. The approximation holds when the sites have similar sampling variances.

Going further. In practice \(I^2\) comes from Cochran’s \(Q = \sum_i (y_i - \hat\mu_{\mathrm{FE}})^2 / s_i^2\) as \(I^2 = \max\{0, (Q - (k-1))/Q\}\); under homogeneity \(Q \sim \chi^2_{k-1}\). Higgins and Thompson (Statistics in Medicine, 2002) introduced \(I^2\); Rücker and colleagues (BMC Medical Research Methodology, 2008) show that it tends to 100% as study sizes grow, for any fixed \(\tau^2 > 0\). Its uncertainty interval is usually wide and should be reported.

7 · The soils did not move

The drawing is the arithmetic on the previous slide. Sharper cores raise \(I^2\) because the sampling bars shrink.

Point at the error bars, not at the cylinders. The 20 and 94 percent are the previous slide’s numbers.

7 · Report \(\tau^2\), and say what \(I^2\) is relative to

Quantity Question
\(\tau^2\) How far do the true site effects spread?
\(I^2\) How large is that spread beside the sampling noise?

Do not use 25%, 50%, and 75% as cut-offs. The same \(\tau^2\) gave both 20% and 94%.

Close the arithmetic on the table. A regression on site means is a separate trap: it is not the core-level slope (ecological bias).

Going further. Ecological, or aggregation, bias arises because a regression across site means estimates the between-site association, which mixes the within-site slope with between-site confounding (Greenland and Morgenstern, International Journal of Epidemiology, 1989).

7 · Perspective: fixed effect or random effects

Fixed effect. One true effect. Sites differ only by noise, so \(\tau^2 = 0\).

Random effects. Each site has its own true effect, drawn around \(\mu\) with spread \(\tau^2\).

The random-effects model is how \(\tau^2\) enters a pooled analysis. A site-level covariate can then explain part of \(\tau^2\). The same two-level structure returns in the design block: fixed and random name the target.

Grey is observed, blue is latent, a square is fixed, a dotted circle is computed from its parents, and the dashed plate repeats over the site index \(i\). Do not say the random-effects mean is automatically the causal effect. One minute on each column.

Going further. Random-effects weights are \(1/(s_i^2 + \hat\tau^2)\) instead of \(1/s_i^2\), so heterogeneity stops the most precise site from deciding the pooled mean alone: with \(s_i^2 = 0.01\) and \(\tau^2 = 0.09\) the weight falls from 100 to 10. Adding a moderator gives a mixed-effects meta-regression, \(\theta_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i + u_i\), with the residual \(\tau^2\) fitted by REML (the metafor package of Viechtbauer). Thompson and Higgins (Statistics in Medicine, 2002) warn that study-level moderators are observational even when every study is a randomised trial.

8 · Check a prediction in three ways

8 · A prediction needs three checks

Check Question Sketch on next slides
Discrimination Do high \(\hat\pi\) rank above low? AUC
Calibration In bins, does \(\bar Y \approx \hat\pi\)? Calibration plot
Utility Does the score beat treat-all / treat-none at some \(t\)? Net benefit

Discrimination, calibration, and usefulness at a decision threshold are different questions (Riley et al. 2022). A large AUC with a miscalibrated probability still misleads the decision.
