We will not fit a prediction model today. The three sketches that follow are drawings, not fitted scores. They belong to the prediction purpose, not to an explanation of \(\beta\).

Say explicitly that you will not teach ROC construction today. The table is the map; the drawings are teaching arithmetic. Two minutes for this slide, then one minute per dimension.

Going further. Steyerberg’s Clinical Prediction Models and the TRIPOD guidance organise validation in this order. Discrimination is invariant to any monotone transformation of \(\hat\pi\), so it cannot detect a miscalibrated model; calibration and net benefit are not. The Brier score \(n^{-1}\sum_i (Y_i - \hat\pi_i)^2\) is a proper scoring rule that mixes both, and decomposes into calibration (reliability) and refinement terms. All three must be estimated on data the model did not see, or corrected for optimism by the bootstrap (Harrell, Regression Modeling Strategies).

8 · Discrimination is the ranking

\[\mathrm{AUC} = \Pr(\hat\pi_{\mathrm{event}} > \hat\pi_{\mathrm{none}})\]

Teaching arithmetic, not a fitted model: 8 of 10 pairs ordered correctly is an AUC of 0.8. The diagonal is 0.5.

The 8-of-10 pairs line is the only number to remember. Red is chance ranking; green is usable separation. Do not conflate with calibration.

Going further. The AUC equals the Mann–Whitney statistic \(U/(n_1 n_0)\), the proportion of event–non-event pairs ranked correctly, with ties counted one half (Hanley and McNeil, Radiology, 1982). It is the area under the ROC curve of sensitivity against \(1 -\) specificity over all thresholds, so it averages over thresholds nobody would use. It depends on the case mix: a more heterogeneous population gives a higher AUC for the same model. For a survival outcome the analogue is Harrell’s \(C\) index.

8 · Calibration is the stated probability

In a bin of sites given the same \(\hat\pi\), the event should occur about that often:

\[\bar{Y}_{\mathrm{bin}} \approx \hat\pi_{\mathrm{bin}}\]

Teaching arithmetic: \(\hat\pi = 0.80\) on 10 sites, and the event on only 3 of them, is a miscalibrated bin. The ranks can still be right.

Give the 3-of-10 example verbally. Ask when a carbon team would care about calibration versus ranking (reporting stock change vs prioritising sites).

Going further. Van Calster and colleagues (Journal of Clinical Epidemiology, 2016) define a hierarchy: mean calibration (\(\bar Y = \bar{\hat\pi}\), the calibration-in-the-large), weak calibration (intercept 0 and slope 1 in the logistic recalibration model \(\mathrm{logit}\,\pi = a + b\,\mathrm{logit}\,\hat\pi\)), and moderate calibration (\(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid \hat\pi) = \hat\pi\), checked with a smoothed curve rather than bins). A calibration slope \(b < 1\) is the signature of overfitting: the predictions are too extreme on new data, which shrinkage (ridge, lasso, or a uniform shrinkage factor) corrects. Binned Hosmer–Lemeshow tests depend on the arbitrary number of bins and are not recommended.

8 · Calibration, drawn

Quick visual; the equation slide carries the definition. Red is the dangerous case: confident probabilities that are wrong.

8 · Utility is whether the score changes the action

At a threshold \(t\), net benefit charges each false action at the odds \(t/(1-t)\):

\[\mathrm{NB}(t) = \frac{\mathrm{TP}}{N} - \frac{\mathrm{FP}}{N}\,\frac{t}{1-t}\]

\(t\) need not be clinical. It is the chance of a peat source, or of a stock change, at which a wasted visit costs as much as a missed one. A useful score beats both “act on every site” and “act on none” on a band of \(t\).

Net benefit is the hardest of the three for this audience; lean on the peat-source example. You do not need to derive the formula — point at TP and FP.

Going further. Decision curve analysis is due to Vickers and Elkin (Medical Decision Making, 2006). The weight \(t/(1-t)\) follows from expected-utility theory: acting at threshold \(t\) is optimal when the harm of a false positive relative to the benefit of a true positive equals \(t/(1-t)\), so the threshold encodes the trade-off and the formula needs no separate utilities. “Treat all” has net benefit \(\bar{Y} - (1-\bar{Y})\,t/(1-t)\) and “treat none” has 0. Net benefit uses calibrated probabilities directly, so a miscalibrated model can have a good AUC and a negative net benefit.

8 · Utility, drawn

Green is “worth using”; red is “worse than ignoring the score”. Threshold \(t\) is a policy choice, not a property of the model.

9 · Respect clusters and repeats

9 · Repeated rows are not replicates

\[Y_{ij} = \beta_0 + u_j + \beta^\top \mathbf{x}_{ij} + \epsilon_{ij}, \qquad u_j \sim N(0,\sigma_u^2), \qquad \rho = \frac{\sigma_u^2}{\sigma_u^2 + \sigma^2}\]

Ignoring the cluster pretends that rows in the same enclosure are as different as rows in different enclosures (Riley et al. 2022). The model is a random intercept. 📝 Godechot’s panel notes use the same nesting for repeated observations of one unit (the slides).

Index \(j\) is the unit that received the treatment; \(i\) is the repeated measure or sub-sample. Ask them to name both indices for their dataset before you show the row count slide.

Going further. In the random-intercept model the intraclass correlation \(\rho\) is the correlation between two observations in the same cluster. For a cluster-level treatment with \(m\) observations per cluster, the variance of the treatment effect is inflated by the design effect \(1 + (m-1)\rho\) (Kish, Survey Sampling), so the effective sample size is \(nm/\{1 + (m-1)\rho\}\): even \(\rho = 0.1\) with \(m = 20\) nearly triples the variance. Ignoring the cluster gives standard errors that are too small for cluster-level covariates. The variance components are estimated by REML, which corrects the downward bias of maximum likelihood for \(\sigma_u^2\) by accounting for the fixed effects (Patterson and Thompson, Biometrika, 1971).

9 · Count the unit before you count the rows

Teaching arithmetic, not a published study:

  • 4 sites, the level that received the treatment
  • 3 plots in each site
  • 20 cores in each plot
  • 3 technical readings on each core

\(4 \times 3 \times 20 \times 3 = 720\) rows. If the treatment was applied to the site, you do not have 720 independent replicates.

Multiply together on the board. The gasp at 720 versus 4 is the learning moment. Technical replicates are not biological replicates.

Going further. Hurlbert (Ecological Monographs, 1984) named this pseudoreplication: testing a treatment effect with an error term from the wrong level of the design. The correct denominator for a site-level treatment is the between-site variation, so with 4 sites the treatment comparison has at most 2 residual degrees of freedom in a two-arm design, whatever the number of cores. Averaging within site and analysing the 4 means gives the same test as a balanced nested mixed model; the mixed model is needed when the design is unbalanced or when lower-level covariates matter. The design-stage remedy is more sites, not more cores, as the design section will show.

10 · Select covariates from a causal sketch

10 · Covariates are a scientific choice

Role Adjustment
Confounder Usually adjust (blocks back-door path)
Collider Do not adjust (opens bias)
Mediator Adjust only if the estimand is direct, not total
Any covariate Avoid univariable screening on \(p\)-values

For an explanatory model, screening on \(p\)-values is the wrong gate. Draw the paths first (Riley et al. 2022). Lasso and ridge answer a prediction problem; they are not a causal adjustment set.

  • Ridge penalises the squares of the coefficients (\(L_2\)).
  • Lasso penalises the absolute values (\(L_1\)).
  • Elastic net mixes the two.

The table is not exhaustive; the next slide is the picture. If someone asks “stepwise AIC?”, answer: same problem — the gate is the DAG and the estimand, not the lowest AIC.

Going further. Pearl’s back-door criterion (Causality, 2009) gives a sufficient adjustment set: no member is a descendant of the exposure, and the set blocks every path from exposure to \(Y\) that enters the exposure through an arrowhead. Selection by \(p\)-value or stepwise AIC ignores these roles, biases the retained coefficients away from zero and makes their nominal intervals too narrow (Harrell, Regression Modeling Strategies, ch. 4). The lasso minimises \(\sum_i (Y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)^2 + \lambda \sum_k |\beta_k|\) and selects variables for prediction; when covariates are correlated it keeps one of them arbitrarily, so a dropped confounder is not evidence of no confounding. Double/debiased machine learning (Chernozhukov and colleagues, Econometrics Journal, 2018) is the principled way to use the lasso inside a causal estimate. Westreich and Greenland’s “Table 2 fallacy” (American Journal of Epidemiology, 2013) warns that the coefficients of the adjustment covariates are not themselves causal effects.

11 · Sensitivity is part of the result

11 · Show that the conclusion can move

A constant hazard ratio, a “vague” prior, or a complete-case fit can each carry the result. Refit under a second defensible assumption and say what moved (Riley et al. 2022). For an observational claim, sensitivity is the report.

Sensitivity is not “try everything until it sticks”. Name one alternative assumption per panel and report direction and size of change. Observational work gets the causal link at the end of the sentence.

Going further. For missing data, a delta-adjusted pattern-mixture analysis shifts the imputed values of the missing \(Y\) by \(\delta\) and reports the tipping point \(\delta\) at which the conclusion changes; \(\delta = 0\) is MAR. For unmeasured confounding, the E-value of VanderWeele and Ding (Annals of Internal Medicine, 2017), \(\mathrm{RR} + \sqrt{\mathrm{RR}(\mathrm{RR}-1)}\), is the minimum strength of association, on the risk-ratio scale, that a confounder would need with both exposure and outcome to explain the estimate away. For a prior, the analysis is repeated under a sceptical and an enthusiastic prior. A sensitivity analysis should vary an assumption the data cannot check; refitting under assumptions that the data already reject is model checking, not sensitivity.

12 · Report so a reader can redo it

12 · Write so the analysis can be rerun

Guidelines (examples) Essentials to report
CONSORT, STROBE, PRISMA, TRIPOD Design, participants, outcomes
SAMPL for complex data Methods, missing data, software version
Prefer Avoid
Median (IQR) when \(Y\) is skewed “Significant” as the whole result
Multivariable (several \(x\)) Multivariate (several \(Y\))
Association when \(x\) was not assigned Causal language without design

SAMPL, on the EQUATOR Network, is the checklist we will use at the end Lang and Altman, 2015. The four lines for this room are SAMPL, in workshop language.

SAMPL is the one they will see again in the reproducibility block. Pick one “avoid” row and ask for a rewrite from the room. One minute.

Essentials of statistical literacy

StatHarbor Analytics, 17 May 2026. Click a panel. The words we police are on the opening cards.

Optional if you are behind. Click one panel rather than reading all seven. The vocabulary card is now in the opening, after expectation.

Essentials of statistical literacy

Panel 9 and panel 12 are the two this hour has already argued. Do not re-teach them. Point and move to the close.

Where we go deeper

Of these twelve, the next hour stays with a problem the poster assumes you have already solved: the distribution of \(Y\).

Soil counts, fluxes, and compositions are often skewed, bounded, discrete, or full of zeros. A Gaussian linear model is then the wrong likelihood, even when the question, the estimand, and the design are clear. Next: the shape of \(Y\).

Close the pitfalls hour on purpose: clear question does not imply Gaussian \(Y\). Thirty seconds, then advance to section 3.

When Y is not Gaussian

🔬 About thirty minutes. QQ plots, six reasons not to transform \(Y\), then four guidelines.

The pipeline rewrites the column first

Hartmann and Rudolph, From EGU 2026 short course SC2.28.

Trace the pipeline left to right and linger on the circled evaluation box. Ask what happens if a log or library-size normalisation is applied before the model is chosen—the metric still looks fine while the estimand changed. This slide sets the stake for everything that follows about transforms versus links.

A Gaussian is not the default

Numerous environmental phenomena do not follow the bell-shaped structure of the Gaussian

Environmental \(Y\) is often:

  • skewed, and strictly positive
  • a count, or a composition
  • full of zeros, or censored at a detection limit
  • dependent in time or in space

A Gaussian lm can still be a useful approximation for a mean. It is a poor default when the support of \(Y\) makes the approximation impossible. This hour follows where we go deeper.

Read the bullet list as a checklist against CA-SYS and SPRUCE: counts, zeros, composition, dependence. The closing sentence is deliberately soft—a Gaussian mean can be fine—before the support argument bites. Link forward to Quetilismus as the cultural habit behind ignoring skew.

Quetilismus

🔗 Adrian Olszewski names the habit after Edgeworth’s word for Quetelet. A unimodal curve is treated as a normal curve. Skew is then a defect to repair: drop the tails, wait for a larger sample, or transform until the histogram looks symmetric.

Name Quetilismus once, then list the three repairs: trim tails, wait for \(n\), transform until symmetric. Students often recognise their own lab practice in the third.

Identify conditional non-Gaussian distributions

How to read a QQ-plot 🔍

Points along the line support the reference shape. A curve, or a tail that leaves the line, is a departure. Illustrative residuals, seed 20261005.

qq-residuals.R

qq_gaussian <- bind_rows(
  tibble(model = "Gaussian lm, Gaussian Y", residual = resid(fit_lm_gauss)),
  tibble(model = "Gaussian lm, Poisson Y", residual = resid(fit_lm_count))
)
ggplot(qq_gaussian, aes(sample = residual)) +
  stat_qq_line() +
  stat_qq() +
  facet_wrap(~model)

This slide is code only; the figure is next. Point at facet_wrap(~model) and the two residual columns—Gaussian \(Y\) versus Poisson \(Y\) through the same lm. Ask which panel they expect to bend before you advance. Seed 20261005 is illustrative, not field data.

How to read a QQ-plot 🔍

Pair with the previous code slide: left panel is the sanity check, right panel is the misspecified count. Finger the right-tail lift as variance growing with the mean, not “non-normality” in the abstract. The preceding slide showed how those panels were produced.

Four shapes on the QQ plot

What is wrong?

Nothing. Points on the line, inside the band.

What is wrong?

Heavy tails: an S-curve, both ends flare away.

What is wrong?

Right skew: a concave-up bend. Left skew bends down.

What is wrong?

Light tails: a flat S, both ends pulled in.

Eight steps: show a panel, ask the room what is wrong, wait, then reveal the answer. Panel A is the Gaussian control: nothing to notice, and the ends are allowed to wander inside the band. Students confuse heavy tails (B) with right skew (C): heavy tails flare at both ends, skew bends one way. D is the mirror image of B. Panels B to D are idealised: each distribution’s own quantiles plus a little noise (\(t_{1.6}\), exponential, uniform), \(n = 60\), seed 20261005, drawn by scripts/generate-slide-figures.R after the dark four-panel poster in figures/sources/qq-four-shapes.png. The band is a pointwise 95% band, not the tail-sensitive band of a later slide.

Axes first: theoretical quantile horizontal, sample quantile vertical. Distinguish tail scatter (allowed) from a systematic bend (signal).

Going further. For the sample \(p\)-quantile, \(\operatorname{Var}\{\hat{F}^{-1}(p)\} \approx p(1 - p) / [n\, f\{F^{-1}(p)\}^2]\), where \(f\) is the density. In the tails \(f\) is small, so the extreme order statistics vary a lot even under the reference model: that is why the ends wander. For uniform order statistics the exact law is \(U_{(i)} \sim \mathrm{Beta}(i, n + 1 - i)\), which gives the pointwise band drawn here and the exact simultaneous bands. David and Nagaraja, Order Statistics (2003), give the asymptotics.

A normality test is a poor gate

  • with a large \(n\), the test rejects a departure too small to change \(\hat{\mu}\)
  • with a small \(n\), it misses a departure large enough to matter
  • the \(p\)-value does not say whether the estimand is distorted
  • the test is aimed at the wrong object if you apply it to raw \(Y\) rather than to the model you will trust

Prefer the QQ plot, and then a likelihood that matches \(Y\). A formal test is a poor substitute for that plot.

Step through the four bullets; the third is the estimand point students miss. Large \(n\) makes the fourth bullet bite—testing raw \(Y\) when the model is conditional. Close with the QQ-plus-likelihood recipe on the slide.

Going further. A consistent goodness-of-fit test has power tending to 1 against any fixed alternative, so with enough data it rejects departures that change nothing about \(\hat{\mu}\). Meanwhile the \(t\) statistic is asymptotically valid without Gaussian errors, with an error in the null distribution of order \(n^{-1/2}\) controlled by the skewness (the Berry–Esseen bound). Choosing the second test on the result of a first one is a conditional procedure whose overall type I error is no longer the nominal level; Rochon, Gondan, and Kieser (BMC Medical Research Methodology, 2012) simulate this for the two-sample case and recommend against the pretest.

Two sample sizes, two failures

Adrian Olszewski’s rule, which this hour follows: stop using them, except when a reviewer demands the number. He counts more than 90 procedures.

  • A small sample leaves them underpowered, so a real departure often passes.
  • Past about 150–200 observations they reject almost any departure, including one too small to matter, while the central limit theorem for a mean is improving.
  • Families aimed at skew, at kurtosis, and at the whole distribution contradict one another. Shapiro–Wilk is one of those families, not a universal test.

Give Olszewski’s “stop using them” line, then the 150–200 observation remark and the small-sample underpowering bullet. “More than 90 procedures” is rhetorical scale—do not treat it as a number to defend. Shapiro–Wilk is one family among many contradictory ones.

The same sample, opposite answers

\(n = 400\), skewness about 0, kurtosis about 3. Shapiro–Wilk gives \(p = 0.123\). Lilliefors, Cramér–von Mises, and Anderson–Darling reject. The QQ plot shows the step either way.

Restate \(n=400\) and the step in the QQ plot before reading any \(p\)-values. Shapiro–Wilk 0.123 versus rejecting Lilliefors/AD is the lesson—tests disagree while the plot stays honest. Ask which diagnostic they would trust for choosing a family.

Six reasons not to transform \(Y\)

Before you transform \(Y\)

Write two answers, and keep them.

  1. Why is transforming the response usually a bad default?
  2. What do you do instead when a Gaussian model for \(Y\) does not fit?

Olszewski’s answers, from the 2022 note, are the six caveats that follow. A multiplicative process is the case he keeps. A skewed histogram is not. 📄 The note.

Pause on the two numbered questions—let someone answer before you advance. Olszewski’s “bad default” is about rewriting the estimand without science; the alternative is a model matched to \(Y\). Skew alone is not on his justification list.

Caveat 1 · A transform changes the hypothesis

A logarithm changes a difference of arithmetic means into a ratio of geometric means. A Box–Cox power such as \(-0.67\) is not a unit anyone measured.

On \(\log Y\) against \(x\), a small coefficient is nearly a percentage: \(\exp(0.05) \approx 1.05\). A coefficient of \(0.5\) is not a 50% change, because \(\exp(0.5) \approx 1.65\). The inequality that separates the two means is written later.

Box–Cox power \(-0.67\) is the absurd unit example. Contrast \(\exp(0.05)\approx 1.05\) with \(\exp(0.5)\approx 1.65\) so students stop saying “0.5 on the log is 50%”. Forward to the log-estimand inequality slide.

Going further. On \(\log Y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \varepsilon\), a one-unit change in \(x\) multiplies the geometric mean, and the median when \(\varepsilon\) is symmetric, by \(e^{\beta_1}\), a change of \(100(e^{\beta_1} - 1)\%\). Box and Cox (Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B, 1964) choose \(\lambda\) in \((Y^{\lambda} - 1)/\lambda\) by profile likelihood. Inference on \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) treating \(\hat{\lambda}\) as known understates the uncertainty, and \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) has no fixed meaning across \(\lambda\); Bickel and Doksum (1981) and the reply by Box and Cox (1982) are the classic exchange on that point.

Caveat 2 · The interval does not travel back

For a convex \(f\),

\[ f\!\left(\mathbb{E}[Y]\right) \le \mathbb{E}[f(Y)]. \tag{1}\]

The two sides meet when \(f\) is linear. A confidence interval, a moment, or a test computed on \(f(Y)\) is not the same object on the original scale after \(f^{-1}\). The log case is the estimand slide. Equation 1 is why. 🔗 eulerism_, carousel PDF (Jensen, slides 8–14).

Read Equation 1 once. The next slide draws the gap. A common misread is to invert a CI on \(f(Y)\) and call it a CI for \(\mathbb{E}(Y)\).

Going further. Jensen’s inequality holds for any convex \(f\) and integrable \(Y\), with equality if and only if \(f\) is affine on the support of \(Y\) or \(Y\) is degenerate. A second-order expansion gives the size of the gap: \(\mathbb{E}\{f(Y)\} \approx f(\mu) + \tfrac12 f''(\mu)\operatorname{Var}(Y)\). What does travel back is a quantile: for a monotone \(f\), \(f^{-1}\) of a quantile of \(f(Y)\) is the same quantile of \(Y\). So an interval for the mean of \(\log Y\), back-transformed, is an interval for the median of \(Y\) when \(\log Y\) is symmetric, not for its mean.

Caveat 2 · Convex pulls the mean up

Convex, such as \(y^2\).

The green point is \(f(\mathbb{E}[Y])\). The red point is \(\mathbb{E}[f(Y)]\). The arrow is the gap in Equation 1.

Concave, such as \(\log y\).

The inequality reverses. Back-transforming a mean on the log scale lands off \(\mathbb{E}(Y)\).

Point at the arrow, not the curve. Convex: the average of \(f(Y)\) sits above \(f\) of the average. Concave, including the log: the gap runs the other way, which is the back-transformed mean.

Caveat 2 · \(\log Y\) changes the estimand

A Gaussian model for \(\log Y\) targets \(\mathbb{E}(\log Y \mid x)\).

\[ \exp\{\mathbb{E}(\log Y \mid x)\} \le \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x), \]

with equality only in degenerate cases. The left-hand side behaves like a geometric mean. The right-hand side is the arithmetic mean on the original scale.

\(\log(Y+1)\) is a third response. Zeros were the reason for the \(+1\), and they are still in the model, only hidden.

For counts, start from a distribution on the non-negative integers (O’Hara and Kotze, 2010; (Agresti 2015)).

Read the inequality: \(\exp\{\mathbb{E}(\log Y\mid x)\}\le \mathbb{E}(Y\mid x)\). \(\log(Y+1)\) hides zeros but changes the response again. Close with counts needing an integer model—O’Hara and Kotze on the slide.

Going further. Under \(\log Y = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon\) with \(\varepsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\) independent of \(\mathbf{x}\), \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x}) = \exp(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \sigma^2/2)\). Ratios of arithmetic means are then also \(e^{\beta_1}\), so the slope survives; only the level is biased. That rescue fails as soon as \(\sigma^2\) depends on \(\mathbf{x}\), which is the usual situation for counts. O’Hara and Kotze (2010) show by simulation that \(\log(Y + 1)\) followed by least squares gives biased estimates for counts with low means, where a Poisson or negative binomial GLM does not.

Caveat 3 · The variance travels with the mean

In a Gaussian model the mean and the variance are separate parameters. After a log they are tied: a log-normal variance depends on the mean. A power chosen only to stabilise the spread changes that relationship even when the power is not a logarithm.

Gaussian \(\mu\) and \(\sigma^2\) separate; log-normal ties them. Any power transform for stabilisation also couples mean and spread—even when it is not \(\log\). One sentence slide: do not rush.

Going further. If \(\log Y \sim \mathcal{N}(m, s^2)\), then \(\mathbb{E}(Y) = \mu = e^{m + s^2/2}\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = (e^{s^2} - 1)\mu^2\). A constant variance on the log scale is therefore a constant coefficient of variation, \(v(\mu) \propto \mu^2\), on the original scale. The same variance function belongs to the gamma family, so a gamma GLM with a log link targets \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x})\) directly with that mean–variance relation (McCullagh and Nelder, Generalized Linear Models, 1989, chapter 8).

Caveat 4 · Skew is the process: a product is not a sum

The classical central limit theorem is a statement about a sum, and it needs a finite variance. A product of positive steps is a different limit. Cascades, growth, and decay land on a log-normal, which is skewed. Cauchy, Lévy, and Pareto sit outside that classical sum.

Species abundance and environmental concentrations are on the product side (Limpert, Stahel, and Abbt, 2001; Andersson, 2021). More rows do not turn a product into a sum. 📄 Olszewski’s longer note is On the ubiquity of skewness in nature (2023).

Contrast sum versus product in one sentence each; Cauchy and Pareto are the “finite variance fails” examples. The carbon examples at the bottom are the punchline—bigger \(n\) does not Gaussianise a multiplicative process. Ask whether read counts feel more like sums or products.

Going further. The Lindeberg–Lévy theorem says that \(\sqrt{n}(\bar{Y} - \mu)/\sigma\) converges to \(\mathcal{N}(0, 1)\) for independent, identically distributed terms with finite variance \(\sigma^2\). Taking logs turns a product \(\prod_k U_k\) of positive, independent factors into a sum \(\sum_k \log U_k\), so the same theorem gives a log-normal limit whenever \(\operatorname{Var}(\log U_k)\) is finite. Without a finite variance the generalised central limit theorem replaces the Gaussian by a stable law (Cauchy and Lévy are members), with a slower, non-\(\sqrt{n}\) scaling. Gnedenko and Kolmogorov, Limit Distributions for Sums of Independent Random Variables (1954), is the classical source; Limpert, Stahel, and Abbt (2001) give the applied log-normal case.

Caveat 4 · The conditionals were already fine

A marginal histogram can look skewed because several Gaussian groups, with different means, were pooled.

  • Each conditional \(Y \mid x\) can already be Gaussian
  • Transforming the pooled column rewrites those conditionals

Ask them to imagine two normal groups with different means, stacked into one histogram. The skew is the mixture. Transforming that mixture is not a repair of either group.

Going further. The marginal density is a mixture, \(f(y) = \sum_k w_k\, \varphi\{(y - \mu_k)/\sigma\}/\sigma\), and by the law of total variance \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}\{\operatorname{Var}(Y \mid x)\} + \operatorname{Var}\{\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\}\). The marginal skewness comes entirely from the spread of the conditional means and the weights. A transformation chosen on \(f(y)\) therefore reacts to the design, not to the error distribution the model assumes.

Caveat 5 · A logarithm needs a positive response

\(\log Y\) is undefined when \(Y\) can be negative. Adding a constant, as in \(\log(Y + c)\), defines a different response. A bone-density T-score is one measurement that is allowed to be negative. Quantile regression does not need the sign. Yeo–Johnson is the power that allows negatives, and the later slide says what that power costs.

\(\log(Y+c)\) is a new response, not a fix for negatives. T-score is the named example of legitimate negative \(Y\). Quantile regression and Yeo–Johnson are the two exits named on the slide—different estimands, different costs.

Going further. Box–Cox is defined only for \(Y > 0\); the two-parameter version \((Y + c)^{\lambda}\) makes \(c\) a parameter whose value changes the estimand. The inverse hyperbolic sine, \(\operatorname{asinh}(Y) = \log(Y + \sqrt{Y^2 + 1})\), accepts zeros and negatives but its coefficients depend on the units of \(Y\) (Bellemare and Wichman, Oxford Bulletin of Economics and Statistics, 2020). For non-negative \(Y\) with exact zeros, a Tweedie or a hurdle model keeps the mean on the original scale.

Caveat 5 · A constant sum is a simplex

If the parts add to a constant, the point is not free in the plane. The picture: \(x + y = 1\) is a line segment, not a square. A sequencing library is the same constraint. The counts in one sample add to the library size, so a gene is a share of that sum. A Gaussian interval on the count, or on a shifted logarithm of the count, does not respect it.

Use \(x+y=1\) as the line-segment picture before sequencing. Library size fixes the sum of counts; a gene share lives on a simplex. Gaussian intervals on raw counts ignore that constraint—preview compositional methods without opening the full chapter.

Going further. Closure, dividing each part by the total, induces negative bias in the correlations between parts: for \(D\) parts with constant sum, the covariances in each row of the covariance matrix sum to zero. Pearson called this spurious correlation in 1897. Aitchison’s log-ratio geometry makes the simplex a Euclidean space, in which distances and means are invariant to the arbitrary total. For sequencing counts, Gloor and colleagues (Frontiers in Microbiology, 2017) argue that this treatment is not optional, because the library size carries no biological information.

Caveat 5 · Mean ± 2σ can leave the scale

Hartmann and Rudolph. The 2-sigma interval is 92 ± 24%, so it leaves 0–100. The bell beside it is the 68%, 95%, 99.7% picture. A normality test does not turn that interval into a recovery rate. The same objection applies to a concentration, a count, or a composition.

Point at the histogram piled at 100% and the 92 ± 24% band leaving the 0–100 scale. The bell’s 68/95/99.7 labels are the wrong feature for bounded recovery. A normality test on such data does not repair the interval—shape and support are separate failures.

Caveat 6 · A power that chases a bell shape names no unit

Yeo–Johnson chooses a power, including for zeros and negative values, so that the transformed sample is closer to Gaussian. When the next method is Euclidean, such as a PCA, the transform that follows \(v(\mu)\) is the variance-stabilising transformation.

  • the criterion is closeness to a bell shape, not a scientific unit
  • the inverse transform is awkward, so a mean on the transformed scale is hard to say in the original unit
  • if you use it, describe the original \(Y\) with the median and the quartiles

It does not replace a likelihood whose support is the support of \(Y\).

Yeo–Johnson optimises toward Gaussianity for PCA-like steps, not toward a scientific unit. If used, report median and quartiles on original \(Y\). VST from \(v(\mu)\) is the principled alternative named in the first bullet’s cross-link.

Going further. Yeo and Johnson (Biometrika, 2000) define \(\psi(y, \lambda) = \{(y + 1)^{\lambda} - 1\}/\lambda\) for \(y \ge 0\) and \(-\{(1 - y)^{2 - \lambda} - 1\}/(2 - \lambda)\) for \(y < 0\), with logarithmic limits at \(\lambda = 0\) and \(\lambda = 2\), and choose \(\lambda\) by maximising a Gaussian likelihood. The criterion is symmetry of the marginal sample, so the chosen \(\lambda\) changes with the mix of covariate values in the data. The transform is continuous and monotone, so quantiles back-transform exactly, which is why the median and quartiles remain interpretable while a mean does not.

The exception · a transform that matches the science

A transform is justified when it matches the science:

  • a multiplicative effect, so that logs are the natural contrast
  • a variance that grows with the mean, and a log that stabilises it
  • a physical rate that is already defined on a log scale

It is not justified by “the histogram looked skewed” alone: that shortcut is what caveats 1 to 6 cost.

Walk the three justified bullets: multiplicative effects, variance growing with mean, physical log scale. The closing sentence is the guardrail—histogram skew is not on the list. Link to the transform-rewrite slide for the cost of ignoring that guardrail.

Match the transform to \(v(\mu)\)

Huber, chapter 13, writes \(v(\mu)\) for the way the variance trends with the mean. Three prototypes cover most assays:

  • constant: \(v(\mu) = c\)
  • Poisson-like: \(v(\mu) = a\mu\)
  • quadratic: the standard deviation proportional to the mean, so \(v(\mu) = b\mu^2\)

A real measurement can add them. For microarray fluorescence he cites Rocke and Durbin (2001): background plus a multiplicative noise, \(v(\mu) = b\mu^2 + c\). A variance-stabilising transformation is built from that curve, so replicate spread no longer tracks the mean. One power for the whole sample, as on the Yeo–Johnson slide, does not use \(v(\mu)\).

Huber’s three prototypes—constant, Poisson-like \(a\mu\), quadratic \(b\mu^2\)—are the vocabulary. Rocke–Durbin adds \(b\mu^2+c\) as a realistic assay. Contrast VST built from \(v(\mu)\) with Yeo–Johnson chasing a bell shape for the whole column.

Going further. The delta method gives \(\operatorname{Var}\{f(Y)\} \approx f'(\mu)^2 v(\mu)\), so the stabilising choice solves \(f'(\mu) \propto v(\mu)^{-1/2}\), that is \(f(\mu) = \int^{\mu} v(t)^{-1/2}\, \mathrm{d}t\). Constant \(v\) gives the identity, \(v = a\mu\) gives \(\sqrt{\mu}\) (Anscombe’s \(\sqrt{Y + 3/8}\) refines it for Poisson counts), \(v = b\mu^2\) gives \(\log \mu\), and \(v = b\mu^2 + c\) gives an inverse hyperbolic sine, the generalised log of Huber and colleagues (Bioinformatics, 2002). The approximation is first order: it is poor for small counts, where the variance of \(f(Y)\) still depends on \(\mu\).

A VST is for a Euclidean next step

Reasons for analysing the transformed scale:

  • a plot, or a heatmap, that spends its ink across the range

  • PCA, or a clustering that uses correlation, no longer dominated by a few very large means

  • a method that assumes identical, homoskedastic noise

That is the machine-learning case: a method whose geometry is Euclidean and wants a roughly constant spread. The transformation is still a new response. Inference for \(\mu\) on the original support stays with the link.

Step the three fragments: heatmaps, PCA dominated by large means, homoskedastic noise for ML. VST is a preprocessing choice for geometry, not automatic inference on the original scale. The link at the end points to GLMs as the inference path.

Going further. DESeq2’s vst() integrates the fitted dispersion trend \(v(\mu) = \mu + \alpha(\mu)\mu^2\) of the negative binomial, and rlog() shrinks each gene’s log fold changes towards zero with an empirical prior (Love, Huber, and Anders, Genome Biology, 2014). Both are meant for distances, clustering, and plots; the differential test itself is done on the counts with the negative binomial likelihood. A Euclidean distance on the transformed scale is then close to a distance that gives each gene a comparable noise level.

Guideline 1 · Summarise beyond the arithmetic mean

Three means, three questions

Sharmi Murali: a sum, a product, or a reciprocal. \(A \ge G \ge H\) for positive numbers, with equality only when every value is the same. A product process wants \(G\), and logging \(Y\) changes the estimand to that mean.

Walk the three columns in the figure: sum, product, reciprocal. \(A \ge G \ge H\) is the inequality to remember. Link logging \(Y\) to the geometric mean and forward to the log-estimand slide—students often report \(A\) after a log transform.

Going further. \(A \ge G\) is Jensen’s inequality for the concave \(\log\): \(\overline{\log x} \le \log \bar{x}\). For a log-normal \(Y\) with \(\log Y \sim \mathcal{N}(m, s^2)\), the geometric mean is \(e^{m}\) (the median) and the arithmetic mean is \(e^{m + s^2/2}\), so the gap grows with the log-scale spread. \(G \ge H\) follows by applying \(A \ge G\) to the reciprocals.

The centre has to match the scale

Scale Measure Example Similarity Centre Spread
Binomial Success or failure Rainy days Equality only \(np\) \(np(1-p)\)
Nominal Category membership Soil types Equality \(\text{mode, }\bar{x}_{\mathrm{geo}},\, np_i\) Entropy
Ordinal \(\text{ordered by } <\) Hurricane category Rank order \(Q_{50}\) \(Q_{75}-Q_{25}\)
Interval \(d=|x_1-x_2|\) Time, location Additive, near 0 Arithmetic mean Arithmetic sd
Rational \(x_1/x_2\) Length, weight, isotope ratio Multiplicative, near 1 Geometric mean Geometric sd
Logistic \((x-l)/(u-x)\) Elevation, depth Log-ratio Logistic mean Logistic variance
Concentration Part of a constant sum TOC, recovery rate Aitchison distance Geometric mean Compositional variance

Hartmann and Rudolph. An arithmetic mean is the interval row. A ratio wants a geometric mean. TOC and a recovery are parts of a sum, not interval measurements.

Walk the gt table row by row: Interval → arithmetic mean; Rational → geometric; Concentration → geometric on parts of a sum. Hartmann and Rudolph’s TOC and recovery examples are compositional, not interval. Ask which centre matches an isotope ratio versus a recovery rate.

Guideline 2 · Show the distribution, not only a box

A box plot hides the shape

A box plot reports a median, a box, and whiskers. It does not show a second mode, a pile of ties, or how many points sit in the tail. The case against using it alone is that those are often the features a carbon response actually has.

Prefer a plot that keeps the raw points. A raincloud adds a density and a short summary beside them (Scherer, 6 June 2021).

List what a box plot hides: bimodality, ties, tail mass. Carbon responses often have those features. Raincloud is the constructive alternative named on the slide—density plus points plus a small summary.

Show the difference, not a star

Ho and colleagues. Panel e is the graphic this workshop keeps: \(\Delta\) and an interval. 📄 The paper.

Scan panels a–c as what not to stop at, then land on panel e: mean difference \(\Delta\) with an interval on a difference axis. Contrast with a lone star or \(p\)-value. This is the reporting standard the pitfalls section keeps returning to.

Guideline 3 · Keep the question, change the test

Four questions, not one test

Olszewski groups comparisons of two or more samples by the question, from the most general to the most specific. About nine hundred named procedures sit in these groups.

Read the four cards left to right as increasing specificity. “Nine hundred” is Olszewski’s scale—use it to deflate test shopping. Ask which card a Mann–Whitney user actually picked (often not the one they think).

3.i · Are the two distributions identical?

First of the four cards, now large. Read the null, \(F_1 = F_2\), and the third bullet: no focus on location or shape. The next slide says which of the three tests to use.

3.i · Whole distributions, then the tails

A test of the empirical distribution functions asks only whether \(F = G\). It does not say whether the difference is a shift, a scale, or a shape.

Olszewski’s family: Kolmogorov–Smirnov uses the largest vertical gap, and it is the least sensitive of the three. Cramér–von Mises integrates the squared gap, so a small gap that persists can count. Anderson–Darling uses the same integral with extra weight in the tails. For a heavy-tailed flux, Anderson–Darling is the one that looks where the mass of the question sits.

\(F=G\) is the whole-distribution null—no location story yet. Compare KS (max gap), CvM (integrated gap), AD (tail-weighted). For heavy-tailed flux, name Anderson–Darling as the tail-aware member of the trio on the slide.

Going further. With empirical distribution functions \(F_n\) and \(G_m\) and the pooled \(H_N\), Kolmogorov–Smirnov uses \(\sup_t |F_n(t) - G_m(t)|\), Cramér–von Mises uses \(\int (F_n - G_m)^2\, \mathrm{d}H_N\), and Anderson–Darling divides that integrand by \(H_N(1 - H_N)\), which inflates the weight where \(H_N\) is near 0 or 1. All three are distribution-free under \(F = G\) for continuous data, so the null distribution does not depend on the shape; ties break that property and need a permutation null. Scholz and Stephens (Journal of the American Statistical Association, 1987) give the \(k\)-sample Anderson–Darling test.

3.ii · Is one sample generally larger?

Second card. The last bullet is the one to say aloud: a rank test does not compare medians in general. The pairs picture has equal medians and still a clear superiority.

3.ii · A rank test is not a mean test

Mann–Whitney estimates \(P(B>A)+\tfrac12 P(B=A)\). Equal medians can be decisive. A gap of 10 can be invisible. For a median, use quantile regression.

Left panel: equal medians, \(p<0.001\), ~70% stochastic superiority. Right panel: medians differ by 10, \(p>0.999\), 50% superiority. Read those numbers off the figure before defining the estimand. Mann–Whitney is not a median test—quantile regression is named for a median question.

3.ii · Ranks answer a different question

Unless the two distributions differ only by a shift, a rank test mixes location, scale, and shape. Mann–Whitney can be small when the means are equal and the variances are not, and large when the means differ and the variances differ the other way. That is Studentization: the null of a rank test is not the null of a mean.

If the planned question is a mean, stay with a procedure for a mean: a permutation Welch test, Freedman–Lane, or a wild bootstrap. Brunner–Munzel is the rank procedure that allows unequal variances, and it still estimates a probability, not \(\mu\). The map of those choices is the six columns.

Studentization is the keyword: rank null mixes location, scale, and shape. If the estimand is \(\mu\), list permutation Welch, Freedman–Lane, wild bootstrap from the slide. Brunner–Munzel still targets a probability. Point to the six-column map link as the decision chart.

Going further. \(U/(mn)\) is an unbiased estimator of \(\theta = P(B > A) + \tfrac12 P(B = A)\), and the Mann–Whitney test is consistent only against alternatives with \(\theta \ne \tfrac12\). Its permutation null assumes \(F = G\), so with unequal variances and \(\theta = \tfrac12\) the rejection rate is not \(\alpha\). Brunner and Munzel (Biometrical Journal, 2000) studentise the estimate of \(\theta\) with a rank-based variance, which makes the test asymptotically valid for \(H_0: \theta = \tfrac12\) without \(F = G\). Neither test is a test of means or medians unless one adds a shift model.

3.iii · Do a location or a quantile differ?

Third card. The null depends on the feature you name: a quantile, a pseudomedian, a scale. Name the feature first, then the test follows.

3.iii · Generalised locations and quantiles, briefly

  • A generalised location, such as the Hodges–Lehmann shift, is rank-based. It is a shift only if both groups share dispersion and shape.
  • A quantile, such as the median, is compared directly by quantile regression.

Perspective only: one minute. The third and fourth columns of the map. In R: quantreg::rq(y ~ group, tau = 0.5). Hodges–Lehmann is the location that Mann–Whitney estimates when the two distributions differ only by a shift; if the spreads differ, it is no longer a shift. Quantile regression names its target and needs no shape assumption.

Going further. The Hodges–Lehmann estimator of a shift is the median of the \(mn\) pairwise differences \(B_k - A_l\); its confidence interval comes from inverting the Mann–Whitney test, and under a Gaussian shift model its asymptotic efficiency relative to the difference of means is \(3/\pi \approx 0.955\) (Hodges and Lehmann, Annals of Mathematical Statistics, 1963). Quantile regression minimises the check loss \(\sum_i \rho_\tau(Y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta)\) with \(\rho_\tau(u) = u\{\tau - \mathbb{1}(u < 0)\}\) (Koenker and Bassett, Econometrica, 1978); its coefficients are differences of conditional quantiles and are equivariant under monotone transformations of \(Y\).

3.iv · Does a model parameter differ?

Fourth card. This is where the mean lives. The third bullet is the bridge to the next slides: when the assumptions are doubtful, keep the parameter and change how the \(p\)-value is computed.

3.iv · Keep the mean, and change the standard error

Olszewski keeps the mean when that is the question. Welch does not need a pretest of the variances. Yuen changes the target to a trimmed mean. A permutation Welch test keeps the mean when the distribution is not Gaussian.

Read the table row by row: equal variances → ordinary \(t\); unequal → Welch without an \(F\) pretest; outliers → Yuen and a new estimand. The bottom row is permutation or bootstrap \(t\) when the mean stays the question. Yuen is the row where the estimand quietly changes.

Going further. Two means with unequal variances is the Behrens–Fisher problem, which has no exact similar test. Welch’s statistic \((\bar{Y}_1 - \bar{Y}_2)/\sqrt{s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2}\) is referred to a \(t\) with the Welch–Satterthwaite degrees of freedom \(\nu = (s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2)^2 / \{s_1^4/(n_1^2(n_1 - 1)) + s_2^4/(n_2^2(n_2 - 1))\}\). When the variances are in fact equal it loses very little power, while the pooled test can be badly anti-conservative when the smaller group has the larger variance. Yuen’s test applies the same construction to trimmed means and Winsorised variances (Wilcox, Introduction to Robust Estimation and Hypothesis Testing).

3.iv · A permutation \(p\) for the same Welch statistic

Olszewski: MKinfer::perm.t.test uses the Welch–Satterthwaite statistic unless you set var.equal = TRUE. Leave the default. Do not pretest the variances with an \(F\) test.

perm-test.R

result <- MKinfer::perm.t.test(
  response ~ group,
  data = data,
  permStat = TRUE
)
MKinfer::h0plot(result)

The call returns the classical \(p\) and the permutation \(p\). Compare them, and look at the null distribution of the permuted statistic as well as the residual QQ plot. Use more than 9999 draws for a stable \(p\), and on the order of 50,000 if the level of interest is below 0.001. Record the seed. Permuting the raw difference of means quietly assumes equal variances. The studentized statistic is the one Janssen (1997) gives for unequal variances; he lists that paper under the post. A reported permutation \(p\) of exactly 0 is a convention about the draws, not a probability.

Going further. A permutation test is exact when the observations are exchangeable under the null; equal means with unequal variances do not give exchangeability. Chung and Romano (Annals of Statistics, 2013) show that permuting a studentised statistic gives a test that is exact under exchangeability and asymptotically valid under the weaker mean null, which is why the Welch statistic is the one to permute. With \(B\) random permutations, report \((1 + \#\{T^*_b \ge T\})/(1 + B)\), which is never 0 and keeps the test valid (Phipson and Smyth, Statistical Applications in Genetics and Molecular Biology, 2010).

3.iv · A robust estimator limits the pull of one point

\[ \rho_c(u) = \begin{cases} u^2/2, & |u| \le c \\ c\,|u| - c^2/2, & |u| > c \end{cases} \]

Huber regression is least squares for small residuals and absolute error for large ones: MASS::rlm(y ~ group, psi = MASS::psi.huber, k = 1.345). With \(c = 1.345\) it keeps 95% of the efficiency of least squares when the errors are Gaussian. The target is the centre of a symmetric error. Under skew it is neither the mean nor the median: say which you report.

Extension of the parameter card: keep a location, stop one wild core from moving it. Yuen’s trimmed mean, two slides back, is the two-group version of the same idea. Ask what the coefficient means if the residuals are strongly skewed: a third location, not \(\mu\).

Going further. Huber’s M-estimator (Huber, Annals of Mathematical Statistics, 1964) solves \(\sum_i \psi_c(e_i/\hat\sigma)\,\mathbf{x}_i = \mathbf{0}\) with \(\psi_c = \rho_c'\), by iteratively reweighted least squares with weights \(\min(1, c/|e_i/\hat\sigma|)\). Its influence function is bounded in \(Y\) but not in \(\mathbf{x}\), so a high-leverage point can still break it: the regression breakdown point is 0. MM-estimators (Yohai, Annals of Statistics, 1987; robustbase::lmrob) reach a 50% breakdown point with high Gaussian efficiency. Standard errors come from the sandwich form \(A^{-1}BA^{-1}\). Wilcox, Introduction to Robust Estimation and Hypothesis Testing, covers the two-group versions.

Guideline 4 · Change the model, not the data

A test is a model with one factor

Lindeløv writes the common tests as linear models. A Pearson correlation is the slope in lm(scale(y) ~ scale(x)): standardising both variables makes that slope equal to \(r\). A \(\chi^2\) test of counts is a Poisson log-linear model, glm(counts ~ group, family = poisson), compared with an intercept-only model.

The model is the more flexible object. It keeps the same contrast and adds a covariate, a variance weight, or a link. A named test usually cannot.

Pearson \(r\) as lm(scale(y)~scale(x)) and \(\chi^2\) as Poisson glm(counts~group) are the two Lindeløv examples. The model generalises with covariates, weights, and links; the named test often does not.

Going further. Pearson’s \(X^2 = \sum (O - E)^2/E\) is the score test of the Poisson log-linear model of independence against the saturated model, and the likelihood-ratio statistic \(G^2 = 2\sum O \log(O/E)\) is its deviance; both are asymptotically \(\chi^2\) with the same degrees of freedom. The Poisson and multinomial likelihoods give the same estimates for the interaction terms once the margins are fitted, which is why the sampling scheme does not change the test (Agresti, Categorical Data Analysis). The approximation needs expected counts that are not too small, the usual rule being about 5.

A paired \(t\) reports the mean

Loïc, seed 1234, 100 people measured twice. The Wilcoxon signed-rank test gives \(V = 1711\) and \(p = 0.005\). The paired \(t\) test gives a mean difference of \(-0.42\), an interval from \(-0.69\) to \(-0.14\), and \(p = 0.003\). The sign is the order of the two levels.

Both reject. Only the paired \(t\) test says how large the mean change is. The signed-rank test is a statement about the ranks of the within-person differences.

Wilcoxon \(V=1711\), \(p=0.005\) versus paired \(t\) mean difference \(-0.42\) with interval \(-0.69\) to \(-0.14\), \(p=0.003\), seed 1234. Both reject; only the \(t\) states how large the mean shift is. Signed-rank is about ranks of within-person differences.

The pair is a random intercept

An ordinary lm on the same rows, without the person, keeps the estimate at \(0.42\) and inflates the standard error to \(0.28\), so \(p = 0.14\). A random intercept, (1 | ind), keeps \(0.42\) and brings the standard error back to \(0.14\). The \(t\) statistic returns to \(2.99\), the paired test.

In that fit the person-level variance is \(3.06\) and the residual variance is \(0.96\). Dropping the pair does not change the estimate. It charges the within-person contrast with the between-person noise.

Naive lm inflates SE to \(0.28\), \(p=0.14\); (1|ind) returns SE \(0.14\) and \(t=2.99\). Person variance \(3.06\) versus residual \(0.96\) on the slide. Dropping the pairing does not move the \(0.42\) estimate—it adds between-person noise to the wrong error.

Going further. With person variance \(\sigma_b^2\) and residual variance \(\sigma^2\), the random-intercept model implies a compound-symmetric covariance for each person: \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_{ij}) = \sigma_b^2 + \sigma^2\) and \(\operatorname{Cov}(Y_{i1}, Y_{i2}) = \sigma_b^2\). The within-person difference has variance \(2\sigma^2\), free of \(\sigma_b^2\), so \(\mathrm{SE} = \sqrt{2 \times 0.96 / 100} \approx 0.14\), against \(\sqrt{2 \times (3.06 + 0.96)/100} \approx 0.28\) when the pairing is ignored. The intraclass correlation is \(3.06/(3.06 + 0.96) \approx 0.76\). For complete, balanced pairs the REML fit reproduces the paired \(t\) test exactly; Pinheiro and Bates, Mixed-Effects Models in S and S-PLUS (2000), derive it.

A logit link stays inside the bounds

For a recovery, or any proportion, model \(\operatorname{logit}(\pi)\) with \(\pi = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\). When \(Y\) is binary this is logistic regression. The same link is the model for a proportion. The reported interval is the inverse logit of the interval for the linear predictor. It stays in \((0, 1)\), and the test is a test of \(\pi\).

It is not the arithmetic mean of a column that was transformed until a QQ plot looked straight.

Model \(\operatorname{logit}(\pi)\) with \(\pi=\mathbb{E}(Y\mid x)\); inverse logit intervals stay in \((0,1)\). Contrast with transforming a column until a QQ plot looks straight—that is not a test of \(\pi\). Binary and proportion cases share the same link sentence on the slide.

Going further. With a known denominator \(m_i\), a proportion is \(Y_i/m_i\) with \(Y_i \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(m_i, \pi_i)\), fitted with cbind(successes, failures) or weights = m. Without a denominator, Papke and Wooldridge (Journal of Applied Econometrics, 1996) use the binomial quasi-likelihood: the estimating equation needs only \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x}) = \operatorname{logit}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta})\), and a sandwich standard error covers the variance. Beta regression adds a full likelihood for \(Y \in (0, 1)\) (Ferrari and Cribari-Neto, Journal of Applied Statistics, 2004); exact 0s and 1s need a zero- or one-inflated version.

When ordinary least squares is the wrong skeleton

If the Gaussian, independent, constant-variance assumptions fail, the replacements are still models.

  • Generalised least squares: a known pattern for the variance, which is the Welch test when the pattern is a group.
  • Generalised estimating equations: a working correlation and a sandwich standard error.
  • A generalised linear model: a distribution for \(Y\) and a link for \(\mu\).
  • A generalised additive model: a smooth function of \(x\) in place of a straight line.

This hour then stays with the generalised linear model. The others are named so a later choice is a choice of estimand, not a new religion.

Four bullets: GLS, GEE, GLM, GAM—each names what breaks in OLS. This hour stays on GLM; the list prevents swapping religion when the estimand should change. Welch as GLS for group variance is the bridge back to earlier slides.

Going further. With \(\operatorname{Var}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}) = \sigma^2\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\) and \(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\) known, Aitken’s theorem makes \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathrm{GLS}} = (\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{Y}\) the best linear unbiased estimator, by applying Gauss–Markov to \(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{Y}\). In practice \(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\) is estimated (feasible GLS, nlme::gls), which is efficient asymptotically but not exact in small samples. GEE instead keeps \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) simple and corrects only the standard error, trading efficiency for robustness to a wrong \(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\).

From tests to models

The caveats and the guidelines are done. The next block describes the models themselves.

Transition. Everything so far was about what not to do to \(Y\) and which question a test answers. From here on, each slide describes a model: least squares, then ANOVA and ANCOVA, then the generalised linear family.

Linear and generalised linear models

📈 About forty minutes. Least squares, ANOVA and ANCOVA, the Poisson and logistic families, then a smooth mean.

Ordinary least squares, a reminder

The formula is the model

In R the same line is response ~ predictors. Read ~ as “described by”.

  • + adds a term. - or -1 drops one, including the intercept.

  • : is an interaction. * is the interaction and the main effects. ^n adds interactions up to degree \(n\).

  • I() is arithmetic. age^2 in a formula is not \(age^2\). The square is I(age^2).

y ~ 1 is the null model: the mean, and no covariate. One mixed formula example is Income ~ age^2 + I(weight/height^2) + gender.

Step fragments: +/-, :/*/^n, I() for arithmetic. y~1 is intercept-only. age^2 in a formula is not squared age—I(age^2) is. Mixed example at the bottom is one sentence.

One row, then every row

For one observation,

\[ Y_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_{i1} + \cdots + \beta_{p-1} x_{i,p-1} + \varepsilon_i. \]

Stacked, that is \(\mathbf{Y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\). The first column of \(\mathbf{X}\) is 1 when the model has an intercept. Least squares chooses \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) to minimise the sum of squared vertical residuals. The fitted values are \(\hat{\mathbf{Y}} = \mathbf{H}\mathbf{Y}\), and \(\mathbf{H}\) is the hat matrix.

Display \(Y_i=\beta_0+\cdots+\varepsilon_i\), then \(\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\). \(\hat{\mathbf{Y}}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{Y}\) introduces the hat matrix for leverage later.

Ordinary least squares is BLUE

The Gauss–Markov theorem: if \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i) = 0\), the \(\varepsilon_i\) are uncorrelated, each has the same finite variance \(\sigma^2\), and \(\mathbf{X}\) has full column rank, then

\[ \hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} = (\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{Y} \]

has the smallest variance among linear unbiased estimators. The errors do not have to be Gaussian for that result. Gauss used normality. Markov removed it.

If, in addition, the \(\varepsilon_i\) are independent and Gaussian, that same \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) is the maximum-likelihood estimator. The \(t\) tests and the \(F\) test on the printout use this extra assumption. If the variances differ or the errors are correlated, the corresponding estimator is generalised least squares, which is the Welch case when the variance is a group.

Gauss–Markov needs zero mean, uncorrelated equal-variance errors, full-rank \(\mathbf{X}\)—not Gaussian errors for BLUE. Gaussianity enters for MLE and for \(t\)/ \(F\) on the printout. GLS is named when variance differs.

Going further. Gauss–Markov restricts the competition to linear unbiased estimators: a biased estimator such as ridge regression, or the James–Stein estimator when \(p \ge 3\), can have a smaller mean squared error. Without Gaussian errors, \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) is still asymptotically normal, with covariance \(\sigma^2(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\), provided no single row of \(\mathbf{X}\) dominates (the Lindeberg condition, \(\max_i h_{ii} \to 0\)); the \(t\) and \(F\) tests are then approximately valid for large \(n\). With Gaussian errors the result strengthens to minimum variance among all unbiased estimators, since OLS attains the Cramér–Rao bound.

A coefficient is a \(t\)

summary(lm) tests each coefficient against 0. The statistic is the estimate divided by its standard error. Angelino and Marcone note that this test assumes Gaussian residuals. It is the interesting test for a slope. It is rarely the question for the intercept.

In a model with several covariates, each line is the test of that coefficient with the other terms already in the model. Mahanty (9 January 2018) maps the same printout. Two of his readings do not travel: multiple \(R\) is not a count of points on the line, and a small \(p\) is not a verdict that the model fits.

Each summary(lm) line tests that coefficient with others already in. Mahanty’s two warnings: multiple \(R\) is not a point count; small \(p\) is not goodness of fit. Intercept \(t\) is rarely the scientific question.

Going further. Under independent Gaussian errors, \((\hat{\beta}_k - \beta_k)/\mathrm{SE}(\hat{\beta}_k)\) has an exact \(t_{n - p}\) distribution because \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) and \(\hat{\sigma}^2\) are independent and \((n - p)\hat{\sigma}^2/\sigma^2 \sim \chi^2_{n - p}\) (Cochran’s theorem). Without normality the ratio is asymptotically standard normal under the conditions of the previous slide. \(\mathrm{SE}(\hat{\beta}_k) = \hat{\sigma}\sqrt{\{(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\}_{kk}}\) grows when \(x_k\) is correlated with the other columns, by the variance inflation factor \(1/(1 - R_k^2)\).

The \(F\) test compares two nested models

The \(F\) statistic on the same printout compares the fitted model with the intercept-only model. The statistic is the drop in the residual sum of squares, per extra parameter, divided by the remaining residual mean square:

\[ \frac{\{\mathrm{SS}(\mathrm{mean}) - \mathrm{SS}(\mathrm{fit})\}/(p_{\mathrm{fit}} - p_{\mathrm{mean}})}{\mathrm{SS}(\mathrm{fit})/(n - p_{\mathrm{fit}})}. \]

With a single slope, this \(F\) matches the \(t\) test of that slope. With several slopes, every coefficient can be quiet and the \(F\) test can still reject. \(R^2\) is the share of the variance of \(Y\) that the fit captures. Adjusted \(R^2\) charges a cost for each extra term. The residual degrees of freedom are \(n\) minus the number of estimated parameters.

Read the \(F\) ratio formula once; with one slope it matches slope \(t\). Several quiet slopes can still yield a significant \(F\). \(R^2\) and adjusted \(R^2\) definitions are on the slide—df is \(n\) minus parameter count.

Going further. Under Gaussian errors and the null that the extra \(q = p_{\mathrm{fit}} - p_{\mathrm{mean}}\) coefficients are zero, the ratio is exactly \(F_{q,\, n - p_{\mathrm{fit}}}\). With \(q = 1\) it equals the square of the \(t\) statistic of that coefficient. It is a monotone function of the likelihood-ratio statistic for Gaussian models, so it is the small-sample version of the deviance test used for GLMs. \(R^2\) never decreases when a term is added; adjusted \(R^2 = 1 - (1 - R^2)(n - 1)/(n - p)\) can.

Four plots, then two more

plot() on an lm draws four checks by default: residuals against fitted values, a normal QQ plot, scale against location, and residuals against leverage (plot.lm). Those are which = c(1, 2, 3, 5).

Tay (14 November 2019) notes the two that are not in that panel. which = 4 is Cook’s distance against the row number. which = 6 is Cook’s distance against leverage over one minus leverage. He treats a Cook’s distance above 1 as a point to inspect. The next four slides are the default four, each with a fit that respects the assumption and a fit that does not. Illustrative draws, seed 20261005.

Default plot(lm) is which 1,2,3,5; Tay adds Cook vs index (4) and Cook vs leverage (6). Cook \(>1\) to inspect. Next four slides are the default panel, seed 20261005 illustrative fits.

Residuals against the fitted values

A flat cloud is the linear mean. A curve means \(f(x)\) is still in the residual.

Left panel flat cloud is the linear mean assumption; right U-shape is missing curvature in \(f(x)\). Pair with the smoothing section later. This is the first of four diagnostic figures—code was on the prior slide.

The residual QQ plot

Same reading as the shape figures. A line supports the Gaussian assumption behind the \(t\) and \(F\) tests. A flare is a heavy tail, and the next slide changes the model for \(Y\).

Gaussian residuals on the line; \(t\) residuals flare both tails. Same vocabulary as the opening QQ shapes. Flare here motivates changing the family for \(Y\), not only the mean structure.

Scale against location

A flat smooth is constant variance, the Gauss–Markov condition. A rise is a variance that travels with the mean: generalised least squares, or a family in which the variance depends on \(\mu\).

Flat smooth of \(\sqrt{|\text{residual}|}\) is constant variance; rising smooth is variance with mean. Link to \(\phi V(\mu)\) in GLMs and to GLS on the replacements slide.

Leverage and Cook’s distance

Leverage \(h_{ii}\) is the diagonal of the hat matrix. A common cut is \(2p/n\) or \(3p/n\) from Belsley, Kuh, and Welsch (1980). The contours are Cook’s distance 0.5 and 1.

Leverage \(h_{ii}\) from the hat matrix; cuts \(2p/n\) or \(3p/n\) from BKW. Cook contours 0.5 and 1 on the figure. High leverage plus large residual is the point to name on the right panel.

Going further. The leverage \(h_{ii} = \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_i\) lies in \([0, 1]\) and the \(h_{ii}\) sum to \(p\), so their mean is \(p/n\) and \(2p/n\) flags twice the average. Cook’s distance is \(D_i = e_i^2 h_{ii} / \{p\, \hat{\sigma}^2 (1 - h_{ii})^2\}\), the scaled change in all fitted values when row \(i\) is deleted; it combines a large residual with a large leverage. A cut of 1 corresponds roughly to moving \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) to the edge of a 50% confidence region (Cook and Weisberg, Residuals and Influence in Regression, 1982). For a GLM the same quantities come from the weighted hat matrix of the final IRLS step.

ANOVA and ANCOVA

ANOVA is a joint test on a model

  • It is not a test: it uses an \(F\) or a \(\chi^2\) statistic
  • It is not a model: it runs on a fitted model
  • It asks whether the levels of a factor jointly move the mean: all the contrasts of that factor at once, not one pair

anova(lm(y ~ group), lm(y ~ 1)) and summary(aov(lm(y ~ group))) give the same \(F\). 📝 Olszewski, A brief story of ANOVA (2025).

Read the three bullets as his three questions: test, model, then what. In his example a three-level factor gives \(F = 174.6\) on 2 and 87 degrees of freedom whether you call aov, compare two nested models with anova, or test the two contrasts \(\mu_{A1} - \mu_{A2}\) and \((\mu_{A1} + \mu_{A2})/2 - \mu_{A3}\) jointly with emmeans. The “variance” in analysis of variance is the residual variance that drops when the factor enters.

Going further. For a factor with \(L\) levels the ANOVA null is \(\mathbf{C}\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{0}\) for any full-rank \((L-1)\)-row contrast matrix \(\mathbf{C}\); the Wald statistic \((\mathbf{C}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})^{\top}\{\mathbf{C}\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})\mathbf{C}^{\top}\}^{-1}(\mathbf{C}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})/(L-1)\) does not depend on which \(\mathbf{C}\) is chosen. In the Gaussian linear model Wald, score, and likelihood-ratio tests coincide and give the exact \(F\); in a GLM they agree only asymptotically, and the same partition is the analysis of deviance. The construction applies to any model that returns \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) and its covariance, including quantile, robust, and mixed models, which is what emmeans::joint_tests() exploits.

ANCOVA · a factor shifts the intercept

The height example is Height ~ Age + Gender. Gender is a factor, so R codes it as an indicator against a reference level. Women are the baseline in the printout. The line for women is \(124.52 + 2.72\,\mathrm{Age}\). The line for men has the same slope and a higher intercept, \(127.3 + 2.72\,\mathrm{Age}\).

That is an analysis of covariance: one continuous covariate, one categorical factor, and a common slope unless you add Age:Gender. The gender coefficient is a difference of intercepts, not the raw difference of the two mean heights. The sums of squares for that fit still depend on which ANOVA type you ask for.

Height~Age+Gender: women \(124.52+2.72\,\mathrm{Age}\), men same slope, higher intercept \(127.3\). Gender coefficient is intercept difference, not raw mean difference. ANOVA type still matters for SS.

Type I, II, and III answer different questions

  • Type I: each term after the terms written before it
  • Type II: each main effect after the other main effects; an interaction after the mains
  • Type III: each term after every other term, including interactions

The three answer different questions. Pick one before fitting, and do not use Type I unless the order is the scientific order.

For a generalised linear model the same partition is an analysis of deviance. The statistic is a likelihood-ratio \(\chi^2\), or a score test, rather than an \(F\) on residual sums of squares. anova(glm(...), test = "LRT") is that comparison of nested models. It is still a test of a term in a model, not a new model. His 2025 notes put the conditional mean, the probability, the quantile, and the hazard on one page as the functions that comparison can target.

Type I, II, III definitions are different questions—pick before fitting. GLM partition is analysis of deviance with LRT, not \(F\) on SS. His 2025 note bundles mean, probability, quantile, hazard as comparison targets—one line, do not over-read.

Going further. Type II respects marginality (Nelder, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series A, 1977): a main effect is not tested in the presence of an interaction that contains it. Type III does test it, and its sums of squares depend on the contrast coding, so it is meaningful only with sum-to-zero contrasts (contr.sum), not R’s default treatment contrasts. For a GLM, the deviance difference between nested models is asymptotically \(\chi^2\) with the difference in parameters (Wilks’ theorem); when \(\phi\) is estimated, as in a quasi-Poisson fit, the ratio to \(\hat{\phi}\) is referred to an \(F\) instead.

aov() is Type I

Olszewski: aov() is Type I, so the treatment \(F\) in his sketch is 16.6 or 38.7 depending on order. car::Anova(type = 2) does not inherit that order. 📝 His longer ANOVA note asks whether the name is a test or a model.

Treatment \(F\) is 16.6 or 38.7 depending on entry order in Olszewski’s sketch. car::Anova(type=2) is marked safe on the figure. Ask what happens if baseline and treatment are swapped in aov().

Which R function gives which type

Function Type Models Watch
aov() I Linear model ⚠️ The order of the terms changes the result
anova() I or III Most models, including GLMs Usually a comparison of two models
car::Anova() II or III Most models Type III needs contr.sum
emmeans::joint_tests() III GLS, GEE, mixed, quantile, robust Tests estimated marginal means, not raw means

Choose the type for the question, then the function that gives it.

The table is Olszewski’s last-but-one page, shortened. Type II is the safe default for main effects without an interaction; joint_tests() is the route when the model is not a plain lm, and it does not need the contrasts to be set by hand.

Going further. emmeans builds a reference grid with every combination of factor levels, predicts at each, and averages those predictions with equal weights by default; in an unbalanced design that grid is not the observed covariate distribution, so the “marginal mean” is a standardised mean, not the sample mean. The denominator degrees of freedom for mixed models can use the Satterthwaite or Kenward–Roger approximations, and a sandwich covariance can be passed in for heteroscedastic data (Lenth, emmeans vignettes).

When an ANOVA assumption fails, generalise the model

Assumption violated Use instead
Equal variance per group Generalised least squares with one variance per group, nlme::gls() with varIdent()
Independence GLS with a correlation, GEE, or a linear mixed model
Gaussian residuals GEE, or a permutation ANOVA
Outliers A robust linear model

Each keeps the arithmetic mean as the null. A switch to Kruskal–Wallis does not: it tests stochastic superiority.

His example, seed 100: three groups with standard deviations 1, 2, and 5. The ordinary ANOVA gives \(p = 0.142\); the GLS fit with a variance per group gives \(p = 0.011\) for the same means. No Levene pretest is needed: start from the GLS fit.

Going further. With \(\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon_{ij}) = \sigma_j^2\), GLS weights each group by \(1/\hat\sigma_j^2\), which is the multi-group generalisation of the Welch test; the denominator degrees of freedom shrink because the variances are estimated, which is why joint_tests(..., mode = "df.error") reports 85 rather than 87. Permutation ANOVA is exact only under exchangeability, so with unequal variances it should permute a studentised statistic (the Welch-type \(F\)), for the same reason as the two-group case.

Generalised linear models

Favour the model, not a pretest

A pretest that switches you from a \(t\)-test to a rank test still does not estimate \(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\). Rank tests answer a different contrast, and they have assumptions of their own.

The teaching sequence for the three models follows the SIB day on logistic regression and GLMs (course). 📝 Day 2, logistic regression and GLMs. 📝 Day 1a, linear models, is the hour before that sequence.

Walk the Mermaid: question → support → family → residual plot → estimate. Pretest switching \(t\) to ranks still misses \(\mu\). SIB day link is for students who want the full logistic/GLM course—optional aside.

The link acts on \(\mu\), not on \(Y\)

Agresti writes a generalised linear model as one object: a distribution for \(Y\), a linear predictor, and a link on the conditional mean (Agresti 2015).

Link on the mean

Model

\[ g(\mu_i) = \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} \]

After the fit, \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x}) = g^{-1}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta})\) is a probability, an expected count, or a positive rate.

Transform of \(Y\)

\[ g(Y_i) = \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon_i \]

That sentence rewrites each observation before the fit. It targets \(\mathbb{E}\{g(Y)\}\), which is not \(g(\mu)\) when \(g\) is nonlinear.

The estimand slides are what the second column costs.

Left column: \(g(\mu_i)=\mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}\). Right column transforms each \(Y_i\) and targets \(\mathbb{E}\{g(Y)\}\). Walk both callouts; estimand slides are the cost of the right column.

Going further. The families of a GLM are exponential dispersion models, \(f(y; \theta, \phi) = \exp\{(y\theta - b(\theta))/a(\phi) + c(y, \phi)\}\), with \(\mu = b'(\theta)\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = a(\phi)\, b''(\theta)\). The link \(g\) connects \(\mu\) to \(\eta = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}\); the canonical link is the one with \(\theta = \eta\). Nelder and Wedderburn (Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series A, 1972) introduced the class, and McCullagh and Nelder, Generalized Linear Models (1989), is the standard reference; Agresti (Agresti 2015) gives the modern treatment.

The family matches the type of \(Y\)

Ordinary least squares on a transformed response still carries a Gaussian error on whatever scale you chose.

A GLM keeps \(Y\) on its support and changes the family:

  • Bernoulli or binomial for a 0/1 outcome or a proportion
  • Poisson for a non-negative integer count
  • Gaussian on the identity link when that support is appropriate

The family table is the first menu. The point here is that the likelihood is written for \(Y\), not for \(g(Y)\).

OLS on \(g(Y)\) still assumes Gaussian errors on the transformed scale. GLM list: Bernoulli/binomial, Poisson, Gaussian identity. Likelihood is written for \(Y\), not \(g(Y)\)—family table is next.

Going further. The maximum-likelihood score equations of any GLM are \(\sum_i \frac{(Y_i - \mu_i)}{\phi V(\mu_i)} \frac{\partial \mu_i}{\partial \boldsymbol{\beta}} = \mathbf{0}\). They depend on the family only through the mean model and the variance function. Wedderburn (Biometrika, 1974) used this to define quasi-likelihood: specify only \(\mu(\mathbf{x})\) and \(V(\mu)\), solve the same equations, and \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) remains consistent and asymptotically normal if the mean is right. The full family matters for the likelihood itself, for prediction intervals, and for anything that uses the shape beyond two moments.

Variance travels with \(\mu\)

Constant variance

\[ \operatorname{Var}(Y \mid \mathbf{x}) = \sigma^2 \]

This is the classical linear-model assumption. A fan on scale against location is the picture that it has failed.

Variance tied to the mean

\[ \operatorname{Var}(Y_i \mid \mathbf{x}_i) = \phi\, V(\mu_i) \]

Poisson: \(V(\mu) = \mu\). Binomial mean \(\pi\): variance \(\pi(1-\pi)\). The link is part of the family, not a fix applied to the residuals afterwards.

Left: \(\operatorname{Var}(Y\mid x)=\sigma^2\). Right: \(\phi V(\mu)\) with Poisson \(V(\mu)=\mu\) and binomial \(\pi(1-\pi)\). Fan on scale-location is the picture for the left column failing.

Going further. In an exponential dispersion family the variance function \(V(\mu) = b''\{(b')^{-1}(\mu)\}\) determines the family within the natural exponential families. Morris (Annals of Statistics, 1982) shows that exactly six have a quadratic \(V\): Gaussian, Poisson, gamma, binomial, negative binomial, and the generalised hyperbolic secant. The Tweedie class, \(V(\mu) = \mu^p\), interpolates: \(p = 0\) Gaussian, \(p = 1\) Poisson, \(p = 2\) gamma, and \(1 < p < 2\) a compound Poisson–gamma with exact zeros. The dispersion \(\phi\) is 1 for Poisson and binomial; a Pearson estimate \(\hat{\phi} = \sum_i (Y_i - \hat{\mu}_i)^2/V(\hat{\mu}_i)/(n - p)\) well above 1 is overdispersion.

Coefficients read on the link scale

The link is chosen so \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) answers a substantive question on the mean.

For \(\log(\mu) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x\),

\[ \frac{\mu(x+1)}{\mu(x)} = e^{\beta_1}. \]

For a logit on \(\pi\), \(e^{\beta_1}\) is an odds ratio. The goal is not to make a histogram look Gaussian. The goal is a coefficient that names an effect on \(\mu\). The compact contrast is the link stays on the mean.

For \(\log\mu=\beta_0+\beta_1 x\), \(\mu(x+1)/\mu(x)=e^{\beta_1}\). Logit: \(e^{\beta_1}\) is an odds ratio. Goal is substantive effect on \(\mu\), not a pretty histogram.

Going further. Wald intervals are computed on the link scale, \(\hat{\beta}_1 \pm 1.96\, \mathrm{SE}\), and then exponentiated, which keeps a ratio positive; profile-likelihood intervals (confint() on a glm) are more accurate in small samples. The odds ratio approximates the risk ratio only when \(\pi\) is small. The odds ratio is also non-collapsible: adding a covariate unrelated to treatment can change it, even in a randomised trial, because the marginal and conditional odds ratios differ (Greenland, Robins, and Pearl, Statistical Science, 1999). A log-linear rate ratio is collapsible in that sense.

The response chooses the family

Support of \(Y\) A first model
Continuous, unrestricted, stable variance Gaussian linear model
Two categories, truly binary Logit, \(Y \sim \mathrm{Bernoulli}(\pi)\)
Non-negative integer, variance near the mean Poisson, log link
Same, with extra-Poisson variance Negative binomial, log link

The next slides keep \(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\) and change the family. A transformation of \(Y\) is the other route, already set aside on the estimand slides.

Table rows: continuous stable variance → Gaussian; binary → logit; count near equidispersion → Poisson; extra variance → negative binomial. Transformation route is explicitly set aside via log-estimand link.

Going further. Goodness of fit for a GLM is judged by the residual deviance \(D = 2\{\ell(\text{saturated}) - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\}\), which is approximately \(\chi^2_{n - p}\) only when every expected count is large; for sparse counts or binary \(Y\) the reference distribution fails and residual plots are the better check. The negative binomial NB2 has \(V(\mu) = \mu + \mu^2/\theta\), a gamma mixture of Poissons; it is not an exponential dispersion family unless \(\theta\) is fixed, so MASS::glm.nb alternates between \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) and \(\theta\). Hilbe, Negative Binomial Regression (2011), covers the variants.

The link stays on the mean
