Angelino and Marcone write the earlier models as \(g(Y) = f(x) + \text{error}\). On a 0/1 response that line can return a probability below 0, because a straight line is not confined to \((0, 1)\).

In the generalised linear model the linearity sits on \(g(\mu)\). The family then draws \(Y\). The quantity you report is still a mean of the response you measured.

Angelino and Marcone: \(g(Y)=f(x)+\text{error}\) on 0/1 can predict outside \((0,1)\). GLM linearises \(g(\mu)\); family draws \(Y\). Reported quantity remains mean of measured \(Y\).

Going further. A linear probability model, least squares on a 0/1 \(Y\), is unbiased for the slope of \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\) when that mean really is linear, but \(\operatorname{Var}(Y \mid x) = \pi(1 - \pi)\) is heteroskedastic by construction, so it needs a sandwich standard error, and its fitted values can leave \([0, 1]\). The logistic model is the canonical-link GLM for the Bernoulli family, so its score equations \(\sum_i \mathbf{x}_i (Y_i - \hat{\pi}_i) = \mathbf{0}\) make the fitted and observed totals agree within every level of a factor in \(\mathbf{x}\).

One skeleton for all three 🔗

  • Family: how \(\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x)\) depends on \(\mu\)
  • Link \(g\): the scale on which \(\beta_1 x\) is linear
  • Mean \(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\): what \(\beta\) describes, after you invert \(g\)

Agresti is the reference for the theory and the worked models (Agresti 2015). Estimation is by maximum likelihood, not by a normal test of \(Y\). The letters are fixed on the notation slide.

Read the graph left to right: \(x_i\) and \(\boldsymbol\beta\) give \(\eta_i\), the inverse link gives \(\mu_i\), the family gives \(Y_i\). Three bullets map to variance structure, scale of \(\beta\), and mean after inversion. Agresti citation is the theory anchor; MLE not normal test on raw \(Y\).

Going further. Each family here is a one-parameter exponential family, \(f(y;\theta,\phi) = \exp\{[y\theta - b(\theta)]/a(\phi) + c(y,\phi)\}\), so \(\mu = b'(\theta)\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = a(\phi)\,b''(\theta) = \phi V(\mu)\): the variance function \(V\) is what “family” means on this slide. The canonical link is \(g = (b')^{-1}\), which gives the identity, the logit and the log for the Gaussian, Bernoulli and Poisson. With the canonical link, \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\) is sufficient for \(\boldsymbol\beta\) and the score equations reduce to \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{y}-\boldsymbol\mu) = \mathbf{0}\). Nelder and Wedderburn’s 1972 paper unified the three cases; McCullagh and Nelder’s Generalized Linear Models is the classical derivation, and Agresti the modern one (Agresti 2015).

A continuous response, on the identity

A continuous response goes to a linear regression. The drawing is Gaussian, and the link is the identity, so \(\mu\) and the linear predictor are the same number.

Identity link: \(\mu=\eta\). The graph ends at Normal\((\mu,\sigma^2)\), with \(\sigma^2\) a constant. This is linear regression dressed in GLM notation—bridge for students who only know lm.

What \(\sigma\) does to a Gaussian

These are the density functions, not a sample and not CA-SYS. \(\sigma\) changes the spread. The mean shifts the curve. The two parameters are separate, which is the Gaussian case the variance function gives up.

Point at the flat red curve first: same mean, larger \(\sigma\). Then the blue shift. Illustrative densities from scripts/generate-density-curves.R. Twenty seconds.

The fit maximises the likelihood

The fit is by maximum likelihood. For a Gaussian linear model that maximiser is the least-squares fit. For the logit and the Poisson log link there is no closed form: the estimates come from an iterative algorithm.

The Newton step for the logit, from the chapter embedded below, uses the Bernoulli variances as weights:

\[ \boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t+1)} = \boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)} + (\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{(t)}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{y} - \boldsymbol{\pi}^{(t)}), \qquad w_i = \pi_i(1-\pi_i). \]

The course notes state that the maximiser is iterative and do not write the update. The displayed step is Burakov’s equation (2.87), in the chapter embedded later. \(\mathbf{W}\) is diagonal. At convergence, \((\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\) is the estimated covariance of \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\).

Going further. The general algorithm is iteratively reweighted least squares: regress the working response \(z_i = \eta_i + (y_i-\mu_i)\,g'(\mu_i)\) on \(\mathbf{X}\) with weights \(w_i = 1/\{V(\mu_i)\,g'(\mu_i)^2\}\), and repeat. This is Fisher scoring; with the canonical link the observed and expected information coincide, so it is also Newton–Raphson, and for the logit \(w_i = \pi_i(1-\pi_i)\) as on the slide. Under regularity conditions (fixed \(p\), information growing with \(n\), true value inside the parameter space), \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) is consistent and approximately \(\mathrm{Normal}\{\boldsymbol\beta, \phi(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\}\). The maximiser need not exist: for a logit under separation the likelihood increases without bound, the failure on the checks figure. McCullagh and Nelder’s Generalized Linear Models gives the derivation.

Hat values and Cook’s distance, again

The same two diagnostics carry over. Keep the hat-value cut at \(2p/n\) or \(3p/n\), the cut already used for the linear model.

A Cook’s distance above about \(4/n\) is the cut in the SIB notes, and a Cook’s distance above 1 is Tay’s cut for plot(lm), also printed on the checks figure: two sources, so quote the one you use.

Hat cut \(2p/n\) or \(3p/n\) as for lm. Say aloud that \(4/n\) is the SIB cut and 1 is Tay’s cut for plot(lm); they are not two versions of one rule. Next slide is residual types.

Going further. For a GLM the hat matrix is \(\mathbf{H} = \mathbf{W}^{1/2}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{1/2}\), taken from the last IRLS step (Pregibon, 1981, Logistic regression diagnostics, Annals of Statistics). Its trace is still \(p\), which is why the \(2p/n\) cut carries over, but \(h_{ii}\) now depends on the fitted mean as well as on \(\mathbf{x}_i\): in a logit, a point with an extreme \(x\) and \(\hat\pi_i\) near 0 or 1 gets a small weight and can have a small leverage. R’s cooks.distance() for a glm uses the one-step approximation \(D_i = r_{P,i}^2\,h_{ii}/\{\hat\phi\,p\,(1-h_{ii})^2\}\), with \(r_{P,i}\) the Pearson residual, rather than refitting without observation \(i\) (Williams, 1987, Applied Statistics).

Three residual scales

Three residuals are worth plotting after a GLM.

  • A deviance residual, plotted against the fitted value and against each covariate. A curve is a missing piece of the linear predictor.

  • A Pearson residual, \((Y - \mu)\) divided by the fitted standard deviation. A trend against a covariate is a term the model does not yet contain.

  • A quantile residual. Under the fitted family it is uniform on \((0,1)\). A QQ plot against normal quantiles is the picture. Extra-large tails are a signal to leave the Poisson for a quasi-Poisson or a negative binomial.

acf() is a quick check that the observations are not ordered in a way the independence assumption forbids. The Poisson simulation later draws the quantile residual for that family.

Step deviance, Pearson, quantile residuals and what curve each catches. Quantile residuals uniform under the fitted family—QQ against normal is the plot. acf() flags ordered dependence.

Going further. With \(d_i\) the contribution of observation \(i\) to the deviance, the deviance residual is \(\mathrm{sign}(y_i-\hat\mu_i)\sqrt{d_i}\) and the Pearson residual is \((y_i-\hat\mu_i)/\sqrt{\hat\phi\,V(\hat\mu_i)}\). Both are only approximately normal, and for binary data or small counts they fall in bands, one per observed value, so a residual-against-fitted plot shows stripes whatever the model. The quantile residual is Dunn and Smyth’s (1996, Randomized quantile residuals, Journal of Computational and Graphical Statistics): \(r_i = \Phi^{-1}(u_i)\), with \(u_i\) uniform between \(F(y_i^-;\hat\mu_i)\) and \(F(y_i;\hat\mu_i)\). If the model is correct and its parameters known, the \(r_i\) are exactly independent standard normal; with estimated parameters this holds asymptotically. The DHARMa package builds the same residual by simulating from the fitted model, which also covers mixed models.

Deviance is the residual sum of squares

Lack of fit is measured by the deviance. The null deviance \(D_0\) compares the intercept-only model with the saturated model. The residual deviance \(D\) compares the fitted model with the saturated model. Maximising the log-likelihood is the same operation as making \(D\) small.

For nested models the likelihood-ratio statistic is the drop in deviance,

\[ D_{\text{small}} - D_{\text{large}} \sim \chi^2, \]

with degrees of freedom equal to the number of extra parameters. In R that is anova(model1, model2, test = "Chisq"). This is the GLM form of the analysis of deviance.

Their pseudo-\(R^2\) is \(1 - D/D_0\). They say that several pseudo-\(R^2\) exist, and that a nested comparison should be the likelihood-ratio test. AIC is the comparison they allow when the models are not nested: deviance, plus a penalty for the number of parameters.

Going further. The scaled deviance is \(D/\phi = 2\{\ell_{\text{sat}} - \ell(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})\}\), and \(\phi = 1\) for the Poisson and the Bernoulli. Wilks’ theorem makes \((D_{\text{small}} - D_{\text{large}})/\phi\) approximately \(\chi^2_q\) for nested models, with \(p\) fixed and \(n \to \infty\); when \(\phi\) is estimated, as in the quasi-Poisson, the reference is an \(F\) distribution (test = "F"). The residual deviance itself is \(\chi^2_{n-p}\) only under small-dispersion asymptotics, large Poisson means or large binomial totals, because the saturated model gains a parameter with every observation. For 0/1 data the deviance is a function of the \(\hat\pi_i\) alone and says nothing about fit; use a grouped check such as Hosmer–Lemeshow, or the residual plots. McCullagh and Nelder’s Generalized Linear Models treats these limits.

Quantile residuals check the family

These are randomised quantile residuals for a Poisson mean: if the family is right, they should look Gaussian. A heavy upper tail is a reason to leave the Poisson, not a reason to run Shapiro–Wilk on the raw counts.

quantile-residuals.R

lower <- ifelse(y <= 0, 0, ppois(y - 1L, lambda = mu_hat))
upper <- ppois(y, lambda = mu_hat)
residual <- qnorm(runif(length(y), min = lower, max = upper))

Walk the code first: each count is spread uniformly over its step of the fitted distribution function, then mapped to the normal scale. The figure is on the next slide.

Quantile residuals check the family

Walk the code, then the QQ plot. Stress that the reference is the fitted family, not marginal normality of the counts. Ask whether a heavy upper tail would change the variance model before they quote a rate ratio. About one minute.

Poisson regression

A count, on the log

A count goes to a Poisson regression. The mean is often written as \(\lambda\). That symbol is \(\mu\) on these slides. The log link keeps \(\mu > 0\), and \(\exp(\beta_1)\) is a multiplicative change in the mean count.

Four working assumptions: \(Y\) is a count per unit of time or space; \(\log \mu\) is linear in \(x\); the observations are independent; and \(\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu\). 📝 StatHarbor, Count data models.

\(\lambda\) on the slide is \(\mu\) here; \(\exp(\beta_1)\) multiplies mean count. Four assumptions at the bottom—especially \(\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid x)=\mu\)—are what CA-SYS will break.

Going further. Under the log link the Poisson log-likelihood is \(\sum_i (y_i\eta_i - e^{\eta_i})\) up to a constant, so the score is \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{y}-\boldsymbol\mu)\) and, with an intercept, the fitted counts add up to the observed total; a factor covariate reproduces each group total exactly. The estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\) stays consistent when the counts are not Poisson, provided \(\log\mu\) is correctly specified: this is the pseudo-maximum-likelihood result of Gourieroux, Monfort and Trognon (1984, Econometrica). What breaks is the standard error, which relies on \(\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid x)=\mu\); a sandwich estimator or a variance model repairs it (Cameron and Trivedi, Regression Analysis of Count Data).

What \(\lambda\) does to a Poisson

\(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \lambda = \mathbb{E}(Y)\). A larger mean is a wider plot. Illustrative probabilities from scripts/generate-density-curves.R, not a soil count.

The right-hand comb is both higher and wider. That is the one-parameter constraint. If their counts are wider than this picture, the next family is the negative binomial.

Poisson · when the count matches the story

Use it when \(Y\) is a non-negative integer and, to a first approximation, \(\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu\).

\[ \log \mu = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x. \]

Colony counts on a plate are the clean case. Sequence reads are a count too, but with two complications: the sampling effort differs between samples, and the counts are more variable than a Poisson.

\(\log\mu=\beta_0+\beta_1 x\) with data-id animate. Colony counts are clean; reads need offset and extra variance—foreshadow CA-SYS. Var equals mean is the assumption to test first.

Poisson · the CA-SYS version

\[ \log \mu_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 \mathrm{tilled}_i + \mathrm{year}_i + \log(\text{library size}_i). \]

\(Y\) is the number of Nitrospira reads in a sample: a nitrite-oxidising genus, 153,771 reads across the 595 samples. The last term is the offset, so \(\beta_1\) is a ratio of rates per read.

Point out that the equation changed in two places from the previous slide: year entered as a factor, and the offset appeared. Neither is cosmetic. Without the offset, a sample sequenced twice as deeply looks twice as rich in Nitrospira.

Poisson · the call

poisson-dispersion.R

casys <- read.csv("data/derived/casys_teaching.csv")

fit_pois <- glm(
  nitrospira_reads ~ tillage + factor(year) + offset(log(total_reads)),
  family = poisson,
  data = casys
)

# Is one parameter doing both jobs?
sum(residuals(fit_pois, "pearson")^2) / fit_pois$df.residual
#> 10.6

A Poisson sets \(\mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu\). A Pearson statistic of 10.6 per degree of freedom says the data are about ten times more variable than that.

Run this if the laptop allows. The dispersion statistic is the number to look at before reading any coefficient, because the coefficient will look fine and the standard error will not. The rule of thumb is that a value near 1 is consistent with the Poisson; 10.6 is not a borderline case.

Going further. The statistic is the moment estimator of the dispersion, \(\hat\phi = X^2/(n-p)\) with \(X^2 = \sum_i (y_i-\hat\mu_i)^2/V(\hat\mu_i)\); under a correct Poisson model \(X^2\) is approximately \(\chi^2_{n-p}\), so \(\hat\phi\) is near 1. The quasi-Poisson fit keeps the Poisson estimates and multiplies every standard error by \(\sqrt{\hat\phi}\); here \(\sqrt{10.6} \approx 3.3\), close to the threefold widening the negative binomial gives a few slides on. The deviance-based estimate \(D/(n-p)\) is less reliable when many \(\hat\mu_i\) are small, which is why McCullagh and Nelder prefer the Pearson form. A formal check is the score test of Dean and Lawless (1989, Journal of the American Statistical Association).

A table of counts is a log-linear model

Penn State STAT 504, Lesson 10 models a contingency table by a Poisson GLM. The cell count is \(Y_{ab} \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(\mu_{ab})\). The lesson is this log-linear model. Independence, with neither margin as the response, is

\[ \log \mu_{ab} = \alpha + \alpha_a + \alpha_b. \]

\(Y_{ab}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\mu_{ab})\); independence \(\log\mu_{ab}=\alpha+\alpha_a+\alpha_b\). PSU STAT 504 lesson—joint model, not logit of one margin.

Going further. Independent Poisson cell counts, conditioned on their total \(n\), are multinomial, and Birch (1963, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B) showed that a Poisson log-linear model with an intercept gives the same maximum-likelihood fitted counts as the multinomial, or as product-multinomial sampling when the fixed margins are in the model. That is why a Poisson glm can fit a contingency table whatever the sampling plan. The residual deviance of the independence model is the likelihood-ratio \(G^2\) on \((A-1)(B-1)\) degrees of freedom for an \(A \times B\) table, asymptotically equivalent to Pearson’s \(X^2\). Bishop, Fienberg and Holland’s Discrete Multivariate Analysis develops the hierarchy of log-linear models.

Independence, drawn

The fitted counts are row total times column total over \(n\), and every odds ratio equals 1. A logit model is the conditional distribution of one margin. This log-linear model is the joint distribution. Dummy coding changes the separate coefficients. Differences of coefficients, the log odds, stay put.

A logit-equivalent log-linear model includes every association among the predictors. The lesson contrasts R’s dummy coding, first level set to 0, with the zero-sum constraint in loglin(). Expected counts do not depend on that choice.

Negative binomial regression, and other counts

Negative binomial · same mean, one extra parameter

Keep the log link. Add a dispersion parameter \(\theta > 0\):

Model

\[ \mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu + \frac{\mu^2}{\theta}. \]

This is the MASS::glm.nb parameterisation: large \(\theta\) means “close to Poisson”. Agresti writes the same quadratic variance with a different symbol for the extra term (Agresti 2015). On these reads \(\hat\theta = 29.3\). 📄 Townes reviews the count families in Review of probability distributions for modeling count data.

Two readings of theta. Algebraically it is the inverse of the extra variance per unit of mean squared. Mechanistically it is a gamma-distributed latent rate, integrated out, which is the derivation on the Poisson-gamma slide. Stress that theta is not a second scientific effect to interpret.

Going further. If \(\lambda = \mu\nu\) with \(\nu \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(\theta, \theta)\), so that \(\mathbb{E}(\nu)=1\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(\nu) = 1/\theta\), integrating \(\nu\) out of \(\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)\) gives \(\Pr(Y=y) = \frac{\Gamma(y+\theta)}{\Gamma(\theta)\,y!}\left(\frac{\theta}{\theta+\mu}\right)^{\theta}\left(\frac{\mu}{\theta+\mu}\right)^{y}\), Cameron and Trivedi’s NB2, with the quadratic variance on the slide. NB1, \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = (1+\delta)\mu\), has the quasi-Poisson’s shape and is a different model. MASS::glm.nb alternates IRLS for \(\boldsymbol\beta\) at fixed \(\theta\) with a maximum-likelihood step for \(\theta\) (Venables and Ripley, Modern Applied Statistics with S). Testing the Poisson against it puts \(1/\theta = 0\) on the boundary of the parameter space, so the likelihood-ratio statistic is a 50:50 mixture of \(\chi^2_0\) and \(\chi^2_1\) and the naive \(p\)-value should be halved (Self and Liang, 1987, Journal of the American Statistical Association).

Same mean, three values of \(\theta\)

The mean stays 8. Small \(\theta\) puts more mass in the tail, because \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mu + \mu^2/\theta\). Large \(\theta\) collapses toward the Poisson. Illustrative, from scripts/generate-density-curves.R.

Cover the grey Poisson last. Ask which \(\theta\) matches a Pearson dispersion near 1. The answer is the large one. The CA-SYS \(\hat\theta\) of 29 is in that direction, not at 1.

One mean, two variance assumptions

Family Rate ratio 95% interval
Poisson 0.972 0.962 to 0.982
Negative binomial 0.971 0.941 to 1.002

This is the slide to slow down on. The point estimate barely moves, 0.972 against 0.971, because both models use the same mean equation. The interval is 3.1 times wider. Under the Poisson the contrast is “detected”; under the negative binomial it crosses one. Nothing about the biology changed between the two rows, only an assumption about the variance. Ask the room which interval they would put in a paper.

Going further. The near-identical point estimates are expected: when \(\log\mu\) is correctly specified, the Poisson score equations give a consistent \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) whatever the true variance (Gourieroux, Monfort and Trognon, 1984), and the negative binomial only re-weights observations, by \(\mu_i/(1+\mu_i/\theta)\) instead of \(\mu_i\). The two fits therefore differ in efficiency and, mostly, in the covariance matrix. A third option keeps the Poisson estimate with a sandwich covariance, valid under any variance function but less stable in small samples (sandwich package). Ver Hoef and Boveng (2007, Ecology) compare quasi-Poisson and negative-binomial weighting and suggest plotting binned squared residuals against \(\hat\mu\) to choose between a linear and a quadratic variance.

Read the mean, then \(\theta\)

  • The rate ratio is a statement about \(\mu\), on the log link, per read
  • \(\theta\) is not a second effect; it describes leftover variation
  • A QQ plot of quantile residuals is the check, not a test that “selects NB”
  • With the wider interval, 1.00 is compatible with these data

A 2.9% lower rate under tillage would matter agronomically. These 595 samples cannot separate it from no difference.

The last line is the honest summary, and it is the pitfalls lesson applied to a real fit: the estimate is the result, and the interval says how much the data constrain it. Resist the temptation to call this a null finding. It is an imprecise estimate of a small contrast, which is a different sentence.

The wrong family, on a QQ plot

Left is what you get if you put these counts into lm(). Right is the family the offset and the dispersion were chosen for. This is the same diagnostic as the earlier QQ slides, now on a real response rather than a draw, and it is the reason the family question comes before the test question.

Same count, three implementations

  • Poisson
  • Negative binomial
  • \(\log(Y+1)\), then Gaussian

\[ \log \mu = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \log(\text{library size}), \qquad \mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu. \]

Tilled against no-till: rate ratio 0.972 (0.962 to 0.982). Pearson \(\chi^2/\mathrm{df} = 10.6\), so that interval is too narrow.

\[ \mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu + \mu^2/\theta, \qquad \hat\theta = 29.3. \]

Same mean equation: rate ratio 0.971 (0.941 to 1.002). The interval is 3.1 times wider and crosses 1.

\[ \mathbb{E}\{\log(Y+1) \mid x\} = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}. \]

That coefficient is not a rate ratio for \(\mu\), and the library size no longer enters as an offset. The two means are not the same function of \(x\).

Arrow keys step the tabs. All three are fits to the CA-SYS reads, from generate-data-figures.R, using casys_teaching.csv.

Tab through Poisson, NB, \(\log(Y+1)\) Gaussian—arrow keys on slide. Rate ratio 0.972 vs 0.971, Pearson 10.6, \(\hat\theta=29.3\), log-plus-one loses offset interpretation. Script path generate-data-figures.R if asked.

A count that is not Poisson

Richness is an integer, so the reflex is a Poisson. Pearson \(\chi^2/\mathrm{df} = 27.8\) says otherwise: richness is a count of OTUs out of 10,633, bounded above and driven by sequencing effort.

A useful corrective. “Integer” does not imply “Poisson”. Richness here is the number of distinct OTUs detected, so it is closer to a capture problem than to a rate: it is bounded by the reference table and it rises with library size. The honest teaching answer is that observed richness is a biased estimator of richness, and that the estimator belongs to the diversity literature rather than to a GLM slide. Note the 2018 panel is the pre-treatment baseline.

A gamma latent rate is a negative binomial

The same derivation gives the negative binomial as a Poisson–gamma compound: the Poisson rate is itself a gamma draw, and that draw is integrated out. The extra variance is unobserved heterogeneity.

\[ \mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu + \frac{\mu^2}{\alpha} \qquad\text{or}\qquad \mathrm{Var}(Y \mid x) = \mu + \alpha\mu^2. \]

McCullagh and Nelder use the first form. The simulation slides call that shape \(\theta\). In the second form, \(\alpha \to 0\) returns the Poisson. Estimating \(\alpha\) leaves the one-parameter exponential family.

Treating \(\alpha\) as known keeps the model inside a one-parameter GLM. Estimating it is full maximum likelihood. StatHarbor reaches for the negative binomial when the variance exceeds the mean and the zeros come from the same process as the positive counts, and compares it with the Poisson by a likelihood-ratio test.

Going further. The derivation is two lines: \(\mathbb{E}(Y) = \mathbb{E}(\lambda) = \mu\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}(\lambda) + \mathrm{Var}(\lambda) = \mu + \mu^2/\theta\) when \(\lambda\) is gamma with shape \(\theta\) and mean \(\mu\). Another mixing law gives another margin with a similar variance: a log-normal \(\lambda\) also has \(\mathrm{Var}(\lambda) \propto \mu^2\), so the Poisson log-normal shares the quadratic variance and differs in the tails. Greenwood and Yule (1920, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society) introduced this gamma mixture for accident counts; Hilbe’s Negative Binomial Regression is the applied reference. The likelihood-ratio test against the Poisson has the boundary problem noted on the \(\theta\) slide.

A point mass of zeros is a mixture

A zero-inflated model is a different generative story. With probability \(\pi_0\) the observation is a structural zero. With probability \(1-\pi_0\) it is a draw from a count distribution \(f\). StatHarbor writes \(\pi_0\) as \(p\).

\[ \Pr(Y=0) = \pi_0 + (1-\pi_0)\,f(0), \qquad \Pr(Y=k>0) = (1-\pi_0)\,f(k). \]

A logit for \(\pi_0\) plus a log-linear Poisson is the zero-inflated Poisson. The negative binomial in \(f\) is the zero-inflated negative binomial. Neither is an exponential-family GLM.

StatHarbor compares a zero-inflated model with an ordinary Poisson by AIC, BIC, or a Vuong test, because those two models are not nested.

Going further. The zero-inflated Poisson is Lambert’s (1992, Technometrics); it is fitted by maximum likelihood, or by EM with the latent structural-zero indicator. Vuong’s test (1989, Econometrica) compares non-nested models through the mean and variance of the per-observation log-likelihood ratios. But the Poisson is the zero-inflated Poisson at \(\pi_0 = 0\), on the boundary, so the two are in fact nested there and the Vuong reference distribution does not hold; Wilson (2015, Economics Letters) documents this misuse. A boundary-corrected likelihood-ratio test, or a plot of predicted against observed zero frequencies, is the safer check. A hurdle model (Mullahy, 1986, Journal of Econometrics) tells the other story: every zero comes from one binary process and the positives from a zero-truncated count.

Four count models, three questions

Model Extra variance Structural zeros Exponential family
Poisson No No Yes
Negative binomial Yes No Only if the dispersion is fixed
Zero-inflated Poisson No Yes No
Zero-inflated negative binomial Yes Yes No

The practical line under the table: diagnose dispersion and extra zeros before you quote a rate ratio. An ill-matched family understates the uncertainty.

Table: extra variance, structural zeros, exponential family column. Practical line under table—diagnose before quoting a rate ratio. ZIP and ZINB are not GLMs in the strict sense.

The count measures a latent share

Sarkar and Stephens separate a measurement model from a model for the latent intensity. The paper is about genes in single cells. The same split applies to any feature counted inside a sample of finite depth: a taxon, a transcript, a fragment.

Let \(s_i\) be the library size, the total count in sample \(i\), and let \(\lambda_{i\ell}\) be the relative intensity of feature \(\ell\), a share of that sample. The measurement model is

\[ Y_{i\ell} \mid s_i, \lambda_{i\ell} \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(s_i \lambda_{i\ell}). \]

Dividing by the depth is this rate: the offset \(\log s_i\), with its coefficient fixed at 1.

\(Y_{i\ell}\mid s_i,\lambda_{i\ell}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(s_i\lambda_{i\ell})\); offset \(\log s_i\) with coefficient 1. Sarkar–Stephens single-cell paper, same logic for any feature in finite depth.

Going further. An offset is a covariate whose coefficient is fixed at 1: \(\log\mu_i = \log s_i + \mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta\) is \(\mu_i/s_i = \exp(\mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta)\), so \(\boldsymbol\beta\) acts on the rate per read. Entering \(\log s_i\) as an ordinary covariate and testing its slope against 1 checks that proportionality. Modelling \(y_i/s_i\) with a Gaussian model is not equivalent: it discards the fact that the variance of a proportion falls with depth. Differential-abundance software often replaces the raw library size by a robust size factor, such as the median-of-ratios of Anders and Huber (2010, Genome Biology), because a few dominant features can distort the total.

The measurement drawing

Library size and relative intensity meet in the Poisson mean. The Pearson-residual figure below is this regression, with the slope of \(\log s_i\) fixed.

Graph: \(\lambda\) and \(s\) meet in the dotted node \(\mu=s\lambda\), then the Poisson draw. Pearson-residual figure below is this regression with fixed log-depth slope.

Three assumptions in the measurement layer

Box 1 of Sarkar and Stephens lists the assumptions behind that Poisson measurement model.

  • In each sample, each molecule is equally likely to be observed.

  • Each molecule is observed independently of the others.

  • Only a small proportion of the molecules present are observed. They cite 10–20% in a typical single-cell experiment.

Assumptions 2 and 3 are the ones they treat as plausible. Assumption 1 fails when stability, cellular location, or sequence changes the chance that a molecule is seen. A bias that is shared across samples can enter the Poisson model as a covariate. A bias that changes from sample to sample is extra noise, and the measurement model that carries it is negative binomial. If a later protocol observes a larger share of the molecules, the measurement variance falls relative to a Poisson.

Step fragments: equal capture probability (often fails), independence (plausible), small observed fraction 10–20% (plausible). Sample-specific bias → NB measurement model per slide text.

A zero is an observation

Sarkar and Stephens refuse the phrase “missing data” for a zero count. The picture: a minute with no cars at an intersection is a zero; an hour when the observer is away is a missing record. A sequencing zero is the first of those. Imputing only the zeros treats them as holes.

They also drop the word “dropout” for a supposed device that writes a zero whatever the true intensity. Spike-in controls, in the papers they cite, did not support a zero-inflated measurement model. A zero-inflated observation model can still be the right margin when the latent intensity itself has a point mass at zero.

Cars-at-intersection versus absent observer—sequencing zero is the first. No impute-only-zeros; spike-in evidence against dropout measurement model. ZI on latent intensity can still be right.

The observation model is the margin

An expression model is a distribution \(g_\ell\) for the latent share \(\lambda_{i\ell}\). The observation model is the distribution of \(Y\) after \(\lambda\) has been integrated out.

A gamma \(g_\ell\) produces a negative-binomial margin. A point-gamma \(g_\ell\), a spike at zero plus a gamma, produces a zero-inflated negative-binomial margin. The Poisson recommendation is for the measurement layer. The margin is usually wider, because the latent share varies across samples.

\(g_\ell\) for latent \(\lambda\); integrate to margin of \(Y\). Gamma → NB margin; point-gamma → ZINB. Poisson recommendation is measurement layer; margin usually wider.

One feature at a time

Latent model for \(\lambda\) Margin of \(Y\) Methods they name
Point mass Poisson Analytic
Gamma Negative binomial MASS, edgeR, DESeq2, SAVER, BASiCS
Point-gamma Zero-inflated NB PSCL
Unimodal, nonparametric Unimodal ashr
Point-exponential family Flexible DESCEND
Fully nonparametric Flexible ashr

Table 1 of Sarkar and Stephens. Each row is a latent model for one feature, combined with the Poisson measurement model. The method column is the software they list for that margin.

Link \(h\) Noise on the latent scale Methods they name

Log Gamma scNBMF, GLM-PCA

Neural network Point-gamma scVI, DCA

Overdispersion relative to the Poisson measurement changes the variance of \(Y\) and leaves the mean of the structured part to be read with that measurement model in mind.

Depth in the offset, dispersion in \(\theta\)

Read the gene labels as any feature. Depth is the offset. The quadratic term in \(\theta_\ell\) is the extra variance.

Panel A: NB variance \(\mu+\mu^2/\theta\) with biology and depth. B: offset regression; C: Pearson \(r\); D: shrink \(\hat\theta_\ell\). Gene labels stand in for any feature.

The Pearson residual for that fit

\[ \log \mu_{i\ell} = \beta_{0\ell} + \log s_i, \qquad r_{i\ell} = \frac{Y_{i\ell}-\mu_{i\ell}}{\sqrt{\mu_{i\ell}+\mu_{i\ell}^2/\theta_\ell}}. \]

The coefficient of \(\log s_i\) is fixed at 1. A Poisson model has no \(\theta_\ell\), so the split between a latent intensity and a depth has a parameter in the negative binomial. The figure’s last panel shrinks a noisy \(\hat\theta_\ell\) toward a smooth of the mean.

Display \(\log\mu_{i\ell}=\beta_{0\ell}+\log s_i\) and Pearson \(r_{i\ell}\) formula. Coefficient on \(\log s_i\) fixed at 1. Last panel shrinks noisy \(\hat\theta_\ell\)—split measurement from dispersion.

Going further. Hafemeister and Satija (2019, Genome Biology, sctransform) fitted this regression feature by feature and regularised the parameters across features; Lause, Berens and Kobak (2021, Genome Biology) showed that with the slope of \(\log s_i\) fixed at 1 and a shared \(\theta\), the Pearson residuals have a closed form and need no per-feature fit. The residuals are usually clipped at about \(\pm\sqrt{n}\), because a feature seen in a single sample gives one enormous value. Shrinking \(\hat\theta_\ell\) toward a trend in the mean is an empirical-Bayes step, the same idea as the dispersion shrinkage in DESeq2 (Love, Huber and Anders, 2014, Genome Biology).

Condition on the total, or integrate a prior

Townes. Vertical arrows condition on the sum. Horizontal arrows integrate a continuous prior. Gamma and Dirichlet are the continuous nodes.

Townes map: vertical condition on sum, horizontal integrate prior. Poisson→multinomial, NB→Dirichlet-multinomial; gamma↔︎Dirichlet. Continuous nodes are gamma and Dirichlet.

The first two moments, by the tower rule

Write the Poisson rate as a latent \(\lambda\) with \(\mathbb{E}(\lambda) = \mu\). The law of total expectation and the law of total variance give the margin without a new pmf:

\[ \mathbb{E}(Y) = \mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(Y\mid\lambda)\} = \mu, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}(\lambda) + \mathrm{Var}(\lambda) = \mu + \mathrm{Var}(\lambda). \]

Read \(\mathbb{E}(Y)=\mu\) and \(\mathrm{Var}(Y)=\mu+\mathrm{Var}(\lambda)\) from the tower identities. No new pmf needed—variance inflation from latent \(\lambda\).

Going further. The law of total variance, \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}\{\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid\lambda)\} + \mathrm{Var}\{\mathbb{E}(Y\mid\lambda)\}\), holds for any latent \(\lambda\) with finite variance, so every Poisson mixture is overdispersed: \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) \geq \mathbb{E}(Y)\), with equality only when \(\lambda\) is constant. That is why overdispersion is the default for counts pooled over heterogeneous units, and why an underdispersed count cannot be a Poisson mixture. A gamma \(\lambda\) with \(\mathrm{Var}(\lambda) = \mu^2/\theta\) gives the negative binomial; a log-normal with \(\log\lambda \sim \mathrm{Normal}(m, \sigma^2)\) gives \(\mathrm{Var}(\lambda) = \mu^2(e^{\sigma^2}-1)\). Karlis and Xekalaki (2005, Mixed Poisson distributions, International Statistical Review) review the family.

Logistic regression

A binary response, on the logit

A binary response goes to a logistic regression. Here \(\mu = \pi = \Pr(Y = 1 \mid x)\). The logit is the link used here. The probit is the alternative they mention later, and it stays close to this curve.

\(\mu=\pi=\Pr(Y=1\mid x)\); logit link in the diagram. Probit mentioned as later alternative—stay on logit for odds-ratio reading.

Detection depends on how hard you looked

Methylobacter is a methane oxidiser, present in 35% of samples. The odds of detecting it multiply by 1.74 per standard deviation of log library size.

This is the logit on a real response, and the result is uncomfortable in a useful way: the strongest predictor of presence is not a treatment, it is sequencing effort. The tillage odds ratio from the same fit is 1.34, with an interval of 0.95 to 1.91, so it is not separable from no effect here. Any presence-absence analysis of sequencing data has to carry library size, exactly as the count model carries the offset.

What \(\beta_1\) does to a probability

\(\pi = \mathrm{logit}^{-1}(\beta_0 + \beta_1 x)\). A larger \(\beta_1\) makes the step steeper. \(\beta_0\) slides it. The curve stays inside \((0, 1)\). Illustrative, from scripts/generate-density-curves.R.

The steep curve is not a larger probability everywhere. It is a faster change. Ask where \(\pi\) is one half on the red curve.

Logit · only if \(Y\) is really binary

Detection of a taxon in a core is binary. A flux cut at an arbitrary threshold is the dichotomisation we have already rejected.

\[ \mu = \pi = \Pr(Y = 1 \mid x). \]

The mean of a 0/1 response is the probability.

\(\mu=\pi=\Pr(Y=1\mid x)\); flux dichotomisation rejected. Mean of 0/1 is probability—say it plainly before the simulated pH slide.

Logit · a simulated detection

\(x\) is centred pH, from \(-1.5\) to \(1.5\). \(Y\) is 1 if the taxon is detected.

\[ \mathrm{logit}(\pi) = \log \frac{\pi}{1-\pi} = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x. \]

Fitted here: \(\hat\beta_0 = -0.32\), \(\hat\beta_1 = 1.44\).

At \(x = 0\), \(\hat\pi = \mathrm{logit}^{-1}(-0.32) \approx 0.42\).

Same seed, same script, still not a field dataset.

\(\hat\beta_0=-0.32\), \(\hat\beta_1=1.44\); at \(x=0\), \(\hat\pi\approx 0.42\). Centred pH \(-1.5\) to \(1.5\); same seed script, not field data.

Logit · the curve is the result

The band is for \(\pi\), not for a single core. A core is still 0 or 1.

fit_bin <- glm(y_bin ~ ph, family = binomial)

Logit · the curve is the result

Stress that the band is for \(\pi\), not for a single 0/1 core. The code slide is excerpt only; the figure is the saved fit. Ask the room to say the estimand in words before they quote \(\exp(\beta_1)\). About one minute.

Logit · odds move by a factor, probabilities do not

A one-unit increase in centred pH multiplies the odds by

\[ \exp(\hat\beta_1) = 4.2 \]

Wald 95% interval: 2.4 to 7.4.

In this fit, \(\hat\pi\) goes from about 0.42 at \(x = 0\) to about 0.75 at \(x = 1\). That is a change of about 0.34 on the probability scale, not a 4.2-fold change in probability.

The interval is symmetric on the logit scale. It is not symmetric on the probability scale.

\(\exp(\hat\beta_1)=4.2\), Wald interval 2.4 to 7.4 on odds scale. \(\hat\pi\) about 0.42 to 0.75 is ~0.34 on probability scale—not a 4.2-fold change in \(\pi\). Interval symmetric on logit, not on \(\pi\).

The regression is for \(\pi\)

Olszewski places logistic regression in the generalised linear model: the target is a function of \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\). For a Bernoulli response that function is \(\mathrm{logit}(\pi)\), and \(\pi\) is the conditional mean. A classifier that thresholds \(\pi\) is one use of the fitted probability.

He dates the logit to Berkson, as an alternative to the probit, and the unified GLM to Nelder and Wedderburn (1972, their paper). In R the same statement is glm(y ~ x, family = binomial(link = "logit")).

Olszewski: logistic regression targets \(\mathrm{logit}(\pi)\); threshold classifier is one use. Berkson logit history and Nelder–Wedderburn GLM paper as on slide; R call family=binomial(link="logit").

A test of two proportions is this model

In the first of the two follow-ups, the Wald statistic for a difference of two proportions, with unpooled variances, matches the average marginal effect of a logistic regression with one binary predictor. The sampling scheme is two independent binomials, without a small-sample correction. He calls the algebra a conceptual bridge: the statistics match, and the note does not re-prove the asymptotic distribution.

The second note matches the pooled two-proportion \(z\), the statistic behind prop.test(), to the Rao score test of the same logistic model. Wald, score, and likelihood-ratio statistics agree asymptotically. The pooled statistic is the score test. Several grouping factors are an analysis of deviance for proportions, the GLM form of the deviance partition. A numeric covariate beside the group is the analysis-of-covariance analogue.

Part I: unpooled Wald matches logistic AME; part II: pooled prop.test matches Rao score. Several groups → deviance; numeric covariate → ANCOVA analogue links on slide.

Going further. For one parameter with log-likelihood \(\ell\), the Wald statistic uses the curvature at \(\hat\beta\), the score statistic the slope and curvature at the null value, and the likelihood-ratio statistic \(2\{\ell(\hat\beta)-\ell(\beta_0)\}\). Under the null all three are asymptotically \(\chi^2_1\), and they agree to first order under local alternatives; in finite samples they differ. The Wald version is the least invariant: it changes with the parameterisation, and for a logit it can lose power as \(|\hat\beta|\) grows (Hauck and Donner, 1977, Journal of the American Statistical Association). prop.test() applies Yates’s continuity correction by default; with correct = FALSE its \(X^2\) is the square of the pooled \(z\). Agresti sets out the three tests for GLMs (Agresti 2015).

One model, every test of proportions

A logistic regression, with its conditional, ordinal, and GEE friends, reproduces the classic tests of proportions and extends them with covariates. The worked list is the companion page.

Conclusion of the logistic block. Read the map clockwise from the blue box. What it replicates:

  • Wald’s \(z\) for two proportions with unpooled standard errors, via estimated marginal means on the probability scale, or the average marginal effect of the binary group indicator
  • Rao’s score \(z\) with pooled standard errors, which is prop.test(): anova(m, test = "Rao")
  • the \(\chi^2\) test for two or more proportions
  • an ANOVA-like joint test for several categorical predictors, and ANCOVA with numeric covariates
  • Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel for stratified or matched data, via conditional logistic regression
  • Breslow–Day for homogeneous odds ratios, via the Rao test of the interaction term
  • Cochran–Armitage for a trend in ordered proportions
  • McNemar and Cochran’s \(Q\) for paired proportions, and Friedman, via GEE with an exchangeable working correlation
  • Mann–Whitney–Wilcoxon and Kruskal–Wallis via ordinal logistic regression, and the paired Wilcoxon via GEE

The point is not to retire the named tests but to see them as special cases of one model, which can then take a covariate, a cluster, or an interaction.

Going further. The equivalences are exact for the score statistics and asymptotic for the Wald and likelihood-ratio versions: the Mantel–Haenszel statistic is the score test of the common log odds ratio in the conditional likelihood, and the Cochran–Armitage statistic is the score test of the linear term in a logistic model with scores for the ordered levels (Agresti, Categorical Data Analysis, chapters 3 and 6). The ordinal model’s link to Mann–Whitney is through the concordance probability: the proportional-odds coefficient for a binary group is a monotone function of \(P(Y_B > Y_A) + \tfrac12 P(Y_B = Y_A)\), close to \(\exp(\beta)^{0.66}/(1 + \exp(\beta)^{0.66})\) under that model (Harrell, Regression Modeling Strategies).

The fitting criterion is cross-entropy

Denis Burakov, in the chapter embedded below, starts from Fisher’s binomial likelihood and writes the average negative log-likelihood. For \(y_i \in \{0,1\}\) and fitted probabilities \(\hat\pi_i\),

\[ -\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[y_i\log\hat\pi_i + (1-y_i)\log(1-\hat\pi_i)\right]. \]

The factor \(1/n\) does not move the maximiser. This is the log loss, also called cross-entropy. In his table, \(y = 1\) and \(\hat\pi = 0.19\) costs \(-\log 0.19 = 1.66\); \(\hat\pi = 0.79\) costs \(0.23\). With an intercept in the model, the fitted probabilities add up to the number of observed events.

Burakov log loss: \(-\frac1n\sum[y\log\hat\pi+(1-y)\log(1-\hat\pi)]\). Table row \(\hat\pi=0.19\) costs 1.66 versus 0.79 costs 0.23. Intercept-only fitted probs sum to event count.

Going further. For 0/1 data the saturated log-likelihood is 0, so the binomial deviance is exactly \(2n\) times this average log loss: minimising cross-entropy and maximising the likelihood are the same fit. The log loss is a strictly proper scoring rule: its expectation under the true \(\pi\) is minimised only by reporting \(\hat\pi = \pi\) (Gneiting and Raftery, 2007, Journal of the American Statistical Association). The intercept property is one row of the canonical-link score equations \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{y}-\hat{\boldsymbol\pi}) = \mathbf{0}\), so the fit is also calibrated in the mean within every level of a categorical covariate. The loss is unbounded as \(\hat\pi_i \to 0\) for an event, which is why one confident mistake can dominate the average.

The logit, rather than the probit

Burakov quotes Berkson’s reasons for the logistic curve over the probit: a closed-form derivative, fits that on Berkson’s data were as close as the probit or closer, and coefficients that exponentiate to odds ratios. A probit coefficient is a change in a standard normal deviate.

Angelino and Marcone add the complementary log-log link, \(\log\{-\log(1-\pi)\} = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}\), for a curve that is asymmetric about \(1/2\). They report that probit and complementary log-log fits are often close to the logit. The link on these slides stays the logit, so that \(\beta_1\) remains a change in log odds.

Berkson reasons on slide: derivative, fit quality, odds ratios. Complementary log-log for asymmetry at 1/2—probit and cloglog often close to logit. Deck stays logit for \(e^{\beta_1}\) reading.

Going further. All three links come from a latent continuous \(Y^* = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon\) with \(Y = 1\) when \(Y^* > 0\): a logistic \(\varepsilon\) gives the logit, a normal one the probit, an extreme-value one the complementary log-log. The standard logistic has standard deviation about 1.81, so logit coefficients are roughly 1.6 to 1.8 times probit ones (Amemiya, 1981, Journal of Economic Literature). The complementary log-log has a direct reading for detection: if the hidden count is Poisson with \(\log\mu = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta\), then \(\Pr(Y > 0) = 1 - e^{-\mu}\) and \(\log\{-\log(1-\pi)\} = \log\mu\), so a library-size offset keeps its meaning and \(\exp(\beta_1)\) is a rate ratio. Near \(\pi = 1/2\) the links are close; they separate in the tails, where the data are usually thin.

One-hot coding is a contrast with a reference

Burakov’s worked table has 700 borrowers, 100 defaults. High delinquency: 80 defaults out of 180. Low delinquency: 20 out of 520. The model is \(\mathrm{logit}(\pi) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x\), with \(x = 1\) for high delinquency.

The intercept \(\hat\beta_0 = -3.219\) is the log-odds in the reference group, a probability of \(0.0385\). The slope \(\hat\beta_1 = 2.996\) multiplies the odds by \(e^{2.996} = 20\). One category is absorbed into the intercept. The coefficient is the contrast with that category.

700 borrowers, 100 defaults; high delinquency \(\hat\beta_0=-3.219\) → \(p=0.0385\); \(\hat\beta_1=2.996\) → odds \(\times 20\). One category absorbed into intercept.

Softmax is the logit for \(K\) classes

For \(K\) mutually exclusive classes, \(\sum_k \pi_k = 1\), Burakov writes the softmax

\[ \Pr(Y = k \mid \mathbf{x}) = \frac{\exp(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_k)}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_j)}. \]

Fixing \(\boldsymbol{\beta}_K = \mathbf{0}\) leaves \(K-1\) vectors. Estimating all \(K\) is the unconstrained softmax. The probabilities match. \(K = 2\) with a zero reference class is binary logistic regression.

Display softmax with \(\boldsymbol{\beta}_K=\mathbf{0}\) identification. \(K=2\) with zero reference recovers binary logit. Cross-entropy loss with 1-of-\(K\) indicators on slide.

Going further. The softmax probabilities do not change when the same vector is added to every \(\boldsymbol\beta_k\), so only \(K-1\) contrasts are identified; a reference class, or a penalty, resolves that. The fit is the multinomial logit, a GLM for a multinomial response (nnet::multinom in R). The odds between two classes, \(\pi_k/\pi_{k'} = \exp\{\mathbf{x}^{\top}(\boldsymbol\beta_k - \boldsymbol\beta_{k'})\}\), do not depend on the other classes: this independence of irrelevant alternatives is a strong assumption when two classes are near-substitutes (McFadden, 1974). For ordered categories, McCullagh’s cumulative-logit or proportional-odds model (1980, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B) uses \(K-1\) intercepts and one slope vector.

The chapter, on this slide

Denis Burakov, Logistic Regression: From Maximum Likelihood to Machine Learning, is the source of the loss, the weight-of-evidence arithmetic, and the softmax. The viewer scrolls the chapter.

Download PDF file.

Embedded PDF—students scroll Burakov for loss, WOE, softmax details. One sentence: this is reference material, not a live read-through. Keep timing short unless asked.

A Wald interval is a large-sample interval for \(\pi\)

Burakov builds the interval for a new covariate row \(\mathbf{x}_0\) on the logit, then inverts it. The covariance of \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) is \((\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\), with \(w_i = \hat\pi_i(1-\hat\pi_i)\).

\[ \mathbf{x}_0^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} \pm 1.96\sqrt{\mathbf{x}_0^{\top}\widehat{\mathrm{Cov}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\,\mathbf{x}_0}. \]

The endpoints are symmetric on the logit. After the inverse logit they sit inside \((0,1)\) and they are not symmetric on the probability scale. The justification is the asymptotic normality of the maximum-likelihood estimator. A small sample, or the perfect separation on the checks figure, is outside that justification.

Build on logit with \((\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\); invert to \((0,1)\), asymmetric on \(\pi\). Separation and small \(n\) break asymptotics—link checks figure.

Going further. The interval is the delta method on the link scale, \(\mathrm{Var}(\mathbf{x}_0^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \mathbf{x}_0^{\top}\,\mathrm{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})\,\mathbf{x}_0\); inverting the endpoints is exact because the inverse logit is monotone, which is why the band is built on the logit rather than on \(\pi\). A profile-likelihood interval, the set of values whose likelihood-ratio statistic stays below the \(\chi^2_1\) quantile, is invariant to reparameterisation and behaves better in small samples; confint() on a glm returns it in R (the method moved from MASS into stats in R 4.4.0). Under separation the profile is flat on one side and the interval is half-infinite, which is honest; the Wald interval is finite and wrong. Pawitan’s In All Likelihood develops the comparison.

Hold the other covariates still

With several covariates,

\[ g(\mu) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_1 + \beta_2 x_2 + \cdots \]

\(\exp(\beta_1)\) is the multiplicative change in the mean, or in the odds, for a one-unit step in \(x_1\) with the other covariates held at the same values.

If \(x_2\) is a mediator or a collider, “held still” is a different estimand, not a more adjusted one.

\(\exp(\beta_1)\) with \(x_2,\ldots\) fixed at chosen values. Mediator or collider makes “held still” a different estimand—not “more adjusted is always better”.

Going further. The odds ratio is non-collapsible: adding a covariate \(x_2\) that predicts \(Y\) changes the coefficient of \(x_1\) even when \(x_2\) is independent of \(x_1\), and so is not a confounder, because averaging a logistic curve over \(x_2\) does not give a logistic curve with the same slope (Gail, Wieand and Piantadosi, 1984, Biometrika). The rate ratio under a log link and the risk difference under the identity are collapsible in that case. A conditional and a marginal odds ratio are both legitimate estimands, but they are different numbers, and comparing them across studies with different adjustment sets mixes the two. Greenland, Robins and Pearl (1999, Statistical Science) separate collapsibility from confounding; the mediator and collider cases are the causal-graph arguments of the Bayesian and causal section.

Two tiers of checks for a logit

🔗 The checks figure. A bent log-odds line, a variance-inflation factor above 5, separation, and fewer than ten events per variable are its warnings. Firth’s penalised likelihood is the remedy it names for separation. The next six slides take the checks one at a time.

The LinkedIn post is behind a login wall, so the thresholds are the figure’s own wording: VIF above 5 is a warning and above 10 a red flag; Cook’s distance above 1 is a cause for concern; ten events per variable is a rule of thumb the figure says some recent work relaxes. Angelino and Marcone’s Cook cut for a GLM is about \(4/n\), stated on the previous influence slide.

Going further. Albert and Anderson (1984, Biometrika) showed that the logistic maximum-likelihood estimate exists and is finite only when the two outcome classes overlap: under complete or quasi-complete separation some \(\hat\beta\) runs to \(\pm\infty\), and R reports fitted probabilities of 0 or 1 with huge standard errors. Firth (1993, Biometrika) penalises the log-likelihood by \(\tfrac12\log|\mathbf{I}(\boldsymbol\beta)|\), the Jeffreys prior, which removes the first-order bias and always gives finite estimates; Heinze and Schemper (2002, Statistics in Medicine) proposed it for separation, and logistf implements it with profile penalised-likelihood intervals. The ten-events-per-variable rule comes from simulation studies (Peduzzi and colleagues, 1996); van Smeden and colleagues (2016) argued that no single threshold guarantees good performance.

Logit check 1 · The log-odds are linear in each continuous \(x\)

The model assumes a straight line on the log-odds scale, not on the probability scale.

Fit a spline, splines::ns(x, 3), and compare it with the straight term. If the curve bends, keep the spline.

Card one of the figure. The check is about \(\eta\), not about \(\pi\): an S-shaped probability is exactly what a straight log-odds line gives.

Going further. The Box–Tidwell check adds \(x \log x\) and tests its coefficient; a natural spline with a likelihood-ratio test against the linear term is the more general version. Binned empirical logits, \(\log\{(y_k + 0.5)/(n_k - y_k + 0.5)\}\) against the bin mean of \(x\), give a quick picture. A missed bend biases the slope and also miscalibrates the predicted probabilities at the ends of the \(x\) range.

Logit check 2 · The covariates are not collinear

A variance inflation factor above 5 is a warning; above 10 the coefficients are unstable. car::vif(fit).

Collinearity inflates the standard errors of the coefficients. It does not spoil the predicted probabilities.

Card two. Students often drop a variable because its coefficient flipped sign; collinearity is the usual reason, and the fix depends on the question, not on the VIF alone.

Going further. \(\mathrm{VIF}_k = 1/(1 - R_k^2)\), where \(R_k^2\) comes from regressing \(x_k\) on the other covariates; in a GLM car::vif uses the weighted information matrix. For a factor with several columns the generalised VIF of Fox and Monette (1992, Journal of the American Statistical Association) is raised to the power \(1/(2\,\mathrm{df})\) to be comparable. Centring removes the collinearity between \(x\) and \(x^2\) or an interaction, but not between two distinct covariates.

Logit check 3 · The rows are independent

This is a design question, not a test. Repeated cores, plots, or dates need a random intercept, lme4::glmer(y ~ x + (1 | plot), family = binomial), or a cluster-robust standard error.

Card three. Point back to \(i\) inside \(j\): the number of independent units is the number of plots, not the number of rows.

Going further. A random intercept gives cluster-specific odds ratios; GEE (geepack::geeglm) gives population-averaged ones, which are smaller in absolute value by roughly \(1/\sqrt{1 + 0.346\,\sigma_u^2}\). On the latent scale the intraclass correlation of a logit model is \(\sigma_u^2/(\sigma_u^2 + \pi^2/3)\), because the standard logistic distribution has variance \(\pi^2/3\).

Logit check 4 · No single row drives the fit

Cook’s distance above 1 flags a row to inspect; \(4/n\) is a looser screen. cooks.distance(fit).

Refit without the row. If the conclusion moves, report both fits; do not delete the row silently.

Card four. The question is whether the conclusion depends on one plot, not whether the point looks odd.

Going further. Pregibon (1981, Annals of Statistics) gives one-step approximations to the change in \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) and in the deviance when row \(i\) is deleted, built from the leverage of the weighted hat matrix at convergence and the Pearson residual. In logistic regression a high-leverage row is one with an extreme covariate value and a fitted probability not close to 0 or 1; rows with \(\hat\pi_i\) near 0 or 1 have small weight and therefore small leverage, even far out in \(x\).

Logit check 5 · There are enough events for the covariates

About ten events per variable is a rule of thumb, counted in the rarer outcome.

With fewer, simplify the model, or penalise it. Some recent work relaxes the rule; none removes the need to count.

Card five. Count the rarer of the two outcomes, not the number of rows, and count parameters, not variables: a five-level factor is four.

Going further. The rule comes from simulations by Peduzzi and colleagues (1996, Journal of Clinical Epidemiology); van Smeden and colleagues (2016, BMC Medical Research Methodology) showed that no single threshold controls bias. Riley and colleagues (2019–2020, BMJ and Statistics in Medicine) replaced it with sample-size calculations that target a small optimism in the fit and a precise intercept, implemented in the pmsampsize package. Penalised likelihood (Firth, ridge, lasso) reduces small-sample bias and overfitting but needs its own validation.

Logit check 6 · No separation, and no empty category

A zero cell, an odds ratio in the hundreds, and a huge standard error are the signs of separation.

Collapse a category that has no events, or use Firth’s penalised likelihood, logistf::logistf().

Cards six and seven of the figure: perfect separation, and the “one in the box” category with no events. Both are the same problem, one cell of the table is empty. R warns that fitted probabilities numerically 0 or 1 occurred; do not ignore that warning.

Going further. Albert and Anderson (1984, Biometrika) showed that the maximum-likelihood estimate is finite only when the outcome classes overlap in covariate space. Firth (1993, Biometrika) penalises the log-likelihood by \(\tfrac12\log|\mathbf{I}(\boldsymbol\beta)|\), the Jeffreys prior, which always gives finite estimates; Heinze and Schemper (2002, Statistics in Medicine) proposed it for separation, with profile penalised-likelihood intervals. The detectseparation package checks for separation before the fit by linear programming.

What these three models do not cover

  • a zero-inflated count is the mixture already named, and it sits outside the exponential family
  • a composition is the conditioning on the library size; a log-ratio, after a deliberate treatment of the zeros, is a further model
  • a continuous flux: often a positive continuous distribution, or a Gaussian model on a scale you can defend
  • dependence in space or in time: the design block is next

Quasi-Poisson is a variance fix, not a full likelihood. Use it only if you can say what the interval means. 📄 A joint model for many species is the Poisson log-normal of Chiquet, Mariadassou, and Robin (the paper).

Bullet list: ZI mixture, composition conditioning, continuous flux, space-time design next. Quasi-Poisson variance fix only if you can interpret the interval—closing honesty slide.

Going further. Wedderburn (1974, Biometrika) showed that the estimating equations \(\sum_i (y_i-\mu_i)\,(\partial\mu_i/\partial\boldsymbol\beta)/V(\mu_i) = \mathbf{0}\) need only a mean and a variance function, not a distribution: this quasi-likelihood gives the Poisson estimates with \(\mathrm{Var}(Y) = \phi\mu\) and standard errors scaled by \(\sqrt{\hat\phi}\). With no likelihood there is no AIC; Burnham and Anderson’s QAIC divides the log-likelihood by \(\hat\phi\) as a workaround. The quasi-Poisson weights observations in proportion to \(\mu_i\), the negative binomial by \(\mu_i/(1+\mu_i/\theta)\), so on the same data they can give different estimates, not only different intervals (Ver Hoef and Boveng, 2007). For dependence in space or time, the generalised estimating equations of Liang and Zeger (1986, Biometrika) extend the same idea with a working correlation.

A smooth mean

Smooth \(\mu(x)\), and say what you assumed

📝 StatHarbor Analytics, Smoothing and nonparametric methods, uses “nonparametric” for a curve that is not fixed in advance as a short formula. Complexity may grow with \(n\). The structure that replaces the formula is smoothness, locality, or a penalty.

Assumption

\[ Y = \mu(x) + \varepsilon, \qquad \mathbb{E}(\varepsilon \mid x) = 0. \] The course notes write \(f(x)\) for this \(\mu(x)\). A straight line \(\mu(x) = \mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}\) is efficient when it is close to the curve, and biased when the curve bends.

Angelino and Marcone make the same opening: a linear model is easy to interpret, and the straight line is often an approximation. 📝 Day 1b, beyond linearity.

Start in the assumption callout: \(\mu(x)\) is the object, not a transform of \(Y\). Point at the sentence about the straight line being efficient only when the truth is nearly linear. A common slip is to treat “nonparametric” as “no assumptions”; locality and smoothness are assumptions, just not a single global polynomial degree.

One path, rising flexibility

Left to right: a straight mean, a local polynomial, a bandwidth chosen by GCV, a spline with knots, a spline with a penalty, then one smooth per covariate.

Each step spends a tuning choice: a bandwidth, a set of knots, or a penalty. The last step is how that choice survives more than one covariate.

Walk the Mermaid arrow by arrow; pause on GCV as the first automatic knob, and on GAM as the escape from the curse of dimensionality. Ask which step first stops being a single formula you could write on one line. The map is the spine for the rest of the section.

Flexibility spends variance

At a point \(x_0\), the bias–variance decomposition for the curve is:

\[ \mathbb{E}\big(\hat\mu(x_0) - \mu(x_0)\big)^2 = \mathrm{Var}\big(\hat\mu(x_0)\big) + \mathrm{Bias}\big(\hat\mu(x_0)\big)^2. \]

Predicting a new \(Y\) adds \(\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon \mid x_0)\) on top of that. More flexibility usually cuts the bias and raises the variance. A small bandwidth is the noisy end. A large bandwidth is the oversmoothed end.

Read the displayed decomposition at \(x_0\), then add verbally that irreducible \(\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon \mid x)\) is extra when predicting \(Y\). Students often blame “overfitting” only on wiggly \(\hat\mu\) and forget the noise term. Tie “small \(h\)” and “large span” to the two ends of the trade-off named on the slide.

Going further. For a local linear smoother with bandwidth \(h\) and a twice-differentiable \(\mu\), the leading terms are \(\mathrm{Bias}\big(\hat\mu(x_0)\big) \approx \tfrac{1}{2}h^2\mu''(x_0)\int u^2K(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\) and \(\mathrm{Var}\big(\hat\mu(x_0)\big) \approx \sigma^2(x_0)\int K^2 / \big(n h f(x_0)\big)\), with \(f\) the design density. Balancing \(h^4\) against \(1/(nh)\) gives \(h_{\mathrm{opt}} \propto n^{-1/5}\) and a mean squared error of order \(n^{-4/5}\), slower than the \(n^{-1}\) of a correctly specified parametric model. The constant depends on the unknown \(\mu''\), which is why plug-in rules and cross-validation exist. The expansion assumes \(h \to 0\) and \(nh \to \infty\), an interior \(x_0\), and a smooth \(\mu\); it breaks at a boundary or a kink. Fan and Gijbels, Local Polynomial Modelling and Its Applications (1996), derive it in full.

A kernel is a local bump

Before the regression, the same idea estimates a density. Each observation contributes a bump of width \(h\):

\[ \hat f_h(x) = \frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n} K\!\left(\frac{x_i - x}{h}\right). \]

They name four kernels: Gaussian, Epanechnikov, uniform, and triangular. Bandwidth matters far more than which of those four you pick.

For a Gaussian kernel, Silverman’s rule of thumb is \(\hat h \approx 1.06\,\hat\sigma\, n^{-1/5}\). They say it is often adequate for a roughly unimodal symmetric density, and that it fails for a skewed, multimodal, or heavy-tailed density. The AMISE-optimal bandwidth shrinks as \(n^{-1/5}\): nonparametric rates are slower than a well-specified parametric rate.

Going further. The asymptotic mean integrated squared error is \(\mathrm{AMISE}(h) = \frac{R(K)}{nh} + \frac{h^4}{4}\,\mu_2(K)^2\,R(f'')\), with \(R(g) = \int g^2\) and \(\mu_2(K) = \int u^2 K(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\). Minimising gives \(h^\ast = \big[R(K)/(\mu_2(K)^2 R(f'') n)\big]^{1/5}\); Silverman’s rule is this formula with a Gaussian \(f\) plugged into \(R(f'')\), which is exactly why it oversmooths a bimodal density. The Epanechnikov kernel minimises AMISE over kernels, but the Gaussian loses only about 5% efficiency, which is the formal reason the kernel choice is secondary. Data-driven alternatives are least-squares cross-validation (unbiased but highly variable) and the Sheather–Jones plug-in (bw.SJ() in R), usually the better default. Silverman, Density Estimation for Statistics and Data Analysis (1986), and Wand and Jones, Kernel Smoothing (1995), are the references.

Local weights for \(\mu(x_0)\)

Model

\[ \hat\mu(x_0) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} w_i(x_0)\, Y_i, \qquad \sum_i w_i(x_0) = 1. \] Nadaraya–Watson sets \(w_i\) from \(K((x_i-x_0)/h)\). That fit is a local constant: a weighted average of nearby \(Y_i\).

Near the edge of \(x\) the window is one-sided, so a local constant is pulled inward and the curve flattens. That boundary bias is why the next fit is local linear.

In the model callout, emphasise \(\sum_i w_i(x_0)=1\) so \(\hat\mu\) is a convex combination of neighbours. At the boundary, name the flattening before students decide the biology “drops off”. The next slide is the fix: fit a line in the window instead of a constant.

Going further. Nadaraya–Watson is \(w_i(x_0) = K\big((x_i - x_0)/h\big) / \sum_k K\big((x_k - x_0)/h\big)\), the solution of a weighted least-squares fit of a constant. Its interior bias is \(h^2\mu_2(K)\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu''(x_0) + \mu'(x_0) f'(x_0)/f(x_0)\big)\): the second term, the design bias, depends on how the \(x_i\) are spread, not on the curve alone. At a boundary the bias is of order \(h\) rather than \(h^2\), because the one-sided window has a non-zero first moment. Both defects vanish for the local linear fit, which is “design-adaptive” in the sense of Fan (1992, Journal of the American Statistical Association, “Design-adaptive nonparametric regression”).

Fit a line in the window

At \(x_0\), minimise the weighted residual sum of squares of a line in \((x_i - x_0)\). The fitted value is the local intercept.

  • Degree 0 is the local constant, Nadaraya–Watson.

  • Degree 1 is local linear. The usual default: less boundary bias, still local.

  • Degree 2 is local quadratic.

The SIB course LOESS often uses the quadratic. The two sources agree on the algorithm and differ on the default degree.

Step the three fragments: 0 recovers Nadaraya–Watson, 1 is the usual compromise, 2 is what LOESS may use. Ask why the fitted value is the intercept at \(x_0\) rather than the slope. Mention the SIB versus StatHarbor default only as a software detail, not a statistical fight.

Going further. Local polynomial regression of degree \(p\) solves \(\min_{\boldsymbol\beta} \sum_i K\big((x_i - x_0)/h\big)\big(Y_i - \sum_{r=0}^{p}\beta_r (x_i - x_0)^r\big)^2\), and \(r!\,\hat\beta_r\) estimates the \(r\)-th derivative \(\mu^{(r)}(x_0)\). Fan and Gijbels show that odd degrees are preferable: going from \(p\) even to \(p+1\) removes a bias term without increasing the asymptotic variance, so degree 1 beats degree 0 and degree 3 beats degree 2 for the curve itself. The fitted value is still linear in \(\mathbf{Y}\), \(\hat\mu(x_0) = \mathbf{e}_1^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{Y}\), which is what lets GCV and effective degrees of freedom apply. Higher degree buys lower bias at the price of more variance in sparse regions, so a larger \(h\) is needed. Fan and Gijbels, Local Polynomial Modelling and Its Applications (1996), chapter 3.

LOESS is that regression, with a span

Model

LOESS is local polynomial regression with a nearest-neighbour window. The span is a proportion of the sample, in \((0, 1]\). A typical example is \(0.3\). The window widens where \(x\) is sparse. The usual weight is the tricube kernel.

  • Choose the span, find the nearest neighbours, weight them, fit the local polynomial.

  • The fit does not start from a named formula, and it does not return one.

  • It wants a large, densely sampled \(x\). The usual use is exploratory smoothing of a scatterplot.

Contrast span \(0.3\) with a fixed \(h\): neighbours count, so sparse regions get a wider window. Fragment 2 is the honesty check—no single \(\beta_0+\beta_1 x\) to publish. Fragment 3 sets expectations: LOESS is for looking, not for a table of coefficients without extra work.

Going further. The tricube weight is \(W(u) = (1 - |u|^3)^3\) for \(|u| < 1\), with \(u\) the distance to \(x_0\) divided by the distance to the \(q\)-th nearest neighbour, \(q = \lceil \text{span} \times n \rceil\). A nearest-neighbour bandwidth is a variable bandwidth, \(h(x_0) \propto 1/f(x_0)\) roughly, so the variance is more even across \(x\) than with a fixed \(h\), at the cost of more bias where data are sparse. Because the fit is linear in \(\mathbf{Y}\), R’s loess() reports an “equivalent number of parameters” \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S})\) and approximate \(F\) tests between spans, but these rest on Gaussian errors and the approximation of Cleveland and Grosse. The method is from Cleveland (1979, Journal of the American Statistical Association, “Robust locally weighted regression and smoothing scatterplots”) and Cleveland and Devlin (1988, same journal).

Robust LOESS reweights large residuals

Robust LOESS protects the local fit from a few extreme \(Y_i\).

  • Fit an initial LOESS curve.

  • Compute residuals.

  • Downweight unusually large residuals.

  • Refit with both the proximity weights and those robustness weights.

The span is still chosen separately. The next slide is that choice, for a linear smoother. Robust weights take the fit outside the linear-smoother case their GCV formula assumes.

Walk the four fragments as an iteration students may not see in code. Stress that span tuning is separate from robust reweighting. Flag the last sentence: once robust weights enter, the neat \(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{Y}\) story on the GCV slide is only approximate.

Going further. Cleveland’s robustness step uses the bisquare weight \(\delta_i = B\big(e_i / (6\,\mathrm{MAD})\big)\) with \(B(u) = (1 - u^2)^2\) for \(|u| < 1\) and \(0\) otherwise, where \(e_i\) are the current residuals and MAD is their median absolute deviation. Two or three iterations are the default (lowess() uses iter = 3; loess(family = "symmetric") uses four). This is iteratively reweighted least squares for a local M-estimator, so a point with \(|e_i| > 6\,\mathrm{MAD}\) gets weight zero. The cost is that the smoother matrix now depends on \(\mathbf{Y}\), so trace-based degrees of freedom, GCV, and standard errors are approximations. Cleveland (1979, Journal of the American Statistical Association) is the original source.

GCV chooses the bandwidth

A linear smoother is \(\hat{\mathbf{Y}} = \mathbf{S}\mathbf{Y}\). Leave-one-out cross-validation refits \(n\) times. Generalised cross-validation approximates it:

\[ \mathrm{GCV}(h) = \frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_i\big(Y_i - \hat\mu(x_i)\big)^2}{\big(1 - \mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S})/n\big)^2}. \]

The bandwidth that minimises GCV is their predictive choice. The same criterion, with the penalty weight in place of \(h\), is how they tune a smoothing spline. Kernel choice stays secondary.

Point at \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S})/n\) in the denominator as the complexity penalty. Ask what happens if \(h\) is so small that \(\hat\mu(x_i)\) nearly interpolates. Link forward to splines: \(\lambda\) replaces \(h\) in the same GCV pattern on a later slide.

Going further. For any linear smoother, leave-one-out needs no refitting: \(\mathrm{CV} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i \big((Y_i - \hat\mu(x_i))/(1 - S_{ii})\big)^2\). GCV replaces each \(S_{ii}\) by the average \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S})/n\), which makes the criterion invariant to rotations of the data and cheap to compute (Craven and Wahba, 1979, Numerische Mathematik, “Smoothing noisy data with spline functions”). It is asymptotically optimal for prediction error, but its curve is often flat near the minimum and it undersmooths in a noticeable fraction of samples, especially with autocorrelated errors, which it mistakes for signal. For penalised splines and GAMs, Wood (2011, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B, “Fast stable restricted maximum likelihood and marginal likelihood estimation of semiparametric generalized linear models”) recommends REML for \(\lambda\) instead: it has a sharper optimum and less undersmoothing. For a non-Gaussian \(Y\), the residual sum of squares is replaced by the deviance (UBRE or GCV on the working model).

A global polynomial moves the whole curve

Extend the line by powers of \(x\). A quadratic uses \(x\) and \(x^2\). Stop at degree 3 or 4: higher powers become strange near the ends of \(x\). A global polynomial also means a local change can alter the fit far away.

Lopez-Ayala, Riley, Collins, and Zimmermann add fractional polynomials: powers that need not be whole numbers. The function is still defined on the whole range of \(x\), so a pile of values in one tail can distort the shape everywhere.

Use the end-of-range warning: high-degree terms wiggle where data are thin. Fractional polynomials are still global—one tail can bend the entire curve. This slide motivates knots and penalties next; local methods are not the only fix for global rigidity.

Going further. A degree-2 fractional polynomial is \(\beta_1 x^{p_1} + \beta_2 x^{p_2}\) with powers chosen from \(\{-2, -1, -0.5, 0, 0.5, 1, 2, 3\}\), where \(x^0\) means \(\log x\) and a repeated power \(p_1 = p_2 = p\) gives \(x^p\) and \(x^p \log x\); \(x\) must be positive, so a shift is often needed. Royston and Altman (1994, Applied Statistics, “Regression using fractional polynomials of continuous covariates”) introduced them, and Royston and Sauerbrei’s book Multivariable Model-building (2008) gives the closed-test procedure for choosing the powers. The search over powers is a model selection step, so the nominal degrees of freedom of the final fit understate the true flexibility: the usual fix is to count about two degrees of freedom per chosen power. A high-degree ordinary polynomial is also ill-conditioned, which is why poly() uses an orthogonal basis.

A regression spline spends its flexibility at knots

Model

Knots \(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_K\) cut \(x\) into intervals. A cubic piece is fitted in each, with \(f\), \(f'\), and \(f''\) continuous at the knots. The truncated-power basis is \(\beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \beta_2 x^2 + \beta_3 x^3 + \sum_k \theta_k (x-\xi_k)_+^3\).

The analyst chooses how many knots, and where. A common default is the quantiles of \(x\). After the continuity constraints, each added knot costs about one degree of freedom, and the spline is most flexible where the knots are dense. A step function, a piecewise constant, misses the trend unless the cut-points are real breaks. That is the dichotomisation case.

In the model callout, read the truncated-power basis once, then say software rarely uses it raw. Knot placement is a scientific choice disguised as a default quantile rule. The dichotomisation link is deliberate: cutting \(x\) is a spline with too few pieces and wrong continuity.

Going further. A cubic regression spline with \(K\) interior knots spans a linear space of dimension \(K + 4\), so once the basis is built the fit is ordinary least squares (or a GLM) and all the usual inference applies, conditional on the knots. That conditioning is the weakness: choosing \(K\) or the knot positions from the same data is model selection, and the reported intervals ignore it. Penalised regression splines resolve this by taking a generous basis (say 10–20 knots) and controlling wiggliness with a penalty instead of the knot count; Eilers and Marx (1996, Statistical Science, “Flexible smoothing with B-splines and penalties”) use a difference penalty on adjacent B-spline coefficients, the P-spline. With enough knots, the result is insensitive to their exact number. Ruppert, Wand and Carroll, Semiparametric Regression (2003), is the applied reference.

B-splines span the same curves

The truncated-power basis is clear and numerically unstable. A B-spline basis spans the same spline space with local support: each basis function is zero outside a few intervals. The design matrix is sparse, and the fit is better conditioned. Software prefers this basis. The knots are still chosen by hand. The next smoother removes that choice and penalises roughness instead.

Students who implement splines meet instability before they meet the maths. Local support means a knot move affects only a band of basis functions—good for interpretation and for sparse matrices. Hand-chosen knots remain; the penalty slide removes counting knots as the main tuning problem.

Going further. A cubic B-spline \(B_k(x)\) is non-zero on at most four adjacent knot intervals, non-negative, and the basis sums to one at every \(x\) (partition of unity); it is built by the Cox–de Boor recursion from piecewise constants. The truncated powers \((x - \xi_k)_+^3\) are almost collinear when knots are close, so \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\) has a huge condition number; the B-spline basis spans exactly the same space with a banded \(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\), so the fitted curve is identical in exact arithmetic and much more stable in floating point. In R, splines::bs() gives this basis and splines::ns() the natural-spline version. De Boor, A Practical Guide to Splines (1978, revised 2001), is the standard reference.

A penalty replaces the knot count

Model
