\[ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_i - \mu(x_i)\big)^2 + \lambda \int \big(\mu''(t)\big)^2\,\mathrm{d}t. \] The first term is fidelity. The integral is curvature. Here \(\lambda\) is the penalty weight, not a Poisson rate and not a relative intensity. Large \(\lambda\) forces a smooth curve. Small \(\lambda\) allows a wiggly one. A straight line has \(\mu'' = 0\), so the penalty does not forbid a bend. It discourages a bend the data do not support.

Read \(\lambda\) carefully against Poisson notation from the previous chapter. Large \(\lambda\) shrinks curvature; small \(\lambda\) chases the data. The straight-line remark prevents the myth that penalised splines cannot be linear—they can, when the data justify it.

Going further. The penalty has a Bayesian reading. Writing the curve in a basis, \(\mu = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta\), the penalty is \(\lambda\,\boldsymbol\beta^{\top}\boldsymbol\Omega\boldsymbol\beta\), and minimising the penalised criterion is the same as finding the posterior mode under the improper Gaussian prior \(\boldsymbol\beta \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \sigma^2 \boldsymbol\Omega^{-}/\lambda)\): \(\lambda\) is a ratio of noise variance to prior “wiggliness” variance. Kimeldorf and Wahba (1970, Annals of Mathematical Statistics) proved the smoothing spline is the posterior mean under an integrated Wiener-process prior, and Wahba (1983, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B, “Bayesian confidence intervals for the cross-validated smoothing spline”) built the intervals mgcv still uses. The same equivalence writes the spline as a mixed model: the null space of the penalty (the straight line) is fixed, the penalised part is a random effect, and \(\lambda\) becomes a variance ratio that REML can estimate. That is the bridge to the design section’s variance components.

The solution is a natural cubic spline

Theorem

The minimiser, over a suitable class of functions, is a natural cubic spline with a knot at every observed \(x_i\).

The same properties hold: piecewise cubic, knots at the distinct \(x_i\), continuous first and second derivatives, and linear beyond the extreme knots. An infinite-dimensional penalty problem becomes a finite spline. In matrix form it is penalised least squares, the ridge pattern \((\mathbf{N}^{\top}\mathbf{N} + \lambda\boldsymbol{\Omega})^{-1}\mathbf{N}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}\).

Effective degrees of freedom are \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S}_\lambda)\). As \(\lambda \to \infty\) the fit approaches a straight line. As \(\lambda \to 0\) it approaches interpolation. GCV, with \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S}_\lambda)\) in the denominator, chooses \(\lambda\).

The theorem is the bridge from calculus to computation: one knot per distinct \(x_i\), yet a finite solve. Walk \(\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S}_\lambda)\) as effective df and tie \(\lambda\to\infty\) and \(\lambda\to 0\) to the bias–variance slide. GCV reuses the same trace in the denominator as for kernel smoothers.

Going further. The theorem (Reinsch, 1967; stated in Green and Silverman, Nonparametric Regression and Generalized Linear Models, 1994, chapter 2) holds over the Sobolev space of functions with square-integrable second derivative, for at least three distinct \(x_i\). With \(\mathbf{S}_\lambda = \mathbf{N}(\mathbf{N}^{\top}\mathbf{N} + \lambda\boldsymbol\Omega)^{-1}\mathbf{N}^{\top}\), the Demmler–Reinsch eigen-decomposition gives \(\mathbf{S}_\lambda = \sum_k \mathbf{u}_k\mathbf{u}_k^{\top}/(1 + \lambda d_k)\), so \(\mathrm{edf} = \sum_k 1/(1 + \lambda d_k)\): each eigenvector is shrunk, the two with \(d_k = 0\) (constant and line) never, and the wiggliest the most. That is why edf is not an integer and why it lies between 2 and \(n\). Other definitions exist, \(2\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S}) - \mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{S}^{\top})\) for the residual degrees of freedom among them; they coincide for a projection and differ for a smoother. Hastie and Tibshirani, Generalized Additive Models (1990), chapter 3, compares them.

Restricted cubics are the applied form of that constraint

The BMJ guide calls the natural spline a restricted cubic spline: cubics between the inner knots, and a straight line before the first knot and after the last. The tails, where points are few and outliers bite, stay stable. With \(k\) knots the model spends \(k-1\) degrees of freedom. Three knots, their usual start, sit at the 10th, 50th, and 90th percentiles of \(x\). Three to five knots is the range they recommend, and AIC can choose the number. The exact quantile locations rarely change the curve.

Coefficients of a spline are not the summary. The partial-effect plot is.

Translate “natural” to straight tails beyond the outer knots—where extrapolation would otherwise explode. Three knots at 10th, 50th, 90th percentiles are a concrete default to quote. End on the BMJ line: report partial effects, not raw \(\theta_k\) from the basis.

Going further. Linearity beyond the boundary knots means \(\mu'' = \mu''' = 0\) there, which removes four parameters from the unrestricted cubic spline with \(k\) knots (\(k + 4\) parameters including the intercept), leaving \(k\), that is an intercept plus \(k - 1\) slopes. Stone and Koo (1985, Proceedings of the Berkeley Conference in Honor of Jerzy Neyman and Jack Kiefer) proposed the restriction; Harrell’s Regression Modeling Strategies (2nd edition, 2015, chapter 2) gives the default knot quantiles for \(k = 3\) to \(7\) and the rms::rcs() parametrisation. A test of linearity is the joint Wald or likelihood-ratio test that the \(k - 2\) non-linear coefficients are zero, and it is valid only if \(k\) was fixed before looking at the data. For a GLM the curve is linear on the link scale in the tails, not on the scale of \(Y\).

The curve can rise twice

On the meningitis data, CSF glucose against bacterial meningitis: a straight line, and a cut at 62 mg/dL, both miss the second rise at high glucose that the three-knot restricted cubic shows.

Assumption

Do not categorise a continuous covariate, and do not assume a straight line, whether the aim is to predict, explain, or describe. Spend degrees of freedom first on the covariates that matter. Choosing the shape by eye, before the fit, spends phantom degrees of freedom and makes the interval too narrow.

Point at 62 mg/dL as the failed dichotomy, then at the second rise only the spline catches. The assumption callout is the policy slide: eyeballing the shape before fitting steals df and narrows intervals dishonestly. About two minutes if you discuss phantom df.

Going further. “Phantom degrees of freedom” is Harrell’s name, in Regression Modeling Strategies, for the parameters a data-driven choice examined and then discarded: picking a cut-point, a transformation, or a knot count after looking at \(Y\). The final model reports only the survivors, so its residual variance, standard errors and \(P\) values are computed as if the shape had been fixed in advance; simulation shows type I error well above nominal. For an optimised cut-point the problem is classical: the minimum \(P\) value over cut-points needs a correction (Altman, Lausen, Sauerbrei and Schumacher, 1994, Journal of the National Cancer Institute, “Dangers of using ‘optimal’ cutpoints in the evaluation of prognostic factors”). The honest alternatives are to pre-specify the number of knots, to penalise and estimate \(\lambda\) as part of the fit, or to bootstrap the whole selection procedure.

A neighbourhood empties as dimension grows

Local methods need nearby observations. As the number of covariates grows, those neighbourhoods empty, and “local” stops meaning a small window. Naive multivariate smoothing breaks down. The practical compromise is an additive mean: one smooth per covariate, estimated as a one-dimensional problem.

Draw an empty ball in \(p\) dimensions with fixed neighbour count—intuition beats formula here. Additive structure is a modelling assumption, not a free lunch: no interactions unless you add them. The GAM slide is the constructive response named on this slide.

Going further. To capture a fraction \(r\) of uniformly spread data with a hypercube neighbourhood in \(p\) dimensions, the edge must be \(r^{1/p}\) of the range: 10% of the data needs 46% of each axis when \(p = 3\) and 79% when \(p = 10\), so “local” is no longer local. Stone (1982, Annals of Statistics, “Optimal global rates of convergence for nonparametric regression”) showed the best achievable rate for a twice-differentiable \(\mu\) is \(n^{-4/(4+p)}\) for the mean squared error, so the sample needed for a given accuracy grows exponentially in \(p\). An additive model restores the one-dimensional rate \(n^{-4/5}\) for each component, whatever \(p\), because each \(s_j\) is estimated from one coordinate. The price is bias whenever the true \(\mu\) has interactions, which can be added back as low-dimensional tensor-product smooths. Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman, The Elements of Statistical Learning (2009), section 2.5, gives the geometry.

Additive smooths

A GAM is an additive smooth on the link

Model

\[ g(\mu) = \beta_0 + s_1(x_1) + \cdots + s_p(x_p), \qquad \mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid \mathbf{x}). \] A logistic GAM uses the logit. A Poisson GAM uses the log. Each \(s_j\) is a one-dimensional smoother. Backfitting cycles through partial residuals until the smooths settle.

Because the mean is a sum, each \(\hat s_j\) has its own plot: a line, a threshold, a saturation, a U-shape. That plot is the component, not a black-box importance score. The BMJ guide names mgcv for this fit, beside rms for the restricted cubic, and treats GAMs and LOESS as the alternatives to fractional polynomials and splines.

Read \(g(\mu)\) first so Poisson and logistic examples slot in from the GLM hour. Each \(\hat s_j\) plot is the estimand for that covariate, holding others in the partial-residual sense. Mention mgcv and rms as the two R cultures named on the slide.

Going further. Modern GAM software does not backfit: mgcv writes each \(s_j\) in a penalised regression-spline basis, absorbs an identifiability constraint \(\sum_i s_j(x_{ij}) = 0\), and maximises the penalised likelihood by penalised iteratively reweighted least squares, with the vector of smoothing parameters chosen by REML or marginal likelihood (Wood, Generalized Additive Models: An Introduction with R, 2nd edition, 2017). Pointwise intervals come from the Bayesian posterior covariance \((\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X} + \mathbf{S}_\lambda)^{-1}\phi\), here with \(\mathbf{S}_\lambda\) the summed penalty matrices; Marra and Wood (2012, Scandinavian Journal of Statistics) show they have close to nominal coverage averaged across the function, not at every \(x\). The \(P\) values in summary.gam() are approximate Wald tests with a reference distribution on the edf (Wood, 2013, Biometrika, “On p-values for smooth components of an extended generalized additive model”) and ignore the uncertainty in \(\lambda\), so they tend to be somewhat too small. Concurvity, the smooth analogue of collinearity, arises when one \(s_j(x_j)\) can be well approximated by the others; mgcv::concurvity() reports it, and high values make each component unstable even when the sum is well estimated.

Which smoother for which task

  • Exploratory visualisation: LOESS.

  • Density estimation: kernel density estimation.

  • A stable smooth regression in one covariate: smoothing splines.

  • Moderate flexibility with the basis written down: regression splines, including restricted cubics and B-splines.

  • Several covariates, with a plot per covariate: a generalised additive model.

Step the five fragments and ask the room to match a CA-SYS or SPRUCE exploratory plot to item 1 versus item 5. KDE is easy to forget because regression stole the hour. Item 4 is when you need a written basis for simulation or reporting.

Experimental design

🧮 Four blocks: contrasts in R, post-hoc tests and multiple testing, experimental designs, then mixed models in lme4. A short application closes each block. The worked answer is SPRUCE, except for the contrasts, which use CA-SYS.

1 · Contrasts in R

Treatment contrasts use one reference

Larmarange, chapter 26. For an unordered factor, R’s default is contr.treatment. A factor with \(n\) levels becomes \(n-1\) columns. The reference level sits in the intercept.

In the marker ~ grade example the intercept is \(1.0669\) and the grade II coefficient is \(-0.3864\). The mean at grade II is the sum:

\[ 1.0669 + (-0.3864) = 0.6805. \]

contr.treatment(n, base =) and contr.SAS() move which level is the reference.

Walk the arithmetic on the slide: intercept \(1.0669\) plus grade II coefficient \(-0.3864\) gives \(0.6805\). Emphasise \(n-1\) columns and one reference in the intercept. Students confuse “coefficient for II” with “mean at II” unless you do the sum aloud. contr.treatment and contr.SAS only move the reference level.

Going further. A factor with \(L\) levels and an intercept gives a cell-means model \(\mu_\ell\) with only \(L\) estimable means but \(L + 1\) parameters in the overparametrised form \(\beta_0 + \alpha_\ell\); a contrast coding is a choice of \(L - 1\) columns that removes the redundancy. The coding matrix \(\mathbf{C}\) (what contrasts() shows) is not the hypothesis matrix: the coefficients estimate the rows of the generalised inverse of \([\mathbf{1}\;\mathbf{C}]\), so reading a coefficient requires inverting the coding (Schad, Vasishth, Hohenstein and Kliegl, 2020, Journal of Memory and Language, “How to capitalize on a priori contrasts in linear (mixed) models: a tutorial”). With treatment coding the hypothesis matrix is simple, \(\beta_\ell = \mu_\ell - \mu_1\), which is why it reads well. In a model with an interaction, the “main effect” of another factor under treatment coding is its effect at the reference level only, a frequent misreading of type III tables. Any estimable contrast \(\sum_\ell c_\ell\mu_\ell\) with \(\sum_\ell c_\ell = 0\) can be obtained afterwards from any coding, for example with emmeans.

Sum contrasts use the mean of the level means

contr.sum compares each level with the unweighted mean of the level means. The last level is coded \(-1\), so one factor’s coefficients sum to zero and the intercept is that grand mean.

The grade level that was coded \(-1\) has deviation \(-(0.1525 - 0.2339) = 0.0814\), which is also \(0.9958 - 0.9144\).

Fitted values, residual sum of squares, and AIC match the treatment-contrast fit. What changes is the reading of each coefficient.

contr.sum centres on the unweighted mean of level means; the \(-1\) coding line explains the \(0.0814\) deviation. Stress that predictions do not move—only the sentences attached to each \(\hat\beta\). Ask which contrast you would use if the scientific question is “grade II versus average grade”.

Going further. Sum coding gives \(\beta_0 = \frac{1}{L}\sum_\ell \mu_\ell\) and \(\beta_\ell = \mu_\ell - \beta_0\) for \(\ell < L\), with the last deviation recovered as \(-\sum_{\ell<L}\beta_\ell\). The grand mean is unweighted, so with unequal group sizes it differs from the overall sample mean of \(Y\); a weighted-effect coding exists when the population mean is the target. Sum coding matters most with interactions: centred codings make the main effects in a factorial model average effects over the other factor’s levels, which is what type III sums of squares assume, whereas treatment coding makes them simple effects at a reference level. The two parametrisations span the same column space, so fitted values, residuals and likelihood are identical, exactly as the slide says; only the hypotheses tested by individual coefficients change.

An ordered factor can carry a trend

Further codings in the same chapter.

  • MASS::contr.sdif compares each level with the one before it. For grade, II minus I is \(-0.39\), the same gap as the treatment contrast against grade I.

  • contr.helmert compares each new level with the mean of the earlier levels. Keep it for a question that is exactly that comparison.

  • contr.poly is the default for an ordered factor. The columns are a linear, a quadratic, and a cubic trend. That is the coding for ordered time or dose when the question is the shape, including a bend. The intercept is again the mean of the level means.

Step three fragments in order: successive differences (contr.sdif), Helmert versus earlier mean, then polynomial trends for ordered dose or time. Tie contr.sdif’s \(-0.39\) back to the treatment contrast against grade I. Pick the coding to match the estimand, not R’s default habit.

Going further. contr.poly returns orthogonal polynomial contrasts: for \(L\) equally spaced levels, columns of degree \(1\) to \(L - 1\), each orthogonal to the others and to the intercept, scaled to unit length. With balanced data the linear, quadratic and cubic sums of squares then partition the factor’s sum of squares exactly, so a lack-of-fit test of linearity is the joint test of the degree-2-and-higher columns. The default assumes equal spacing; for unequally spaced doses pass the actual values, contr.poly(L, scores = ...), or the trend coefficients describe the wrong shape. Helmert contrasts are also mutually orthogonal under balance, but successive differences are not, so their coefficients are correlated. Because a coefficient’s meaning depends on scaling, report the fitted level means or the trend in units of \(x\) rather than the raw .L and .Q coefficients.

Eight minutes: which contrast? 📝

Pick one factor in your study, or system in CA-SYS, or warming in SPRUCE.

  • How many levels?
  • What is the reference, if there is one?
  • Is the question “each level against a baseline”, “each level against the mean”, or “the shape along an order”?

Eight minutes. The next slide is the CA-SYS reading. Do not hint at contr.sum until they have written a question in words.

One reading: CA-SYS systems

system has four levels, SD1, SD2, TS1, TS2. They are not an order.

  • Against a baseline, SD1 in the intercept: three coefficients, SD2, TS1, and TS2. That is contr.treatment
  • Against the mean of the four systems: contr.sum. The coefficients add to zero
  • Tillage, no-till against tilled, is a different contrast. It collapses SD1 with SD2, and TS1 with TS2. It is not one of the three system coefficients

Warming at SPRUCE is the ordered case: five steps, so a linear trend is the first column of contr.poly. The system factor is the one that needs a reference chosen on purpose.

Do the SD1 arithmetic only if someone asks. The point is that “the TS2 coefficient” is not “the mean at TS2” until you add the intercept. Mention the SPRUCE polynomial in one sentence and stop.

2 · Post-hoc tests and multiple testing

Contrasts from the model you fitted

When the groups and the covariates are in one model, the comparisons are contrasts from that fit. Heiss separates two averages. An average marginal effect averages the contrast over the observed rows. A marginal effect at the mean evaluates it at the average covariate. marginaleffects defaults to the first. emmeans defaults to the second. They differ when the model is not linear.

Reporting several of those contrasts is still a family. The adjustment is simultaneous inference for the family you planned, not a correction chosen after the significant rows appear.

Heiss: average marginal effect versus effect at mean; marginaleffects vs emmeans defaults. Several contrasts still form a family needing simultaneous inference.

Going further. For an inverse link \(h = g^{-1}\), the average marginal effect is \(n^{-1}\sum_i \partial h(\mathbf{x}_i^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})/\partial x_1\) and the marginal effect at the mean is \(\partial h(\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})/\partial x_1\); by Jensen’s inequality they differ unless \(h\) is linear over the range of the data. Both are smooth functions of \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\), so their standard errors come from the delta method, which is what both packages compute. emmeans averages over a reference grid with equal weight per level, which is not the observed covariate distribution in an unbalanced design; say which grid you used. For a logit the derivative is \(\beta_1\pi(1-\pi)\), at most \(\beta_1/4\) at \(\pi = 1/2\): the divide-by-four rule of Gelman and Hill’s Data Analysis Using Regression and Multilevel/Hierarchical Models.

A row of coefficients is a family

\[ H_{0j}: \beta_j = 0, \quad j = 1, \ldots, p. \]

One row per coefficient hides that family. The matching statement is a joint region, or simultaneous intervals: Bonferroni intervals for a Bonferroni test, Tukey or Scheffé intervals for those contrast families. A logistic, Poisson, or negative-binomial model has the same family. The criterion does not change because the distribution did.

StatHarbor Analytics, Multiple Testing Correction (2026), from the slide “Multiplicity in Linear Models”. Testing and interval estimation are dual: a family-wise procedure induces simultaneous intervals with joint coverage at least \(1-\alpha\). Pointwise bands on a curve are the individual-test object; a simultaneous band is the corrected one.

Going further. Bonferroni controls the family-wise error rate at \(\alpha\) under any dependence, by the union bound; Holm’s step-down version (1979, Scandinavian Journal of Statistics) rejects at least as much with the same guarantee. Both are shortcuts of the closed-testing principle of Marcus, Peritz and Gabriel (1976, Biometrika): reject \(H_{0j}\) only when every intersection hypothesis that contains it is rejected at level \(\alpha\). For the coefficients of one fitted model, \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) is approximately multivariate normal, so the exact critical value is a quantile of the maximum of correlated \(|z|\) statistics; Hothorn, Bretz and Westfall (2008, Biometrical Journal) implement this in multcomp::glht for lm, glm and mixed models alike (Bretz et al. 2016).

The follow-up matches the family

After a global analysis of variance, name the contrasts before you test them.

  • All pairwise means: Tukey.

  • Each treatment against a control: Dunnett.

  • Arbitrary contrasts: Scheffé, or simultaneous inference from the fitted model.

The correction belongs to that family. It is not a fee paid because several means exist.

Tukey all pairs, Dunnett versus control, Scheffé arbitrary—match procedure to planned family. Correction is not a tariff for having many means.

Going further. Tukey’s procedure uses the studentised range, the law of the largest standardised difference among \(K\) means, and is exact for a balanced design with equal variances; the Tukey–Kramer version for unequal group sizes is conservative. Dunnett’s uses the joint multivariate-\(t\) law of the \(K-1\) comparisons with the control, which share the control mean and are positively correlated, so its critical value is smaller than Tukey’s. Scheffé’s critical value, \(\sqrt{(K-1)F_{K-1,\nu}(1-\alpha)}\) with \(\nu\) residual degrees of freedom, covers every contrast at once, including those chosen after looking, and is therefore the widest. All three are cases of simultaneous inference on linear functions of \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) (Bretz et al. 2016).

Genomics is a large exploratory family

A screen of thousands of genes, tens of thousands of proteins, or millions of variants is a different family. Missing a true signal is costly, and a controlled share of false leads is acceptable.

That is a false-discovery rate: Benjamini–Hochberg, an adaptive version, or a \(q\)-value. A \(q\)-value is not a posterior probability that the finding is true. A family-wise guarantee of no false claim at all spends power the screen cannot afford.

Thousands of features: FDR (BH, adaptive, \(q\)-value) not FWER. \(q\) is not posterior probability of truth—power trade-off on slide.

Going further. With \(V\) false rejections among \(R\), the false-discovery rate is \(\mathbb{E}\{V/\max(R,1)\}\). Benjamini and Hochberg (1995, Journal of the Royal Statistical Society, Series B) sort the \(m\) \(p\)-values and reject the \(k\) smallest, for the largest \(k\) with \(p_{(k)} \leq k\alpha/m\); this controls the rate at \(m_0\alpha/m \leq \alpha\) under independence and under positive regression dependence (Benjamini and Yekutieli, 2001, Annals of Statistics), and under any dependence if \(\alpha\) is divided by \(\sum_{k=1}^{m} 1/k\). Adaptive versions estimate the null proportion \(m_0/m\) and gain power when many features are non-null. Storey’s \(q\)-value (2003, Annals of Statistics) is the smallest false-discovery rate at which a feature would be called: a property of a list, not of one feature (Farcomeni 2008).

One false claim, or some false signal

  • A family-wise error rate is the right target when even one false claim is costly.

  • A false-discovery rate is the right target when the aim is broad discovery and some controlled false signal is acceptable.

  • Dependence, a hierarchy, or a structured set of contrasts often recovers power that a generic worst-case correction spends.

A procedure that controls the family-wise rate also controls the false-discovery rate, usually at that cost in power.

Because \(V \le R\) whenever \(R > 0\), the false-discovery rate is at most the family-wise error rate. Holm rejects every hypothesis Bonferroni rejects, for the same family and the same level. Questions before choosing: what is the family, is the setting confirmatory or exploratory, what false-positive burden is tolerable, and are the tests independent, positively dependent, or structured? Report the family, the criterion, the procedure, and the adjusted summary. Do not choose the correction after seeing which rows were small.

Which procedure for which family

Scenario Family Typical procedure
Primary confirmatory endpoints Small and prespecified Holm, gatekeeping, closed testing
All pairwise means Structured contrasts Tukey HSD
Many genomic features Very large, exploratory Benjamini–Hochberg, adaptive BH, \(q\)-values
Complex dependence Structured and correlated Westfall–Young, or a bootstrap calibration

Define the family and the error criterion before the claims. This grid is from StatHarbor Analytics, Multiple Testing Correction (2026).

Walk the table: confirmatory Holm/gatekeeping; pairwise Tukey; genomics BH; dependence Westfall–Young or bootstrap. StatHarbor 2026 grid—define family before claims.

Two places to go next

(Bretz et al. 2016) develops these procedures as maximum statistics and shows the R implementations. (Farcomeni 2008) reviews the false-discovery proportion, dependence among the statistics, and the genomic case.

Bretz multiple comparisons book for R implementations; Farcomeni review for FDR and dependence. Bibliography slide—thirty seconds unless someone asks for a recommendation.

3 · Experimental designs

The analysis inherits the plan

A mixed model does not repair an undescribed design. Before the formula, name:

  • the unit that received the treatment
  • the unit that was measured
  • what was randomised, blocked, or only observed
  • which rows are repeats of the same unit

Carbon data are often hierarchical, multi-site, and repeated in time.

Open with the headline: the formula cannot fix a plan you never wrote down. Walk the four bullets in order and ask the room to name SPRUCE’s treatment unit (the enclosure) and the observational unit on a flux row (collar by date). The common slip is to treat every depth–date line as an independent replicate; that mistake is what the random effect is meant to encode, not to paper over.

Going further. Hurlbert (1984, Ecological Monographs, “Pseudoreplication and the design of ecological field experiments”) named the error: testing a treatment effect with an error term built from samples that are not independent replicates of the treatment. His taxonomy separates simple pseudoreplication (one unit per treatment, many samples from it), temporal pseudoreplication (repeated dates treated as replicates), and sacrificial pseudoreplication (real replicates pooled before the test). The formal statement is that the correct denominator for a treatment comparison is the variance between experimental units, the stratum in which the treatment was randomised; a mixed model recovers it only if the random-effect structure matches that stratum. Bailey, Design of Comparative Experiments (2008), makes the distinction between experimental unit and observational unit the starting point of every analysis.

A study leaves some conditions free

In a study, conditions that move the outcome are outside the investigator’s control. A field example is the weather, the food, and the predators.

  • In an observational study the variable of interest itself is not assigned. That is where the reminder sits: correlation is not causation.

Field weather is the uncontrolled nuisance example. Step the fragment: the exposure of interest is not assigned, so the causal section’s \(do(x)\) language matters later. SPRUCE is not this slide—it is closer to the controlled-experiment and trial pair.

A trial randomises the assignment

A randomised controlled trial still cannot hold every nuisance fixed. It does control who receives the intervention.

  • With a large enough sample, the nuisances average out, and the contrast can be read as an effect of that assignment.

  • The trial is prospective: the outcome is unknown when the groups are assigned.

Fragment one: randomisation controls assignment, not every nuisance. Fragment two: prospective timing—outcome unknown at allocation. Ask whether warming at SPRUCE is “assigned” in this sense (yes on enclosure). Bridge to the causal deck: randomisation is a design fact, not a covariate trick.

Going further. In potential-outcome notation, each unit has \(Y_i(1)\) and \(Y_i(0)\), and randomisation makes the assignment independent of both, so the difference of group means is unbiased for the average treatment effect without any model for the nuisances. “Average out” is a statement about the randomisation distribution, not about one realised allocation: a given trial can still be imbalanced on a covariate, and adjusting for a pre-specified prognostic covariate then gains precision without bias (Senn, 1994, Statistics in Medicine, “Testing for baseline balance in clinical trials”, argues against testing for imbalance instead). Fisher’s randomisation test uses the assignment mechanism itself as the reference distribution, so its validity needs no normality or independence assumption beyond the design. Imbens and Rubin, Causal Inference for Statistics, Social, and Biomedical Sciences (2015), chapters 5–7, develop the finite-population theory.

A meta-analysis studies the studies

Huber calls a meta-analysis an observational study of earlier experiments or studies. Two motives: a larger effective sample, and the hope that study-level bias averages out across studies.

The hierarchical version, one estimate per site, is fixed effect or random effects.

Meta-analysis is observational at study level: you did not randomise which papers exist. The two motives on the slide are sample size and hope that study-level bias averages out—say both, without overselling the second. Point to the link back to fixed versus random effects for “one estimate per site”.

Going further. With study estimates \(\hat\theta_j\) and within-study variances \(v_j\), the fixed-effect (common-effect) estimate is the inverse-variance mean \(\sum_j w_j\hat\theta_j / \sum_j w_j\) with \(w_j = 1/v_j\); the random-effects model adds \(\theta_j \sim \mathcal{N}(\theta, \tau^2)\) and uses \(w_j = 1/(v_j + \tau^2)\), which flattens the weights and widens the interval. DerSimonian and Laird (1986, Controlled Clinical Trials, “Meta-analysis in clinical trials”) give the moment estimator of \(\tau^2\); REML or Paule–Mandel estimators and the Hartung–Knapp–Sidik–Jonkman interval behave better when the number of studies is small. \(I^2\) describes the share of variability due to heterogeneity, not its size. Borenstein, Hedges, Higgins and Rothstein, Introduction to Meta-Analysis (2009), is the standard text.

Randomise the nuisance you cannot block

When the nuisance is unknown in advance, randomisation is the practical strategy: at a large enough sample, its effect averages out. It also limits unconscious bias, such as extra care for the samples that were hard to obtain.

He points to Senn (2004) for the ways a “better” scheme than simple randomisation can go wrong.

When blocking is impossible, randomisation is the practical defence—both against unknown nuisances and unconscious handling bias. Mention Senn as the caution on fancy schemes without lecturing the chapter. Ask for one carbon-lab nuisance you could randomise but not block (plate position, run order).

Going further. Randomisation does two separate jobs. It removes systematic bias, because any unmeasured covariate is independent of treatment in expectation; and it justifies the error term, because under the randomisation the expected treatment mean square equals the residual mean square when there is no effect (Fisher, The Design of Experiments, 1935). Restricted randomisation (blocking, stratification, minimisation) keeps both properties only if the analysis includes the restriction, which is why a blocked design must have the block in the model. Run order and plate position are classic carriers of drift; a randomised order turns drift into noise, while a systematic order turns it into bias. Senn’s 2004 discussion of “better” allocation schemes (Statistics in Medicine) makes the point that deterministic balancing can break the link between design and analysis.

Balance makes each effect identifiable

A balanced design gives every factor combination the same number of replicates. Each factor’s effect can then be separated. A nuisance that is balanced against the factor of interest is in the same position.

  • An unbalanced plan can be forced by cost. The warning is that the analysis is then on thin ice.

Balanced means equal replication per combination so effects separate. Step the fragment: unbalanced designs are common when money runs out—keep the “thin ice” phrase for the methods section. Do not confuse balance with randomisation; you need both stories in different papers.

Going further. In a linear model \(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}) = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta\), a linear function \(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta\) is estimable when \(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\) lies in the row space of \(\mathbf{X}\); an empty cell, as in the right-hand grid, makes the corresponding interaction non-estimable. With equal replication the columns for different factors are orthogonal, so the sums of squares decompose uniquely and the order of terms does not matter. Unbalanced data break that orthogonality: sequential (type I), partial (type II) and marginal (type III) sums of squares then test different hypotheses, and type III tests on main effects in the presence of an interaction depend on the contrast coding. Searle, Linear Models for Unbalanced Data (1987), is the reference; Agresti covers estimability in Foundations of Linear and Generalized Linear Models (Agresti 2015).

A pair is one block

The left pot is a pair: the nuisance (the pot) is shared, and the treatment is assigned inside it. The right pot lets the side of the pot wear the treatment’s name.

Cover the right pot first. Fisher’s complaint about Darwin was this picture: every cross-pollinated plant on one side. The coin is the randomisation inside the block. Thirty seconds, then the Huber link on the next slide.

Pairing is the smallest block

Block what you can, and randomise what you cannot. A paired design is the simplest block: twins, or the same person before and after.

  • Fisher criticised Darwin for putting every cross-pollinated plant on the same side of the pot. A coin flip for the side would have kept that side from masquerading as the cross.

  • When a natural pair does not exist, a matched design builds pairs on age, sex, or health, and treats one of the pair.

Fisher’s Darwin pot-side story is the vivid warning—blocking is about removing a directional nuisance. Fragment two: matched pairs when nature does not give you twins. Pairing is the smallest block; SPRUCE blocking discussion earlier is the same idea at enclosure scale.

Going further. For a paired comparison, \(\mathrm{Var}(\bar d) = 2\sigma^2(1 - \rho)/n\), where \(\rho\) is the within-pair correlation, against \(2\sigma^2/n\) for two independent groups of the same size. The relative efficiency of the paired design is therefore \(1/(1 - \rho)\): with \(\rho = 0.5\) pairing halves the number of units needed. The price is \(n - 1\) instead of \(2n - 2\) residual degrees of freedom, which matters only when \(n\) is very small or \(\rho\) is close to zero. More generally, the efficiency of a randomised complete block design relative to a completely randomised one is estimated from the block and residual mean squares (Cochran and Cox, Experimental Designs, 1957; Montgomery, Design and Analysis of Experiments, chapter 4). Matching without analysing the pairs throws the gain away, and for a binary \(Y\) the matched analysis (McNemar, conditional logistic) targets a conditional, not a marginal, odds ratio.

Two questions for every factor

📄 Krzywinski, Altman and Blainey, Nested designs, Nature Methods (2014). Their one-line advice: when the noise comes from several levels, analyse the experiment as a nested design.

  • Crossed or nested: crossed when every level of one factor meets every level of the other; nested when each lower level belongs to one upper level, as mouse 3 only ever sits under drug 2
  • Fixed or random: fixed when you chose the levels to compare them and would use them again, such as a drug or a dose; random when the levels are a sample, such as mice or cells, and the target is their variance

A fixed factor is compared against the variance of the level it was applied to.

Two questions, asked of every factor before any formula. Use the drug–mouse–cell example of the next slide: drug is fixed, mouse and cell are random; mice are nested in drug when each mouse receives one drug. The last sentence is the practical one: a drug given to whole mice is compared with mouse-to-mouse variation, not with cell-to-cell variation. That is pseudoreplication seen from the design side.

The paper itself is behind a paywall; the slide uses only its standfirst and its figure of crossed and nested factors, redrawn on the next slide.

Going further. In a balanced two-level nested design with \(a\) drugs, \(b\) mice per drug and \(n\) cells per mouse, the expected mean squares are \(\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{MS}_{\text{drug}}) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2 + n\sigma_{\text{mouse}}^2 + bn\,\theta_{\text{drug}}^2\) and \(\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{MS}_{\text{mouse(drug)}}) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2 + n\sigma_{\text{mouse}}^2\). The drug \(F\) ratio is therefore \(\mathrm{MS}_{\text{drug}}/\mathrm{MS}_{\text{mouse(drug)}}\) on \(a-1\) and \(a(b-1)\) degrees of freedom: more cells per mouse shrink \(\sigma_\varepsilon^2/n\) but leave the mouse term, so the number of mice drives the power. Montgomery, Design and Analysis of Experiments, chapter 14, gives the derivation.

Four ways to arrange drug, mouse and cell

Colour marks a fixed factor, grey a random one. Historical source, kept for reference only: figures/sources/crossed_versus_nested_design.jpg, the figure “Factors may be crossed or nested” in Krzywinski, Altman and Blainey (2014). That file is too small to show at slide size, so the slide uses a TikZ redraw instead.

Walk the four panels left to right, top to bottom. a: three fixed factors, every combination present, a factorial; no random term at all. b: each mouse receives one drug and each cell belongs to one mouse—the drug comparison has as many replicates as mice. c: each mouse receives both drugs, so the mouse acts as a block and the drug contrast is made inside each mouse. d: tissues nested in mice, cells nested in tissues, and the drug assigned to cells, so drug is crossed with the hierarchy at the bottom. Ask where SPRUCE sits: panel b, with enclosure for mouse and warming for drug.

The original is a small published figure; it is redrawn here because the copy available was too small to read. In the original, tissue is a fixed factor (two named tissues compared); that is why it is coloured.

Crossed, nested, blocked, split

Schielzeth and Nakagawa, Figure 1. Solid lines are a partial cross. Dashed lines complete it. The SPRUCE reading of the same picture is crossed, nested, or both. 📄 The paper.

Four panels: crossed, nested, randomised block, split-plot. Point to solid versus dashed lines in the caption—partial versus completed cross. Map split-plot to large-unit versus within-unit factors without re-deriving the cookie-dough story. [#sec-crossed-nested], in the mixed-model block, puts the SPRUCE labels on the same picture.

Ten minutes: name the units 📝

On your own study, or on SPRUCE or CA-SYS, write four lines:

  • the unit that received the treatment, or the exposure
  • the unit that was measured
  • what was assigned, blocked, or only observed
  • which rows are repeats of the same unit

Ten minutes, writing, not talking. If a group has no study of their own, send them to SPRUCE. Do not show the next slide until the time is up.

One reading: SPRUCE

SPRUCE is the designed case, so the words are easier to pin down than in an observational table.

  • Treatment unit: the enclosure. Warming and CO\(_2\) are assigned there
  • Measured unit: a depth on a date, inside that enclosure
  • Assigned: the ten chambered enclosures. Two ambient plots were never given a warming level
  • Repeats: depths and dates that share an enclosure are not new enclosures
If you need… The replicate is…
The warming slope The enclosure, \(n = 10\)
A collar method Collars within an enclosure
A seasonal curve Dates within an enclosure

Read the four bullets, then the table. \(n = 10\) is the line that surprises people who counted depth-date rows. Offer CA-SYS only if a group chose it: plot is \(j\), point is \(i\).

4 · Mixed models

Each layout names its random terms

The design decides which factors get a random intercept. The lowest random level, the cell, is the residual \(\varepsilon\).

Layout Random factors Independent unit Random part in lme4
a Crossed none each culture in the grid none: lm()
b Nested mouse, cell the mouse (1 | mouse)
c Crossed and nested mouse, cell the mouse (1 | mouse), and (1 | mouse:drug) with several cells per mouse and drug
d Nested and crossed mouse, cell the mouse (1 | mouse/tissue), with tissue fixed or random

SPRUCE is layout b with one more level: warming for drug, enclosure for mouse, collar inside the enclosure, and dates as the cells.

Read the “independent unit” column first: in b, c and d it is the mouse, whatever the number of cells. That column is the outer plate of the diagrams that follow. Then the formula column. Layout c is the one people under-specify: if each mouse has several cells under each drug, the drug effect can vary between mice, and (1 | mouse:drug) is that variance; without it the drug test uses the cell-level residual and is too optimistic.

Going further. With \(m\) rows per cluster and intraclass correlation \(\rho = \sigma_u^2/(\sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2)\), the variance of a mean over \(J\) clusters is inflated by the design effect \(1 + (m - 1)\rho\) relative to \(Jm\) independent rows (Kish, Survey Sampling, 1965). The effective sample size is \(Jm / \big(1 + (m-1)\rho\big)\), which tends to \(J/\rho\) as \(m\) grows: adding cells to a mouse cannot buy more information than the mice themselves carry. Even \(\rho = 0.1\) with \(m = 21\) triples the variance. For a treatment assigned to clusters, as in layout b, the test has of the order of \(J\) minus the number of cluster-level parameters degrees of freedom, not \(Jm\).

Crossed, nested, or both

Warming and \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) are crossed on the enclosure. Collars and dates are nested in the enclosure: each inner plate repeats inside the outer one. A technical replicate nested in a core is a further level, and it is rarely the level of inference.

Read the plates from the outside in: warming and \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) are constants on enclosure \(j\); collar \(k\) and date \(i\) are nested inside. Ask where a core technical replicate would hang, and whether inference should live there. Schielzeth Figure 1, in the design block, is the same vocabulary with different ink; layout b of the drug–mouse–cell figure is the same shape.

A random intercept is a distribution on \(u_j\)

Use a Gaussian equation for \(Y_{ij}\) only when \(Y\) can live on that scale. For a count or a detection, the cluster term sits on the link, as in the diagram. Only the outer plate counts as independent replication.

\(u_j\) is a property of the cluster. It is not a covariate you forgot to measure, unless you are willing to say so. Ignoring it is the pitfall repeated rows are not replicates.

Read the plates: \(u_j\) is drawn once per independent unit, and every replicate \(i\) inside that unit shares it. The equation is on the link scale, not on the count scale unless \(Y\) is Gaussian. \(u_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_u^2)\) is a distribution over enclosures, not a missing fixed covariate. If someone proposes “just add enclosure as a factor”, ask whether they have all levels or a sample of bogs—that leads into the next slide’s columns.

Going further. For a Gaussian \(Y\), the random intercept induces a compound-symmetric covariance within a cluster: \(\mathrm{Var}(Y_{ij}) = \sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2\) and \(\mathrm{Cov}(Y_{ij}, Y_{i'j}) = \sigma_u^2\), so the intraclass correlation is \(\rho = \sigma_u^2/(\sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2)\). On a non-identity link the story changes: \(\beta_1\) in a generalised linear mixed model is a conditional (cluster-specific) effect, and the marginal, population-averaged effect is attenuated; for a logit link, roughly \(\beta_1/\sqrt{1 + 0.346\,\sigma_u^2}\) (Zeger, Liang and Albert, 1988, Biometrics). The likelihood integrates over \(u_j\), which has no closed form for non-Gaussian \(Y\); Laplace approximation (glmer() default) or adaptive Gauss–Hermite quadrature (nAGQ > 1) is needed, and both are less accurate with few observations per cluster. Testing \(\sigma_u^2 = 0\) puts the null on the boundary of the parameter space, so the likelihood-ratio statistic follows a 50:50 mixture of \(\chi^2_0\) and \(\chi^2_1\), not \(\chi^2_1\) (Self and Liang, 1987, Journal of the American Statistical Association).

Fixed and random name the target

Fixed

A contrast you want to report, such as the warming slope.

If you already have every level in the population, a fixed indicator for each level is coherent.

Random

A level you want to generalise over, such as enclosures drawn from a bog.

Ten enclosures is a small sample of a variance. \(\hat\sigma_u\) will be noisy.

Partial pooling is the practical reason to bother: an enclosure with two bad weeks borrows strength from the others. The meta-analysis version, one estimate per site, is fixed effect or random effects.

Left column: warming slope is a fixed estimand you report. Right column: enclosures are a random draw from a bog population, and ten of them makes \(\hat\sigma_u\) noisy—quote that line. Partial pooling is the punchline for sparse clusters. The meta-analysis slide, earlier in the hour, is the same idea with one estimate per site.

Going further. In the balanced Gaussian random-intercept model, the predicted cluster effect (the BLUP) is \(\hat u_j = \lambda_j(\bar Y_{\cdot j} - \hat\mu)\) with shrinkage factor \(\lambda_j = \sigma_u^2/(\sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2/m_j)\): a cluster with few rows or noisy rows is pulled harder towards the grand mean. That is partial pooling between the two extremes, no pooling (a fixed effect per cluster, \(\lambda_j = 1\)) and complete pooling (ignore clusters, \(\lambda_j = 0\)). Robinson (1991, Statistical Science, “That BLUP is a good thing: the estimation of random effects”) reviews the theory, and Gelman and Hill, Data Analysis Using Regression and Multilevel/Hierarchical Models (2007), chapter 12, gives the applied reading. With ten clusters \(\hat\sigma_u^2\) is imprecise and can be estimated as zero, a singular fit, in which case the model reduces silently to complete pooling.

Nesting is a property of the experiment

Schielzeth and Nakagawa (Methods in Ecology and Evolution, 2013). A nested factor can be fitted with the syntax of a crossed factor, provided each nested level has a unique label. The design still decides what the variances mean.

The difference that matters is the interaction variance. In a nested experiment it is pooled with the main-effect variance of the nested factor. A crossed experiment can separate the two. The pooling is fixed by which combinations occur, not by the formula.

If a cross would have been feasible and meaningful, the data are nested by design, and the pooling has to be stated. If a cross would be irrelevant, the nesting is natural.

Schielzeth and Nakagawa: unique labels let nested factors use crossed syntax, but the experiment still decides what variances mean. The interaction variance pools with the nested main effect in a nested design; a cross can separate them. Ask whether enclosure-by-collar should be crossed or nested given how SPRUCE was built—the plate diagram earlier in this block is the answer key.

Going further. In a balanced two-way random model with \(a\) levels of \(A\), \(b\) of \(B\) and \(n\) replicates per cell, the expected mean squares are \(\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{MS}_A) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2 + n\sigma_{AB}^2 + bn\sigma_A^2\) and \(\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{MS}_{AB}) = \sigma_\varepsilon^2 + n\sigma_{AB}^2\), so \(\sigma_A^2\) is separable from \(\sigma_{AB}^2\) only because \(B\) is crossed with \(A\). If \(B\) is nested in \(A\), the corresponding mean square estimates \(\sigma_\varepsilon^2 + n(\sigma_{B}^2 + \sigma_{AB}^2)\): the two variances are confounded, which is the pooling Schielzeth and Nakagawa describe. In lme4 syntax, (1 | A/B) expands to (1 | A) + (1 | A:B), and (1 | A) + (1 | B) with uniquely labelled \(B\) levels fits the same model; the syntax cannot recover a variance the design never separated. Searle, Casella and McCulloch, Variance Components (1992), give the full derivations.

Four variances, if the design allows them

A crossed design can estimate each of those four variances. Nested sampling leaves the interaction inside the nested factor’s variance.

Figure 2 flows from two factors and their interaction into trait value, then down to four variance components. In a cross you can estimate interaction variance separately; nested sampling pools it. Students often fit (1|nested) and interpret \(\sigma_u^2\) as “noise” without stating what was pooled.

The four layouts in lme4

One formula per panel. The data frames are placeholders; the point is which term each design needs.

layouts.R

library(lme4)
# a  crossed and all fixed: a factorial, no random term
lm(y ~ drug * dose * diet, data = d_a)
# b  drug given to whole mice, cells inside mice
lmer(y ~ drug + (1 | mouse), data = d_b)
# c  every mouse receives both drugs
lmer(y ~ drug + (1 | mouse) + (1 | mouse:drug), data = d_c)
# d  tissues inside mice, drug assigned to cells within a tissue
lmer(y ~ drug + (1 | mouse/tissue), data = d_d)

In b, give each mouse a unique label: mouse 1 under drug 2 is not mouse 1 under drug 1. lmerTest::lmer() adds Satterthwaite tests; in b the drug test has about the number of mice minus two degrees of freedom.

Read the four calls against the panels. a has no random term because every factor is fixed. b: the drug is a mouse-level covariate, so its standard error is driven by the mouse variance. c: the mouse is a block; the drug contrast is within mouse. d: (1 | mouse/tissue) expands to (1 | mouse) + (1 | mouse:tissue); drug varies within tissue, so it is tested against the cell-level residual, and (drug | mouse) would let the drug effect vary between mice.

The unique-label point is the most common coding error: with labels 1–2 reused under each drug, (1 | mouse) silently crosses mice with drug. Either relabel, or write (1 | drug:mouse).

Going further. In balanced nested data, REML reproduces the classical nested ANOVA: the estimated mouse variance is \((\mathrm{MS}_{\text{mouse(drug)}} - \mathrm{MS}_\varepsilon)/n\), truncated at zero, and the Satterthwaite degrees of freedom for drug equal \(a(b-1)\), the mouse-within-drug count. With unbalanced data the two diverge, and Kenward–Roger (pbkrtest) is the safer small-sample choice. A zero variance estimate gives a singular fit; lme4 warns, and the model then collapses to complete pooling for that level (Bates, Mächler, Bolker and Walker, Journal of Statistical Software, 2015).

ML and REML answer different targets

The practical gap is often small. A likelihood-ratio comparison of two models uses ML.

Question ML REML
Fixed part of the model ✅ More accurate for the fixed part Usable, and usually close
Variance components Usable, and usually close ✅ More accurate for the random variances
Comparing two models ✅ Required for a likelihood-ratio test Switch to ML before the comparison
On a typical fit Often close to REML The usual reporting fit
Angelino and Marcone, September 2026. Their general wisdom on ML and REML.

Use the rendered table: ML for comparing nested models by likelihood ratio; REML for variance components on a typical report. The row “Comparing two models” is the one students forget—switch to ML before the LRT. Angelino and Marcone are the source note at the bottom of the table.

Going further. For a Gaussian linear model with \(p\) fixed effects, ML gives \(\hat\sigma^2 = \mathrm{RSS}/n\), biased downward by the factor \((n - p)/n\) because it ignores the degrees of freedom spent on \(\boldsymbol\beta\); REML maximises the likelihood of \(n - p\) error contrasts \(\mathbf{K}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}\) with \(\mathbf{K}^{\top}\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{0}\), which do not depend on \(\boldsymbol\beta\), and recovers \(\mathrm{RSS}/(n - p)\) (Patterson and Thompson, 1971, Biometrika). In mixed models the same correction matters most when the number of clusters is small relative to the cluster-level fixed effects, exactly the SPRUCE case. Because the REML likelihood depends on \(\mathbf{X}\), two REML fits with different fixed effects are likelihoods of different data, so their ratio is meaningless; two REML fits with the same fixed part and different random parts can be compared. Harville (1977, Journal of the American Statistical Association) also gives the Bayesian reading: REML is the marginal likelihood after integrating \(\boldsymbol\beta\) out under a flat prior.

nlme carries the residual correlation

The same course names two R packages. nlme is the one they single out for a flexible covariance. lme4 is the other. The split below is what the two fitting functions actually expose.

Question nlme::lme lme4::lmer
Residual correlation in time ✅ correlation =, including corAR1() No correlation argument on lmer()
Likelihood method = "REML" or "ML" ✅ REML = TRUE by default
More than one cluster One random formula is the natural case ✅ Several (1 | group) terms in one formula

Read the three rows: nlme::lme exposes correlation =, including corAR1(); lmer() does not take a correlation argument on the residual. lme4 wins on multiple (1|group) terms in one formula. Pick the package to match whether residual time correlation or multiple clusters is the harder problem.

Going further. Neither package gives exact \(F\) tests for fixed effects in unbalanced mixed models, because the null distribution of the Wald statistic depends on estimated variance components. nlme reports \(t\) and \(F\) with “containment” denominator degrees of freedom taken from the grouping level where each term varies; lmer() reports no \(P\) values at all. lmerTest adds the Satterthwaite approximation, which matches the first two moments of the variance of \(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) to a scaled \(\chi^2\), and pbkrtest the Kenward–Roger method (Kenward and Roger, 1997, Biometrics), which also inflates the covariance matrix of \(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}\) for the uncertainty in the variance components and is the more accurate choice with few clusters. With ten enclosures, both give denominator degrees of freedom near the enclosure-level count, not the number of rows; a parametric bootstrap of the likelihood ratio is the alternative when the approximations are in doubt. The AR(1) term in nlme models \(\mathrm{Cor}(\varepsilon_{ij}, \varepsilon_{i'j}) = \phi^{|t_i - t_{i'}|}\) within a cluster (Pinheiro and Bates, Mixed-Effects Models in S and S-PLUS, 2000, chapter 5).

Eight minutes: where does \(u_j\) sit? 📝

On your hierarchy, or on SPRUCE:

  • what is \(j\), and what is \(i\)?
  • which factor is fixed, because you would use those levels again?
  • which factor is random, because the levels are a sample you want to generalise?

Eight minutes. The answer slide is SPRUCE, because the enclosure is a sample of a bog and the warming levels are the ones the design would use again.

One reading: an enclosure effect

  • \(j\) is the enclosure. \(i\) is a depth, or a date, inside it
  • Warming and CO\(_2\) are fixed. Those levels are the contrast
  • The enclosure is random if the target is a bog, not these ten boxes alone. Ten levels is a small sample of \(\sigma_u^2\)
  • \(u_j\) sits on the link. Depths that share an enclosure share that draw

A model that ignores \(j\) treats two depths in one enclosure as if they were two enclosures. That is the interval that is too small.

Write \(u_j\) on the link, not on the copy-number scale, unless they are already on \(\log_{10}\). Ten enclosures means \(\hat\sigma_u\) will be noisy. Say that, then stop. The longitudinal random-slope picture is in the file and hidden.

Going further. Power for a cluster-level treatment depends on the number of clusters far more than on rows per cluster: for a two-arm comparison with \(J\) clusters per arm and \(m\) rows each, the standard error of the difference is \(\sqrt{2(\sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2/m)/J}\), which hits a floor of \(\sqrt{2\sigma_u^2/J}\) however large \(m\) becomes. The required number of clusters is the individually randomised sample size times the design effect \(1 + (m-1)\rho\), divided by \(m\), plus a small-sample correction because the test has about \(2J - 2\) degrees of freedom (Donner and Klar, Design and Analysis of Cluster Randomization Trials in Health Research, 2000). For complex designs, simulate: generate data from the fitted mixed model with the planned \(J\), \(m\) and effect size, refit, and count rejections, for example with the simr package (Green and MacLeod, 2016, Methods in Ecology and Evolution). With ten enclosures, the useful planning question is the smallest warming slope detectable with reasonable power, not whether more depths would help.

The line is allowed to vary inside \(j\)

Model

\[ Y_{ij} = (\beta_0 + u_{0j}) + (\beta_1 + u_{1j})\, t_{ij} + \varepsilon_{ij}. \]

\(j\) is the person, the child, or the enclosure. \(i\) is the wave. \(\beta_0\) and \(\beta_1\) are the mean intercept and the mean slope. \(u_{0j}\) and \(u_{1j}\) are the cluster’s departures, mean zero, usually Gaussian.

The sketch replaces a separate line for each of sixteen children with these four parameters. Ignoring \(j\) treats the waves as independent, and the interval uses the wrong variance. The same hierarchy is what lets a longitudinal sample be a multilevel model.

Read the model callout: random intercept \(u_{0j}\) and random slope \(u_{1j}\) on time inside cluster \(j\). Sixteen separate lines become four population parameters plus cluster departures. Ignoring \(j\) is independence across waves—the wrong standard error. This is the generative story behind the spaghetti plot two slides ahead.

The call, then the picture

Warming sits on the enclosure. Time sits on the wave. The excerpt is from generate-slide-figures.R. The second call is the nlme version of the same mean, with an AR(1) residual. It is not the fit drawn on the next slide.

lmer-call.R

lmer(
  Y ~ time * warming + (time | enclosure),
  REML = TRUE
)

nlme::lme(
  Y ~ time * warming,
  random = ~ time | enclosure,
  correlation = nlme::corAR1(form = ~ time | enclosure),
  method = "REML"
)

A farm block under a split-plot is the same (1 | cluster) term. Chapter 29 of A Guide on Data Analysis fits that term by REML, and tests the whole-plot factor against the farm variance.

Walk lmer(Y ~ time * warming + (time | enclosure), REML = TRUE) first: warming on enclosure, time on the wave. The nlme block adds corAR1(form = ~ time | enclosure)—same mean structure, explicit residual correlation. Say the second call is not the fit drawn on the next slide. Posit chapter 29 links farm blocks to (1|cluster) for split-plot thinking.

Twelve enclosures, two slopes

Illustrative, seed 20261005, not a SPRUCE fit. The lines are lmer(Y ~ time * warming + (time | enclosure), REML = TRUE). One mean structure, and a departure \(u_{0j}\) and \(u_{1j}\) for each enclosure.

Pair with the code slide: this is the saved simulation at seed 20261005, not SPRUCE data. Point at ambient versus warmed colours and per-enclosure lines—each line is \(u_{0j}\) and \(u_{1j}\) around the shared time * warming mean. Thirty seconds if you are short on time; the estimand is still enclosure-level warming over time, not independent flux points.

Causal estimands

⚖️ Twenty minutes. Simpson, the DAG roles, and the network search. A Bayesian reading of the same mean is in the close.

A coefficient is not an intervention

SPRUCE assigns warming and \(\mathrm{CO}_2\). A slope on assigned warming estimates the effect of that assignment, in that bog, under the design assumptions.

An observational regression of taxon counts on organic carbon does not estimate “the effect of setting carbon to a new value”, unless the adjustment set matches the causal question.

Randomisation is a design fact. Adding covariates is not a substitute for it.

SPRUCE warming and \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) are assigned; the slope answers that assignment in that bog. CA-SYS organic-carbon regression is observational—no \(do(\text{carbon})\) without the right adjustment set. Covariates adjust associations; they do not recreate randomisation. This slide opens the causal hour. Simpson is next.

Going further. In the potential-outcomes notation, each unit has a response \(Y(x)\) for every level \(x\) it could receive, and the average causal effect is \(\mathbb{E}\{Y(x_1)\} - \mathbb{E}\{Y(x_0)\}\). The regression contrast \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x_1) - \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x_0)\) equals it under three identification conditions: consistency (\(Y = Y(x)\) for the level actually received), conditional exchangeability (\(Y(x) \perp X \mid \mathbf{L}\) for an adjustment set \(\mathbf{L}\)), and positivity (\(\Pr(X = x \mid \mathbf{L}) > 0\) for every stratum). Randomisation makes exchangeability hold by design with \(\mathbf{L}\) empty; in observational data it is an untestable assumption about \(\mathbf{L}\). The standard references are Hernán and Robins, Causal Inference: What If, and Imbens and Rubin, Causal Inference for Statistics, Social, and Biomedical Sciences.

Simpson

Two hospitals, one survival rate

François Giltaire, Le paradoxe de Simpson. A public-health report prints one number per hospital. Survival is 86% at A and 79% at B. The booking that follows from those two numbers is A.

State the two overall rates—86% at A, 79% at B—and let the room say “choose A”. Do not spoil the strata yet; the next slide breaks the table. Giltaire is the narrative source on the slide.

Inside each severity, B is higher

A takes mostly light cases. B takes mostly severe cases. Each hospital has 1000 patients.

Light cases Severe cases Overall
A 90% of 800 70% of 200 86%
B 95% of 200 75% of 800 79%

Light cases: B is above A. Severe cases: B is above A. The single published rate still ranks A above B.

Walk the table: A has 800 light and 200 severe; B has 200 light and 800 severe—1000 patients each. Within light, B beats A; within severe, B beats A again. Ask why the headline still favours A; the weight slide answers with arithmetic.

The overall rate is a weighted mean

The overall rate is the subgroup rates weighted by how many patients sit in each subgroup.

\[ \frac{800 \times 0.9 + 200 \times 0.7}{800 + 200} = 86\% \]

\[ \frac{200 \times 0.95 + 800 \times 0.75}{200 + 800} = 79\% \]

The denominators match. A puts a weight of \(n = 800\) on the light cases, where the rates are high. B puts a weight of \(n = 200\) on those same cases. That difference in weights is enough to reverse the overall ranking.

Read the two displayed fractions that yield 86% and 79%. Point at the \(800\) versus \(200\) weights on light cases—the subgroup rates favour B, but the mix favours A overall. This is the mechanism CA-SYS will reuse without necessarily reversing the sign.

Going further. The remedy is standardisation: apply each hospital’s stratum rates to one common case mix. With the pooled mix of 1000 light and 1000 severe cases, A gives \(0.5 \times 0.90 + 0.5 \times 0.70 = 80\%\) and B gives \(0.5 \times 0.95 + 0.5 \times 0.75 = 85\%\), so B is ahead once the weights are equal. In causal terms this is the back-door adjustment formula, \(\Pr\{Y = 1 \mid do(x)\} = \sum_s \Pr(Y = 1 \mid x, s)\,\Pr(s)\), valid when severity \(S\) closes every back-door path from hospital to survival. Whether to pool or to stratify is not a statistical question: Pearl showed that the same table can call for either answer depending on whether the third variable is a confounder or a mediator (Pearl, “Comment: Understanding Simpson’s Paradox”, The American Statistician, 2014). Direct standardisation in epidemiology is the same computation, with a reference population chosen in advance.

Severity mixes two hospitals that are not the same

Severity changes which hospital the patients go to, and it changes the survival rate. Pooling the light cases with the severe cases compares hospitals that did not treat the same mix.

The paradox, in one sentence: a trend that holds in every subgroup can reverse once the subgroups are pooled, because the subgroups do not have the same proportions. The soil version is the same arithmetic on CA-SYS.

DAG: severity \(\to\) hospital and severity \(\to\) survival; hospital \(\to\) survival. Pooling mixes severities with different hospital proportions—that opens a non-causal association. Read the one-sentence paradox, then jump to [#sec-casys-confounding] for tillage zones where weights shift the contrast without a full reversal.

The same arithmetic, on the soil table

Contrast Samples No-till minus tilled 95% interval
All zones pooled 595 \(+47.5\) OTUs \(-7.5\) to \(+102.6\)
Mixed zone only 226 \(+80.3\) OTUs \(-6.0\) to \(+166.7\)

Work through the two rows. The pooled contrast uses 595 samples and gives +47.5 OTUs. Restricting to the mixed zone, where no-till and tilled fields sit side by side, nearly doubles it to +80.3 on 226 samples. The hospital table had a reversal; this is the same mechanism without one, which is the more common case in practice: the weights differ, so the pooled number is not the contrast you wanted. Say clearly that both intervals include zero, so neither row is a detected difference. The honest sentence is that the practice contrast is imprecise here and that its point estimate depends on which samples you compare.

Why the two rows differ

The CA-SYS zones are not a nuisance; they are part of the platform’s design.

Zone No-till samples Tilled samples
Mixed 114 112
No-till zone 171 0
Tilled zone 0 198

Two of the three zones contain only one practice. In those rows the tillage contrast is not identified at all: any difference between them is also a difference between places.

This is a positivity, or overlap, failure, and it is worth naming. Within the no-till zone there is no tilled sample to compare against, so no amount of modelling recovers a within-zone contrast there. A regression with zone as a covariate will still print a tillage coefficient; it will be driven entirely by the 226 mixed-zone samples plus an extrapolation. The mixed zone exists because the designers wanted to test the spatial arrangement of practices, which makes it the right place to read the contrast.

Going further. Positivity requires \(0 < \Pr(X = x \mid \mathbf{L} = \mathbf{l}) < 1\) for every stratum \(\mathbf{l}\) of the adjustment set that the target population contains. Here \(\Pr(\text{tilled} \mid \text{no-till zone}) = 0\) is a structural violation, fixed by the design, not a random zero that a larger sample would fill. Two honest responses exist: change the estimand to the population where overlap holds (the mixed zone), or keep the full population and accept that the answer rests on a parametric extrapolation. Weighting estimators make the problem visible, since the inverse probability weight \(1/\Pr(X = x \mid \mathbf{L})\) is infinite there. Petersen and colleagues, “Diagnosing and responding to violations in the positivity assumption” (Statistical Methods in Medical Research, 2012), is the standard treatment.

The arrow pattern behind both tables

Zone decides which practice a field receives, and the soil in a zone also acts on the community. That is the hospital graph with new labels: severity mixes two hospitals.

Point out that this diagram is an assumption, not a result. It says zone acts on richness through something other than tillage, which is plausible because soil type is unbalanced across zones, but the data cannot prove the arrow. That is the message of the DAG slides that follow.

Going further. In this graph the path practice \(\leftarrow\) zone \(\to\) richness is a back-door path: it starts with an arrow into the treatment. Pearl’s back-door criterion says a set \(\mathbf{L}\) identifies the effect of practice on \(Y\) when no member of \(\mathbf{L}\) is a descendant of practice and \(\mathbf{L}\) blocks every back-door path; here \(\mathbf{L} = \{\text{zone}\}\) qualifies. The identifying formula is then \(\mathbb{E}\{Y \mid do(x)\} = \sum_{z} \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x, z)\,\Pr(z)\), and the previous slide shows that two of the three terms \(\mathbb{E}(Y \mid x, z)\) are not estimable. A valid adjustment set therefore needs both a correct graph and positivity within it. The reference is Pearl, Causality (2nd edition, 2009), chapter 3.

Roles on a graph

The DAG is a set of assumptions

Johannes Hertel, Linking statistics, computational biology and biochemistry, lists three assumptions before the arrows can be read as causes.

Assumption

There is a DAG that is a causal representation of the system. A variable is independent of its non-effects given its direct causes. The only conditional independences in the data are the ones that DAG states.

The second sentence is his causal Markov condition. The third is faithfulness. He notes that a complete measurement of every variable in the graph is generally impossible, so the practical question is which variables the analysis has to include.

Read the assumption callout slowly: causal DAG, Markov (independence given parents), faithfulness (only those independences). Incomplete measurement is the practical punchline—which nodes must enter the model? The six numbered role slides after the fork and the chain answer that question one third variable at a time.

Going further. The causal Markov condition states that each variable is independent of its non-descendants given its parents, which is equivalent to the factorisation \(p(v_1, \ldots, v_K) = \prod_k p(v_k \mid \mathrm{pa}_k)\) and to “d-separation implies independence”. Faithfulness is the converse: every conditional independence in the distribution is a d-separation in the graph. It fails when two paths cancel exactly, for example a direct effect \(+\beta\) and an indirect effect \(-\beta\); the set of such parameter values has Lebesgue measure zero, which is the usual justification, but near-cancellation makes tests unreliable in finite samples (Uhler and colleagues, Annals of Statistics, 2013). A third, often implicit, condition is causal sufficiency: no unmeasured common cause of two measured variables. Spirtes, Glymour and Scheines, Causation, Prediction, and Search (2000), is the standard statement of all three.

One independence, two causal stories

A directed acyclic graph is what lets you check a correlation pattern against a causal theory. One pattern can belong to more than one theory.

The shared statistical statement in Hertel’s pair of examples: \(T\) is correlated with \(X\) and with \(Z\) whether or not you condition, and \(Z\) is conditionally independent of \(X\) given \(T\).

Set up the puzzle: one conditional-independence pattern, two directed stories. Read the shared statement about \(T\), \(X\), and \(Z\) without showing arrows yet. Ask the room what differs between fork and chain if \(Z\perp X\mid T\) in both—the next two slides answer with arrow direction.

Going further. The graphical rule behind this is d-separation. A path between two nodes is blocked by a conditioning set \(S\) when it contains a chain \(A \to B \to C\) or a fork \(A \leftarrow B \to C\) with \(B \in S\), or a collider \(A \to B \leftarrow C\) with neither \(B\) nor any descendant of \(B\) in \(S\). Two nodes are d-separated given \(S\) when every path between them is blocked; under the causal Markov condition, d-separation implies conditional independence in every distribution the graph generates. Fork and chain produce the same d-separation statements, so no test on observational data can tell them apart; only a collider leaves a distinct signature. The reference is Pearl, Probabilistic Reasoning in Intelligent Systems (1988), and Koller and Friedman, Probabilistic Graphical Models (2009), chapter 3.

\(T\) is a common cause

\(T\) causes \(X\) and \(T\) causes \(Z\). There is no direct arrow between \(Z\) and \(X\). Conditioning on \(T\) leaves \(Z\) independent of \(X\).

Fork: \(T\to X\) and \(T\to Z\); no edge between \(X\) and \(Z\). Conditioning on the common cause \(T\) blocks the non-causal \(X\)–\(Z\) association. This is the skeleton of role 1, the confounder.

\(Z\) acts through \(T\)

\(Z\) causes \(T\) and \(T\) causes \(X\). The conditional independence is the same as in the fork. The arrows are not settled by that independence.

Chain: \(Z\to T\to X\). Same \(Z\perp X\mid T\) as the fork, but the adjustment rules differ: the chain is the skeleton of role 4, the mediator. Arrows are not identified by that independence alone. Pair with [#sec-same-ci] as the setup slide.

Role 1 · A confounder causes both \(X\) and \(Y\)

Carbon and the taxon both respond to moisture, so the unadjusted slope of \(Y\) on \(X\) mixes the blue path with the orange back-door path.

Adjust for it, by design or in the model. Otherwise the coefficient is not the effect of \(X\). Simpson’s hospitals and the CA-SYS zones are this graph.

Point at the orange back-door path \(X \leftarrow Z \to Y\) first, then at the blue arrow. Age between a disease phenotype and a metabolite is Hertel’s example; soil moisture between carbon and a taxon is ours. Ask the room what happens if moisture were instead the way carbon acts on the taxon: then it is role 4, and adjusting removes part of the effect.

Going further. Pearl’s back-door criterion: a set \(\mathbf{L}\) identifies the effect of \(X\) on \(Y\) when no member of \(\mathbf{L}\) is a descendant of \(X\) and \(\mathbf{L}\) blocks every path that starts with an arrow into \(X\). The identifying formula is \(\mathbb{E}\{Y \mid do(x)\} = \sum_{z} \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x, z)\Pr(z)\), the standardisation that reversed the hospital ranking. Under the null of no effect the fork gives \(X \perp Y \mid Z\), which is why conditioning on \(Z\) removes the spurious association. An unmeasured confounder cannot be adjusted for; the sensitivity slide at the end of this block bounds how strong it would have to be (Pearl, Causality, 2nd edition, 2009, chapter 3).

Role 2 · A collider is caused by both

Conditioning on \(Z\), or keeping only the samples that passed it, opens the orange path and creates an \(X\)–\(Y\) association. Restricting a study to healthy people is the selection-bias version.

Leave it out. Conditioning on a collider anyway is called overmatching.

Collider: \(X\to Z\leftarrow Y\), a v-structure in the Bayesian-network notes. Hertel’s example is inflammation. Here the filter on sequencing depth is the collider: if both carbon and the taxon raise the read count, the samples that survive the filter carry an association the field never had.

The graph without the blue arrow factorises as \(p(x,y,z)=p(x)\,p(y)\,p(z\mid x,y)\), so \(p(x,y)=p(x)p(y)\) and \(X\perp Y\). After selecting on \(Z\), \(p(x,y\mid z)=p(z\mid x,y)\,p(x)\,p(y)/p(z)\), which does not factor into a function of \(x\) times a function of \(y\), so \(X\not\perp Y\mid Z\).

Going further. The classical case is Berkson’s bias (Berkson, Biometrics Bulletin, 1946): two diseases independent in the population become negatively associated among hospital patients, because admission is a common effect of both. Adjusting for a descendant of a collider opens the path too, more weakly. Unlike confounding bias, collider bias cannot be removed by collecting more covariates after the selection; it has to be prevented at the design stage, or corrected with inverse probability of selection weights when the selection model is known. Hernán, Hernández-Díaz and Robins, “A structural approach to selection bias” (Epidemiology, 2004), is the reference.

Role 3 · A variance factor touches only \(Y\)

A batch effect adds noise to \(Y\) without creating an \(X\)–\(Y\) correlation. It can hide one.

Adjust for it, for power: by blocking in the design, or as a term in the model. The coefficient of \(X\) is unbiased either way; only its interval changes.

DAG: \(X\to Y\) and \(Z\to Y\) only—no \(Z\to X\). Adjust for precision, not because \(Z\) confounds. This is the blocking argument of the design hour in graph form.

The graph factorises as \(p(x,y,z)=p(x)\,p(z)\,p(y\mid x,z)\). There is no back-door path, so the \(X\) slope is identified with or without \(Z\). The law of total variance splits the noise: \(\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid X)=\mathbb{E}\{\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid X,Z)\mid X\}+\mathrm{Var}\{\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X,Z)\mid X\}\). A batch effect sits in the second term, and conditioning on \(Z\) drops it. On a non-identity link the story is subtler: for a logistic model, omitting a precision variable attenuates the conditional odds ratio, because odds ratios are not collapsible, even though \(Z \perp X\).

Role 4 · A mediator carries part of the effect

The orange path is indirect. The blue arrow is direct. The total effect of assigned warming is both together.

Leave it out for the total effect. Adjusting for productivity estimates something else, and only under further assumptions. Write the path you mean before you add the covariate.

Warming \(\to\) productivity \(\to\) flux is indirect; warming \(\to\) flux is direct. The total effect of assigned warming does not condition on productivity. Adjusting for productivity targets a different estimand—say “write the path first” before opening lm().

Going further. With nested potential outcomes \(Y(x, M(x'))\), the total effect splits exactly into a natural direct effect, \(\mathbb{E}\{Y(1, M(0))\} - \mathbb{E}\{Y(0, M(0))\}\), and a natural indirect effect, \(\mathbb{E}\{Y(1, M(1))\} - \mathbb{E}\{Y(1, M(0))\}\).
