Randomising warming identifies the total effect, but not these two pieces: they also need no unmeasured confounding of the mediator–outcome relation, and no such confounder affected by warming itself (the cross-world assumption). If a soil variable drives both productivity and flux, conditioning on productivity opens a collider path and biases even the direct effect. The usual references are Robins and Greenland (1992), Pearl, “Direct and indirect effects” (2001), and VanderWeele, Explanation in Causal Inference (2015).

Role 5 · A moderator changes the effect

The orange arrow points at the blue arrow, not at a node: \(Z\) changes how much \(Y\) moves when \(X\) moves.

Model an interaction, or report each stratum. Matching on \(Z\) does not do that job, and the sample must be large enough for the interaction.

Hertel’s example is sex: women and men may show different metabolite profiles in response to a disease. Ours: the warming effect on flux may depend on how far down the water table sits. There is no new conditional independence to block or open—a moderator is neither a fork nor a collider.

Write \(\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X=x,Z=z)=\beta_0+\beta_1 x+\beta_2 z+\beta_3 xz\). Then \(\beta_3\) is the change in the \(X\) slope per unit of \(Z\). Matching on \(Z\) mixes the stratum slopes; it does not estimate \(\beta_3\).

Going further. Effect modification is scale-dependent: on a log link, \(\beta_3 = 0\) means the rate ratio is the same in each stratum of \(Z\), yet the difference in \(\mu\) can still differ between strata, and the reverse holds on the identity scale. So “no interaction” must always name the link \(g\). VanderWeele distinguishes effect modification (\(Z\) merely observed) from causal interaction (joint intervention on \(X\) and \(Z\)), which needs \(Z\) itself to be unconfounded. With a balanced binary \(Z\), the standard error of \(\beta_3\) is roughly twice that of a main effect, so detecting an interaction of the same size takes about four times the sample (Gelman, Hill and Vehtari, Regression and Other Stories, 2020).

Role 6 · An instrument touches only \(X\)

\(Z\) moves \(X\) and reaches \(Y\) only through \(X\). It creates no spurious \(X\)–\(Y\) correlation.

Optional. It does not need to enter the regression. Some designs are built around it, when \(X\) and \(Y\) share a confounder nobody measured.

Instrument path: \(Z\to X\to Y\) with no arrow from \(Z\) to \(Y\). Students confuse instruments with confounders because both touch \(X\); check for a \(Z\to Y\) edge.

The graph factorises as \(p(z,x,y)=p(z)\,p(x\mid z)\,p(y\mid x)\), so \(p(y\mid x,z)=p(y\mid x)\), which is \(Z\perp Y\mid X\). A confounder would add \(Z\to Y\) and break that equality.

Going further. The three instrument conditions are relevance (\(Z \not\perp X\)), exclusion (\(Z\) acts on \(Y\) only through \(X\)) and independence (\(Z \perp U\) for the unmeasured confounder \(U\)); only the first is testable. With a binary \(Z\) and a homogeneous linear effect, the Wald estimator \(\{\mathbb{E}(Y \mid Z=1) - \mathbb{E}(Y \mid Z=0)\}/\{\mathbb{E}(X \mid Z=1) - \mathbb{E}(X \mid Z=0)\}\) recovers the effect of \(X\); two-stage least squares is its multivariable form. With heterogeneous effects it identifies only the local average effect among units whose \(X\) responds to \(Z\), under monotonicity (Imbens and Angrist, Econometrica, 1994). Mendelian randomisation is the genetic-variant case on this slide.

Adjust what the role requires

The control list, for an explanatory analysis.

  • Role 1, a confounder: control it, by matching or in the model, or the interpretation is wrong.

  • Role 2, a collider: leave it out.

  • Role 3, a variance factor: control it, for power.

  • Role 4, a mediator: leave it out, unless the question is that path.

  • Role 5, a moderator: an interaction or a stratum, not a match alone.

  • Role 6, an instrument: optional. Some designs are built around it.

Step the six fragments as a control checklist: confounder in, collider out, variance factor in for power, mediator out unless that path is the estimand, moderator needs interaction, instrument optional. About one minute if you step each line—pause after collider versus confounder because that pair is what exams test.

Sensitivity is the causal report

For an observational claim, say what would have to be true for the conclusion to change:

  • a missing confounder associated with both \(x\) and \(Y\) at a stated strength
  • a different adjustment set from the same diagram
  • a different family, if the mean is sensitive to the variance assumption
  • a different prior, if the fit is Bayesian and the sample is small

SPRUCE still needs a sensitivity story for the chamber itself: the design page monitors chamberless areas because the enclosure is not identical to an open bog. The pitfalls card is show that the conclusion can move.

Observational claims need explicit what-ifs: unmeasured confounder strength, alternate adjustment sets, family change, prior change for small Bayesian fits. SPRUCE chamberless plots are the designed sensitivity for enclosure versus open bog—cite the design page line on the slide. Link [#sec-sensitivity] in pitfalls for the reporting habit.

Going further. The first bullet has a closed-form answer. For an observed risk ratio \(RR\) (or a rate ratio from a log-link GLM), the E-value \(RR + \sqrt{RR(RR - 1)}\) is the minimum strength of association, on the risk-ratio scale, that an unmeasured confounder would need with both treatment and outcome to move the estimate to the null, conditional on the measured covariates (VanderWeele and Ding, Annals of Internal Medicine, 2017). It rests on the Ding–VanderWeele bounding factor, which makes no assumption about the confounder’s distribution, so it is conservative. Rosenbaum’s sensitivity parameter \(\Gamma\) plays the same role for matched designs: it bounds how far the odds of treatment can differ between two matched units because of an unobserved covariate (Rosenbaum, Observational Studies, 2002). For the Bayesian bullet, the analogue is to report the posterior under at least two priors and say whether the decision changes.

Causally orient the edges

A direct link is not a direction

The Bayesian-network course notes separate two graphs. A dependency network joins two variables that still share a statistical relationship once the others are accounted for. That removes indirect co-variation. It does not say which way the link runs.

A regulatory direction needs a further claim, in the notes’ words: a change in \(X\) produces a change in \(Y\), and a change in \(Y\) does not change \(X\). That is the same gap as Hertel’s fork and chain, one independence, two causal stories.

Dependency networks remove indirect association once other variables are accounted for—they do not orient edges. Regulatory direction needs asymmetry under intervention, as the notes quote. Tie back to [#sec-same-ci]: statistics alone did not pick fork versus chain.

Going further. For Gaussian data the dependency network is the Gaussian graphical model: an edge \(A - B\) is absent exactly when the partial correlation of \(A\) and \(B\) given all other variables is zero, which is a zero in the precision matrix \(\Sigma^{-1}\). The graphical lasso (Friedman, Hastie and Tibshirani, Biostatistics, 2008) estimates a sparse \(\Sigma^{-1}\) when the number of variables exceeds the sample size. This undirected graph is the moral graph of any DAG that generated the data: it contains every DAG edge plus an edge between co-parents of a collider, so it can show a link that no causal arrow supports. For compositional counts such as 16S data, partial correlations on raw proportions are biased by the sum constraint, which is why methods such as SPIEC-EASI work on log-ratios first (Kurtz and colleagues, PLoS Computational Biology, 2015). Lauritzen, Graphical Models (1996), is the reference text.

Three graphs, one distribution

With \(Z\) between \(X\) and \(Y\), and no direct arrow from \(X\) to \(Y\), the notes count four directed acyclic graphs. Three of them encode the same conditional independences. Those three are one Markov equivalence class, drawn as a completed partially directed acyclic graph: an edge is directed only when the direction is identified, and left undirected when it is not.

Statistics on the observational distribution will not separate the three. The notes’ next step is a causal analysis.

With \(Z\) between \(X\) and \(Y\) and no \(X\to Y\), four DAGs collapse to one Markov equivalence class of three—CPDAG leaves some edges undirected. Observational data cannot pick among the three; the notes point to causal analysis next. Knock-out and time series are the two escapes on the following slides.

Going further. Verma and Pearl (1990) proved that two DAGs are Markov equivalent exactly when they have the same skeleton and the same v-structures, that is the same unshielded colliders \(A \to B \leftarrow C\) with \(A\) and \(C\) non-adjacent. Here the three graphs without a collider at \(Z\) share skeleton \(X - Z - Y\) and have no v-structure, while \(X \to Z \leftarrow Y\) has one, so it sits alone in its class. The CPDAG (or essential graph) directs an edge when every member of the class agrees on it; Meek’s rules (1995) propagate those compelled orientations from the v-structures without creating a new collider or a cycle. With linear structural equations and non-Gaussian errors the class can shrink to one graph (LiNGAM, Shimizu and colleagues, 2006), which is one way extra assumptions buy direction.

A knock-out supplies the direction

When the graph was not drawn by an expert, the notes’ first source of direction is a perturbation. If knocking out \(X\) changes \(Y\), the notes write \(X \to Y\). They read that experiment as an intervention on \(X\).

It is empirical, and it does not need a fitted network. It is a wet-lab result. Orienting a whole network this way needs many perturbations, which the notes say does not scale.

Perturbation first: knock out \(X\), watch \(Y\), write \(X\to Y\) and link to \(do(X)\). Wet-lab empirical direction without fitting a network—but scaling to whole networks needs many knockouts, which the notes dismiss. Contrast with passive observational equivalence on the previous slide.

Going further. A perfect intervention on \(X\) cuts every arrow into \(X\), so comparing the distribution of \(Y\) with and without the knock-out tests \(X \to Y\) against \(Y \to X\) directly; it also distinguishes members of the same Markov equivalence class. Eberhardt, Glymour and Scheines (2005) showed that \(p - 1\) single-variable interventions are sufficient, and in the worst case necessary, to orient every edge of a \(p\)-node graph, which is the “does not scale” remark in formal terms. Knock-outs are rarely perfect: compensation, off-target effects and incomplete silencing make them soft interventions, which change \(p(x \mid \mathrm{pa}_X)\) rather than fix \(X\). Mixing observational and interventional data under a known intervention target is what the GIES algorithm of Hauser and Bühlmann (JMLR, 2012) does.

Time supplies the direction

The other source in the notes is a series. The regression is the dependency-network regression, moved forward one time step.

Model

\[ Y(t+1) \approx a_1 X_1(t) + \cdots + a_k X_k(t). \]

If \(a_i \ne 0\), then \(X_i\) regulates \(Y\) from one time to the next. The notes call this a dynamic Bayesian network: an initial state, the same transition from time to time, and the chain unrolled.

Read the model callout: \(Y(t+1)\) regressed on \(X_1(t),\ldots,X_k(t)\). Non-zero \(a_i\) means \(X_i\) regulates \(Y\) forward in time—direction from lag, not from partial correlation alone. Dynamic BN unrolls the same transition; link to flux time series only if you stay within the slide’s regression wording.

Going further. Testing \(a_i = 0\) in this lagged regression is Granger causality (Granger, Econometrica, 1969): \(X_i\) Granger-causes \(Y\) when its past improves the prediction of \(Y\) beyond the past of every other included series. It is a statement about prediction, and it equals a causal claim only when all common drivers are in the regression, the sampling interval is shorter than the causal lag, and there is no instantaneous effect within a time step; a slow, unmeasured seasonal driver produces spurious lags. A dynamic Bayesian network adds the assumptions that the transition is first-order Markov and stationary, so one two-slice graph describes the whole series (Murphy, Dynamic Bayesian Networks, PhD thesis, 2002). Because arrows only point forward in time, the acyclicity restriction is lifted across slices, which is how feedback loops re-enter.

Three complementary approaches to learn the structure of a DAG

  • Constraint-based: test a conditional independence. If the test rejects independence, the nodes are joined.

  • Score-based: give each candidate graph a score for how well it describes the data, and maximise the score.

  • Hybrid: learn the skeleton from the constraints, then refine it with a score.

Marco Scutari’s benchmark, as the notes report it, for the developer of bnlearn: at small samples the constraint-based algorithms were more accurate than the score-based ones, and as accurate as the hybrids. Tabu search, a score-based method, was faster than the constraint-based algorithms and faster than simulated annealing.

Step the three items: constraint-based joins nodes when independence tests fail; score-based maximises a graph score; hybrid skeleton then score. The figure on the next slide is the same three families, with the algorithm names under each panel. These learn association structure under DAG assumptions, not causation by default.

Going further. The reference constraint-based method is the PC algorithm (Spirtes and Glymour, 1991): start from the complete undirected graph, delete an edge \(A - B\) as soon as some subset \(S\) of the current neighbours makes \(A \perp B \mid S\) pass the test, then orient the v-structures and apply Meek’s rules. Its output is a CPDAG, and it is consistent as \(n \to \infty\) under causal Markov, faithfulness, causal sufficiency and correct tests; Kalisch and Bühlmann (JMLR, 2007) extended this to sparse high-dimensional Gaussian graphs. Score-based methods maximise a decomposable score such as BIC or BDe, and the greedy equivalence search of Chickering (2002) is consistent under the same assumptions. Searching all DAGs exactly is NP-hard, which is why hill-climbing and tabu are heuristics. FCI relaxes causal sufficiency and returns a partial ancestral graph that admits latent confounders.

The three searches, named

A generated sketch. The names are the ones bnlearn groups under each search.

Walk the three panels. Constraint-based removes the edge when the conditional-independence test does not reject: PC, Grow-Shrink, IAMB, MMPC. Score-based prefers the higher-scoring DAG: hill-climbing and tabu, drawn here as the extra arrow \(B \to C\). Hybrid takes that skeleton and then scores: MMHC and RSMAX2. The picture is a generated sketch, not a fitted graph.

A searched graph is still a DAG

The notes’ limits on that search. The graph has no cycles, so a feedback loop or a self-regulation is outside it. Adding observations can change the direction of an edge. A network of thousands of nodes is too large to enumerate, and the search then needs a heuristic.

The two implementations they name are bnlearn in R and PyAgrum in Python.

Close the limits first: no cycles, so feedback is out; more data can flip an edge; thousands of nodes need a heuristic. The next slide says why the loop is excluded, then the two implementations. Causal claims still need design, \(do\), or a perturbation. Thirty seconds.

Going further. The number of DAGs on \(p\) labelled nodes grows super-exponentially (Robinson’s recursion gives about \(4.2 \times 10^{18}\) for \(p = 10\)), so exhaustive search stops at a few dozen nodes even with dynamic programming. Edge flips with more data are expected rather than alarming: the score difference between equivalent orientations is zero, and between near-equivalent ones it is small, so the bootstrap edge-frequency of Friedman, Goldszmidt and Wyner (1999), available as boot.strength() in bnlearn, is the honest report of how stable each arrow is. Cycles can be handled by unrolling in time, as on the dynamic slide, or with cyclic structural causal models, whose equilibrium semantics is more delicate (Bongers and colleagues, Annals of Statistics, 2021).

Why the graph cannot loop

A Bayesian network is a product of one conditional per node, each given its parents:

Model

\[ p(v_1, \ldots, v_K) = \prod_{k=1}^{K} p(v_k \mid \mathrm{pa}_k). \]

The product is a probability only because the nodes have a topological order: every parent comes before its child. Integrate out the last node, then the one before, and each factor sums to one. A loop \(X \to Y \to X\) has no first node, so \(p(x \mid y)\,p(y \mid x)\) need not be a joint at all. d-separation reads independences off that ordered product, so it needs the order too. Feedback comes back by unrolling in time.

One idea: the arrows must admit an order, because the factorisation is built along it. Say “integrate from the leaves upwards” and the room sees why every factor normalises. Then the loop: there is no node to start from. d-separation and the Markov condition are statements about that factorisation, so a cyclic graph has neither.

Going further. A concrete failure: take \(p(x \mid y) = \mathcal{N}(y, 1)\) and \(p(y \mid x) = \mathcal{N}(x, 1)\). Their product is proportional to \(\exp\{-(x - y)^2\}\), whose integral over the plane is infinite, so no joint density has these two conditionals. In general two conditionals are compatible only if their ratio factorises as \(a(x)/b(y)\) (Arnold and Press, Journal of the American Statistical Association, 1989), which a loop does not guarantee. The topological order also makes ancestral sampling possible: draw each node after its parents. Undirected graphs (Markov random fields) allow cycles but pay with a global normalising constant, and cyclic structural causal models are defined through equilibrium solutions rather than a factorisation (Bongers and colleagues, Annals of Statistics, 2021). The dynamic Bayesian network keeps the order by indexing the loop in time: \(X_t \to Y_{t+1} \to X_{t+2}\) is acyclic.

Two implementations

bnlearn pyAgrum
Language R Python, on the C++ library aGrUM
Structure search The long catalogue: PC, Grow-Shrink, IAMB, MMPC, hill-climbing, tabu, MMHC, RSMAX2 Hill-climbing, tabu, MIIC and 3off2; PC and FCI in recent releases
Inference Conditional queries. Exact for discrete and Gaussian networks; approximate otherwise Lazy propagation and other junction-tree methods, plus sampling. Exact inference is the main engine
Also Bootstrap of the graph, interventions, counterfactuals Influence diagrams, credal networks, Markov random fields
Where the cost sits Conditional-independence tests, which grow with the conditioning set The junction tree. A large clique dominates the exact query

Neither search supplies a causal reading by itself.

bnlearn is the structure-learning catalogue behind labels such as MMPC and RSMAX. pyAgrum is the inference engine, and it also holds influence diagrams and credal nets. Do not quote a speed ratio between the packages. Tabu against constraint-based searches was Scutari’s comparison of algorithms, already on the search slide.

Quiz

❓ Fifteen questions on situations outside the two datasets, then the SAMPL draft.

Before the quiz ❓

Fifteen questions on new situations, not on the two datasets: four on the pitfalls, four on non-Gaussian responses, one on smoothing, two on the design, and four on the causal and Bayesian reading. Pick an option, then press x to check. Press q to reset that question.

Use the notation from the first hour: \(Y\) is the response, \(x\) is the covariate, \(\mu = \mathbb{E}(Y \mid x)\), and \(j\) is the unit that received the treatment.

About fifteen minutes, one minute a question. Ask the room before anyone checks. The check key is x. f still flips a flashcard, earlier in the deck. None of the fifteen needs CA-SYS or SPRUCE: the point is to transfer the rule to a study the room has not seen. If the clock is short, take questions 1, 5, 9, 10, and 13, one per block.

A ratio of 0.97 (0.95 to 0.99), with \(p < 0.05\)

  • A large effect, because the test rejects.
  • Detected, and small. Ask whether 3% matters.
  • Too uncertain to quote, because the interval is narrow.

The lab analysis failed for every soil core with pH below 4

pH is recorded for every core, including the cores with no result.

  • Missing completely at random: drop the cores.
  • Missing at random given pH. pH has to enter the model.
  • Missing not at random, so no model can use these rows.

Fertiliser dose split at its median into “low” and “high”

The dose was applied at eight levels, from 0 to 200 kg N per hectare.

  • Fine: two groups make the effect easier to explain.
  • Keep dose continuous, and allow a curve if the response bends.
  • Use three groups instead of two, to keep more information.

Significant in grasslands (\(p = 0.01\)), not in forests (\(p = 0.30\))

The same liming treatment was tested in both ecosystems.

  • The treatment works in grasslands only.
  • Estimate the difference of the two effects, with its interval.
  • Report both \(p\)-values side by side; the reader can compare.

Earthworms counted in quadrats of 0.25, 0.5, and 1 m²

You want the effect of tillage on earthworm density.

  • Fit a Poisson model to the raw counts, ignoring the area.
  • Poisson or negative binomial, with \(\log(\text{area})\) as an offset.
  • Divide each count by its area, then fit a Poisson to the densities.

Pearson \(\chi^2/\mathrm{df} = 10.6\) after a Poisson fit

The log-link rate ratio is the quantity you care about.

  • Take \(\log(Y+1)\) and fit a Gaussian linear model.
  • Keep the log link. Replace the Poisson variance.
  • Trust the Poisson interval: the mean model is right.

\(\exp(\hat\beta_1) = 4\) in a logit model of seedling survival

One-unit increase in centred soil moisture, other covariates held fixed.

  • The survival probability is multiplied by 4.
  • The odds of survival are multiplied by 4.
  • The log-odds increase by 4.

\(\exp(\text{mean of } \log Y)\) reported as the mean of \(Y\)

\(Y\) is soil organic carbon stock, positive and right-skewed.

  • Correct: exp undoes the log.
  • It estimates the median, below the mean. Correct for it, or fit a log-link GLM.
  • It overestimates the mean, because the log stretches small values.

A smooth of soil temperature reports \(\mathrm{edf} = 1.0\)

The basis had \(k = 10\) functions.

  • The smooth failed. Refit with a larger \(k\).
  • The penalty shrank the curve to a straight line. A linear term is enough.
  • Temperature has no effect.

Warming is set per open-top chamber

Four warmed chambers, four control chambers, and ten soil cores in each.

  • 80 cores: 40 replicates per arm.
  • Four replicates per arm. Cores are repeats inside \(j\).
  • Ten replicates, one for each core position.

Twenty plots run downhill along a moisture gradient

Four treatments, five plots each.

  • Assign the treatments in order down the slope.
  • Make five blocks along the slope, and randomise the four treatments inside each.
  • Randomise all twenty plots, and ignore the slope.

Hospital B is worse overall, and better inside each severity

A publishes 86% survival, B publishes 79%. Inside light cases, and again inside severe cases, B is higher.

  • Book A. The overall rate is the only estimand.
  • The pooled ranking can reverse because the case-mix differs. Severity belongs in the contrast.
  • The reversal means one of the two tables is a calculation error.

Inflammation \(Z\) is caused by both \(X\) and \(Y\)

  • Put \(Z\) in the regression. It is associated with both.
  • Do not control \(Z\). Conditioning on a common effect creates a correlation.
  • Control \(Z\) only when the sample is large.

No-till raises soil moisture, and moisture raises \(\mathrm{CO}_2\) efflux

You want the total effect of no-till on efflux.

  • Adjust for moisture, to compare like with like.
  • Do not adjust for moisture. It is a mediator of the total effect.
  • Adjust for moisture only if it correlates with no-till.

A 95% posterior interval of 0.2 to 0.9 for a warming slope

  • In 95% of repeated experiments, an interval built this way covers the true slope.
  • Given the model, the prior, and the data, the slope lies in 0.2 to 0.9 with probability 0.95.
  • The prior no longer matters once the interval excludes 0.

SAMPL, in workshop language 📝

Lang and Altman, SAMPL ask for the analysis to be explicit enough to audit. For this room, four lines are the minimum:

  1. Purpose: describe, compare, explain, predict, explore, or confirm
  2. Estimand, with the unit and the contrast
  3. Family, link, and what you did about missing rows
  4. The estimate, the interval, and the assumption you checked

“We used a mixed model” is not one of the four lines. The pitfalls card is write so the analysis can be rerun.

Rewrite the model you arrived with 📝

Take the analysis you named at the start. Replace one paragraph so that a colleague could say what \(\mu\) is.

  • What is \(Y\), and what is \(j\)?
  • Which of the twelve pitfalls is the one you are currently violating?
  • Does the family match the support of \(Y\)?
  • What would you refit tomorrow morning as a sensitivity analysis?

Ten minutes if the clock allows, otherwise five, and one example aloud. This is the second protected exercise.

The SAMPL guideline

Download PDF file.

Leave this slide up while the room starts reading. The writing clock is the next slide: twenty minutes.

Twenty minutes: draft the plan 📝

The PDF on the previous slide is Lang and Altman, SAMPL. Write a plan a colleague could rerun. Use the checklist as the headings.

  • What is \(Y\), and what is one replicate?
  • What is \(\mu\), and which contrast?
  • Which family, and what did you assume about missing rows?

Twenty minutes, protected. If the clock is short, ten minutes and one volunteer reads the first three lines.

A checklist you can paste above a script

  1. Question and purpose, in one sentence
  2. Estimand, including the population and the unit
  3. Experimental or observational, and where the treatment was applied
  4. Support of \(Y\): continuous, count, binary, composition, censored
  5. Dependence: site, enclosure, date, space
  6. Missingness: how much, and a mechanism you are willing to defend
  7. Linearity of the linear predictor, checked, not assumed
  8. Family check: a QQ plot of the right residual
  9. Sensitivity: one alternative that could move the claim
  10. A report that separates association from an assigned effect

Reproducibility

🔁 A reference stack. One source file, then the practices that keep a rerun honest.

One source file

Copying the result is the slow path

Data and style updates are inevitable. Doing them by hand is slow, and it is where the numbers drift.

The diagrams in this block are from Jadey Ryan, Salt Lake City R Users Group, 1 July 2024. 📝 Lehmann and Blampey, Good practices.

Start by asking how many people have copied a number from a console into a manuscript. Everyone has. The loop at the bottom of the figure is the cost: a data correction arriving after the copy means every downstream number has to be found again by hand, and the one you miss is the one that reaches the reader. This is the same argument as the derived-data script in the datasets block, applied to the report.

Quarto renders the report and the slides

A data update or a data error changes the data source. A style update changes the tables, the plots, or the markdown. One render then writes the report and the presentation.

Compare the two diagrams side by side if you can. The number of arrows is similar; what changed is that no arrow crosses an application boundary by hand. This deck is an instance: the CA-SYS dispersion statistic on the GLM slides comes from a script, so it cannot disagree with the figure beside it.

Quarto is the publishing step

A publishing system for more than one language and more than one output. The path, from the Hello, Quarto keynote:

  • a .qmd file
  • knitr, or Jupyter
  • markdown, then Pandoc, then HTML, PDF, Word, or slides

The artwork on the original slide is Allison Horst’s, for Julia Lowndes and Mine Çetinkaya-Rundel, rstudio::conf 2022.

The middle step is the one people miss: the engine runs the code and produces plain markdown, and Pandoc then handles every output format. That separation is why the same source can become a paper and a deck, and why a format-specific problem is usually a Pandoc or template problem rather than an R problem.

One source, several outputs

A .qmd can be:

  • an article, a report, or a manuscript
  • a presentation, like this one
  • a dashboard, a website, or a book
  • an interactive document

The full lists are in the format guide, and the gallery holds the finished examples.

Three parts, in this order

The anatomy of a .qmd:

  1. a YAML header, for the metadata and the options
  2. the narrative, in markdown
  3. the code chunks, which import, wrangle, and draw

The header does not compute. The chunks do. The narrative is what a reader sees between them.

YAML is fussy about spaces

The header sits between two lines of three dashes. Each option is key: value, and each nested level is two spaces.

---
title: Quarto demo
format:
  html:
    toc: true
---

Option lists for HTML, PDF, Word, and reveal.js are the reference. This deck’s header is index.qmd.

Tabs instead of spaces is the single most common first error, and the message it produces is unhelpful. Mention that the header is also where the bibliography and the execution options live, both of which this deck relies on.

Markdown is the narrative

Syntax Result
**bold** bold text
#, ## a section, then a slide in this deck
[label](https://example.com) a link
![](image.png) a figure
`code` inline code
$\mu$ inline maths

The markdown guide is the rest of the syntax.

A chunk is the analysis, not a paste

  • A chunk opens with ```{r} and closes with ```
  • In RStudio, insert one with the toolbar button, or Ctrl+Alt+I
  • Run the current chunk with Ctrl+Shift+Enter

In this deck a chunk that would refit a model is marked eval: false, and the figure beside it is a file written by generate-data-figures.R. The number on the slide and the number in the file are then the same number.

This is the house rule for the deck and the reason the GLM slides can show a call and a fitted figure without refitting anything during the render. The trade is explicit: the script is the source of truth, and the slide quotes it.

A parameter is an input of the document

A parameterised report is a function: one .qmd, several reports, and the thing that changes is a value in params.

  • A parameter is a string, a number, or a Boolean
  • A data frame has to be serialised to travel in, then rebuilt inside the document. The pointers are Dervieux and Helveston

For this room the natural parameters are a site, a year, or a warming level: one report per SPRUCE plot, or one per cropping system, from a single source file. Say that a parameter is not a substitute for a function; the computation should still live in a script that the document calls.

The default has to exist in the data

params:
  system: "SD1"

The parameter name is a column you will filter on. Any name is legal. If SD1 is not a level in the data, the code errors or the output is blank.

The same key swaps a site, a year, or a warming level without a second copy of the file.

Read it with params$

params is a list, so params$system is the string.

  • In a sentence, use inline R: a backtick, the letter r, then params$system
  • In a plot title, paste(params$system, "richness") or glue::glue("{params$system} richness")
  • Search the file for the hard-coded string and replace it with params$

Three ways to render a variant

  1. The Render button, or Ctrl+Shift+K, uses the defaults in the header
  2. From R, quarto::quarto_render() takes execute_params = list(system = "SD1")
  3. From the terminal, quarto render template.qmd -P system:'SD1'

The parameterised-reporting workshops cover rendering every variant in one go. The source of the talk is jadeynryan/slc-rug-quarto.

Several formats, still one file

format:
  html:
    embed-resources: true
  pdf: default
  docx: default
format-links: [pdf, docx]
  • embed-resources: true puts the HTML in one file
  • format-links chooses which formats the HTML page offers for download
  • format-links: false offers none
  • Give HTML and reveal.js different output-file names, or the second render overwrites the first

Use a tool, or write one

The practices in the rest of this block are from Nathalie Lehmann and Quentin Blampey, Good practices (2024). The second project shape is the one that needs them.

Both shapes end in a result somebody has to be able to rerun. The difference is who carries the maintenance: in the left-hand shape it is somebody else’s package, and in the right-hand one it is you. Most people in this room are in the left-hand column and still need the Git and dependency slides.

Version the work

final_final is not a history

The name records a mood. It does not record which lines changed.

Read the four red questions aloud; they are the real content of this figure. Each one is a question a reviewer or a future you will ask, and none of them can be answered from a filename. Note that this is not a joke slide: the deck’s own history is a sequence of commits, which is how the figure numbers and the slide text stayed consistent.

Git answers the revision questions

  • which file made the figure in the article
  • what was deleted in the last version
  • which package versions the code used
  • why a small update for a revision changed the results

A commit is the answer to those four. A filename is not.

The hub is the shared copy

This deck’s hub is the GitHub repository named in the footer. The history of a slide is the history of the .qmd, not a second exported HTML.

Names, inputs, and the output

A function nobody else can read has three obligations:

  • a function name and argument names that say what they are
  • the input types, and the values that are allowed
  • a precise description of what comes back

data and x fail the first test. So does a function whose only documentation is its body.

The example in the source deck is a function called with an argument named “data”, which could be anything. Connect this to the codebook idea from the datasets block: a column name and a variable name carry the same obligation, which is to make the units and the allowed values visible without reading the implementation.

Put the documentation on a site

If you can, write the documentation as a site. The worked example is gustaveroussy.github.io/sopa, a GitHub Pages site for a package.

For an R package, pkgdown builds that site from the roxygen comments and the vignettes. GitHub Pages, or the gh-pages branch this deck already uses, is a place to put it.

Keep a rerun possible

Dependencies move after you publish

The package did not change. The ground under it did. A rerun on “whatever is current” is a different computation.

This is the slide that explains why a five-year-old analysis script fails today even though nobody touched it. The version you published against is a column in this picture, and the column moves. The practical consequence is on the lockfile slide: record the column, or the rerun is not the same computation.

An unkept package goes stale

If you do not maintain the code, it becomes deprecated.

Maintenance here is a decision about each new column of dependency versions:

  • test the release against them, or
  • state which column it still supports

Keep the old name until a stated date

def annotate_cell_types(X, d):
    warning("This function is deprecated and will be removed in Jan. 2025")
    return get_cell_types(X, markers=d)

The old name stays, warns, and forwards. The warning names the removal date.

The named example is Seurat, where renames across major versions broke a lot of downstream code. The remedy costs almost nothing: a shim, a warning, and a date. Note that the date is the part people omit, and it is the part that lets a user plan.

Release notes name the version

The example is v0.2.2, marked [0.2.2] - 2024-08-07:

  • a major line says the new disk format is backward compatible
  • a minor line adds an argument
  • a fixed line names a bug

The version number is the contract. The note is what the contract changed.

Record the column you tested

A version number is not enough if the dependency column is unnamed. Write down the versions that belonged to that release:

  • the numpy, pandas, and PyTorch numbers in the timeline
  • or renv.lock in an R project

The Posit::conf 2024 dev-ops materials keep that column in renv.lock. A later rerun then uses the lockfile, not today’s default install.

The lockfile is the dependency column made explicit. renv::snapshot() writes it and renv::restore() rebuilds the library from it on another machine.

Going further. A lockfile fixes package versions, but a simulation or a resampling result also depends on the random-number generator. R changed the default sample() algorithm in version 3.6.0 (from “Rounding” to “Rejection”), so the same set.seed() gives different permutations, bootstrap samples and cross-validation folds before and after that release; RNGkind() records the generator, and sessionInfo() records the R version that a lockfile alone does not pin. Parallel code needs a stream-safe generator such as L’Ecuyer-CMRG, or the result depends on the number of workers. Floating-point reductions in a different order, for example in a multithreaded BLAS, can change the last digits of a fitted coefficient, so a reproducibility test should compare numbers to a stated tolerance rather than for exact equality. This deck’s seed 20261005 is fixed in the figure scripts for that reason.

A test is code that checks a use

Testing means writing code that checks whether the package still does what it did.

A passing run is evidence about the cases you wrote down. It is the moment you can say the code is stable for those cases. A case you did not write is still unchecked.

The matrix shows which change broke

That grid, run on every change, is continuous integration.

The grid is the argument for automation in one picture. One row is a use case, one column is a version, and the cell is a pass or a fail. Nobody fills that grid in by hand across operating systems and language versions, which is why the next slide is a workflow file. The question printed under the grid is the one CI exists to answer.

Actions runs the tests for you

Open the log to see the failing case. Do not rerun the matrix by hand on a laptop and then forget which laptop. The example run is this Actions log.

A push to main can also publish

Continuous delivery is the same kind of file, with a deploy step at the end.

This deck’s .github/workflows/publish.yml runs on a push to main: it checks out the repository, installs Quarto, renders from the committed freeze, and publishes to gh-pages.

The getting-to-production workflow is the container version of the same pattern: on a push to main it builds an image and pushes it. The trigger is the same; the last step is a deploy instead of a test report.

Worth being concrete about this deck, because it is the live example in the room. The R code runs on a laptop, the results are committed under _freeze, and the Action installs Quarto only. That keeps the build fast and means a missing R package on the runner cannot break a published deck. The trade is that a changed chunk has to be re-rendered locally before the push.

Say which layer you are shipping

Before that deploy, separate three layers:

  • the presentation layer is what a person clicks
  • the processing layer is the work those clicks cause
  • the data layer is where the values are stored and read

A data update then changes the data layer. It does not require a new copy of the report, which is the Quarto path from the first slides.

The short list to take back

For any analysis:

  • an assert that refuses a bad input
  • a style guide, a formatter, and a linter
  • a pre-commit hook, so the check runs before the commit

For a package other people will use:

  • a dependency manager and a lockfile
  • tests on more than one operating system and language version
  • a documentation site, a release, and a version number you keep maintaining

Close the block by mapping it back to the workshop: the derived-data script, the figure script, the estimates JSON, and the link checker are the first list applied to a slide deck. If the room takes one thing, it is that the number in the text and the number in the figure should come from the same run.

Close

✅ What to open next, then the room.

What to open next · the response

  • zero-inflated and hurdle models, when zeros are a second process
  • compositions: log-ratios, and a deliberate rule for zeros, before any CLR
  • splines and GAMs, when the warming or moisture curve is the result
  • quantile residuals and calibration, once the family is in place

The longer GLM development is the SIB day already cited, and Agresti (Agresti 2015).

What to open next · design, cause, and reuse

  • a full mixed model for the SPRUCE indices \(i\) and \(j\), on a defensible scale for flux
  • spatial and temporal correlation, when the random intercept is not enough
  • a Bayesian hierarchical fit, with two priors and a comparison of the slope
  • a mechanistic model, when \(\mu\) is the solution of a process rather than a linear predictor
  • a reproducible script, and a report that meets SAMPL

FAIR habits matter here because the next person will need your unit, your missingness rule, and your seed.

A Bayesian reading, later

The causal hour did not use a posterior. These slides are the perspective if a later analysis does.

The mean can stay; the probability statement changes

A Bayesian fit of the same GLM still uses

\[ g(\mu_i) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i \]

and a family for \(Y \mid x\). What changes is the status of \(\beta\).

  • the likelihood is the model for \(Y\) given \(\beta\)
  • the prior is what you are willing to say about \(\beta\) before these rows
  • the posterior is the updated distribution of \(\beta\) given the rows

Riley and colleagues note one use directly: a probability such as \(\Pr(\text{rate ratio} < 0.9 \mid \text{data})\) (Riley et al. 2022).

The GLM mean equation \(g(\mu_i)=\beta_0+\beta_1 x_i\) stays; what moves is whether \(\beta\) is fixed or uncertain. Walk the three bullets: likelihood for \(Y\mid\beta\), prior before the rows, posterior after. Riley’s example is a probability about a rate ratio, not a repeated-sampling interval—say that sentence aloud.

Going further. Bayes’ theorem gives \(p(\beta \mid \mathbf{y}) = p(\mathbf{y} \mid \beta)\,p(\beta) / p(\mathbf{y})\), where the normalising constant \(p(\mathbf{y}) = \int p(\mathbf{y} \mid \beta)\,p(\beta)\,d\beta\) is the marginal likelihood. For a GLM with a non-identity link there is no conjugate prior for \(\beta\), so the posterior is computed numerically, by Markov chain Monte Carlo (Stan’s Hamiltonian sampler in brms or rstanarm) or by a Laplace or INLA approximation. The Bernstein–von Mises theorem says that, for a fixed number of parameters, a prior with positive density at the true value, and a correctly specified, regular model, the posterior is asymptotically normal, centred at the maximum-likelihood estimate with covariance equal to the inverse Fisher information; this is why Bayesian and Wald intervals agree in large samples. Gelman and colleagues, Bayesian Data Analysis (3rd edition, 2013), is the standard text.

Three objects, in order

No prior is “non-informative” merely because it was the software default. A prior labelled vague can still move \(\hat\beta\) when the sample is small or the outcome is sparse. That check is a sensitivity analysis.

Read the graph: the prior sits on the blue \(\boldsymbol\beta\), the likelihood on the arrows into the grey \(Y_i\); conditioning on the grey nodes gives the posterior \(p(\beta\mid Y)\). The punchline is the default-prior warning—“vague” can still pull \(\hat\beta\) with sparse data or ten enclosures. Name refitting with a second prior as the honesty check on the next slide.

Going further. A flat prior is not invariant: flat on \(\beta\) is not flat on \(e^{\beta}\), so “non-informative” depends on the scale chosen. Weakly informative priors put most mass on plausible values, for example \(\beta_1 \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)\) on a log-rate scale with standardised \(x\), which rules out a 100-fold change per standard deviation without favouring a sign. A prior predictive check simulates \(Y\) from \(p(\beta)\) and the family before seeing the data, and reveals priors that imply impossible responses, such as fluxes of \(10^{6}\). For a group-level standard deviation \(\sigma_u\) with few groups, Gelman (Bayesian Analysis, 2006) showed that the inverse-gamma\((\epsilon, \epsilon)\) prior is far from vague and recommended a half-\(t\) or half-normal prior instead. Reporting the posterior under two priors is the minimal sensitivity analysis.

What you are allowed to say

A Wald interval from glm is a statement about the sampling distribution of the estimator, under the model, if the experiment were repeated.

A posterior interval is a statement about \(\beta\) given this sample and this prior.

They can be numerically close for a large sample and a gentle prior. They are not the same sentence. Write the one you computed.

With ten enclosures, the prior on \(\sigma_u\) is not a detail. Refit with a second prior and show the slope again.

Contrast two sentences: Wald interval is about the estimator under repeated experiments; posterior interval is about \(\beta\) given this sample and this prior. They may look similar with lots of data—they are not interchangeable wording. Ten enclosures is the concrete SPRUCE hook for priors on \(\sigma_u\); insist on a second prior in the supplement.

Going further. A 95% confidence procedure covers the fixed true \(\beta\) in 95% of repeated samples; a 95% credible interval contains 95% of the posterior mass for this sample. The two coincide asymptotically under the Bernstein–von Mises conditions, and exactly in a few cases, such as a normal mean with known variance and a flat prior, but can differ sharply with small samples, parameters near a boundary (a variance near zero), or sparse binary outcomes. Credible intervals come in two flavours: the equal-tailed interval, from the 2.5% and 97.5% posterior quantiles, and the highest-density interval, the shortest region of given mass, which differ for a skewed posterior such as that of \(\sigma_u\). Neither has guaranteed frequentist coverage unless the prior matches the data-generating process; a simulation-based calibration check (Talts and colleagues, 2018) tests whether the computation itself is correct.

Where it earns its place this afternoon

  • partial pooling of sites or enclosures, with an honest prior on the variance
  • a parameter that must stay positive, such as a variance or a rate
  • a small sample, where a weakly identified \(\beta\) should not look precise
  • a hierarchical summary across studies, which is the meta-analysis version of the same idea

It does not remove confounding, and it does not choose the estimand.

Read the bullet list as scopes, not sales points: partial pooling, positive parameters, small-\(n\) uncertainty, hierarchical meta-analysis. Close with the last line—Bayes does not open blocked back-door paths and does not replace design thinking. The coefficient-versus-intervention slide already opened the causal hour. Do not re-teach Simpson here.

Going further. Any MCMC fit has to pass diagnostics before the posterior is read. Run at least four chains from dispersed starting values and check the rank-normalised split \(\hat{R} < 1.01\) and a bulk and tail effective sample size above about 400 (Vehtari and colleagues, Bayesian Analysis, 2021); with Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, any divergent transition is a warning that the sampler missed part of the posterior, typically the funnel between group effects and a small \(\sigma_u\), which a non-centred parameterisation usually removes. Diagnostics say the computation is reliable, not that the model is right; that is the role of the posterior predictive check, which simulates replicate data \(\mathbf{y}^{\text{rep}}\) from \(p(\mathbf{y}^{\text{rep}} \mid \mathbf{y})\) and compares a statistic, such as the proportion of zeros or the variance-to-mean ratio, with its observed value (Gelman, Meng and Stern, Statistica Sinica, 1996). Partial pooling itself is the shrinkage of each group estimate towards the overall mean by a factor that depends on \(\sigma_u^2\) and the group’s sample size, the Bayesian counterpart of the best linear unbiased predictor in a mixed model.
